Chapter 1 · Step-by-step

Animal Kingdom

Five bases of classification and every animal phylum - the starting point of NEET Zoology.

Animal KingdomDiversity & OrganisationChapter Introduction

Subtopics in this Chapter

1. Basis of classification2. Symmetry3. Germ layers4. Coelom5. Segmentation6. Notochord7. Porifera8. Cnidaria9. Ctenophora10. Platyhelminthes11. Aschelminthes12. Annelida13. Arthropoda14. Mollusca15. Echinodermata16. Hemichordata17. Chordata18. Vertebrates

Deep Dive · The five bases of classification

Levels of organisation decide the first split: Porifera stop at the cellular level (loose cells, no tissues), Cnidaria and Ctenophora reach the tissue level, Platyhelminthes reach organ level, and all higher phyla reach the organ-system level. Symmetry follows: sponges are mostly asymmetrical, cnidarians/ctenophores/adult echinoderms are radial, and everything else (including echinoderm larvae) is bilateral - bilateral symmetry is linked with cephalisation (head formation) and active movement.
Germ layers and coelom are the two most-asked bases. Diploblastic animals (Cnidaria, Ctenophora) have only ectoderm + endoderm with jelly-like mesoglea between - never call mesoglea mesoderm. Triploblastic animals add mesoderm, and the fate of mesoderm creates the coelom story: in schizocoelomates the mesoderm splits to form the cavity (annelids, arthropods, molluscs), while in enterocoelomates pouches pinch off from the embryonic gut (echinoderms, chordates). This embryological split is why molluscs group with annelids and echinoderms group with chordates despite looking nothing alike.
Coelom types - memorise this table
TypeBody cavityExamples
AcoelomateNo cavity; mesoderm fills the spacePlatyhelminthes (Taenia, Fasciola, Planaria)
PseudocoelomateCavity NOT fully lined by mesodermAschelminthes (Ascaris, Wuchereria)
SchizocoelomateTrue coelom from splitting mesodermAnnelida, Arthropoda, Mollusca
EnterocoelomateTrue coelom from gut pouchesEchinodermata, Chordata
Fig: Body-wall (pink) - cavity (blue/white) - gut (centre). Only coelomates have a fully mesoderm-lined cavity.
NEET traps
  • Echinoderm larvae bilateral, adults radial - asked almost every alternate year.
  • Comb plates = Ctenophora, flame cells = Platyhelminthes, choanocytes/collar cells = Porifera, cnidoblasts = Cnidaria, nephridia = Annelida, Malpighian tubules = Arthropoda, radula = Mollusca, water vascular system = Echinodermata.
  • Notochord is mesodermal in origin, never ectodermal.
NEET Practice MCQ
1

Which pairing of excretory/special structure and phylum is correct?

Solution: Flame cells belong to Platyhelminthes and choanocytes to Porifera; Malpighian tubules are the excretory organs of insects (Arthropoda).

Related Diagram · Vertebrate heart evolution

Fig: Two chambers suffice for gills; lungs plus high metabolism demand four.

Chapter 1 · Topic 1

Basis of classification

Animal KingdomDiversity & OrganisationStep-by-step Topic
Animals are classified on levels of organisation (cellular, tissue, organ, organ-system), symmetry (asymmetrical in sponges; radial in cnidarians, ctenophores and adult echinoderms; bilateral in most), diploblastic vs triploblastic organisation, coelom (body cavity lined by mesoderm), segmentation (metamerism) and notochord. The coelom types decide big groupings: acoelomates (Platyhelminthes — no cavity), pseudocoelomates (Aschelminthes — cavity not fully lined by mesoderm) and coelomates (annelids, arthropods, molluscs, echinoderms, chordates). Annelids–molluscs–arthropods are schizocoelomates (cavity from splitting of mesoderm); echinoderms and chordates are enterocoelomates (cavity from gut pouches). Notochord is mesodermally derived.
NEET facts:
  • Comb plates = Ctenophora; flame cells (solenocytes) = Platyhelminthes; collar cells (choanocytes) = Porifera.
  • Echinoderm larvae are bilaterally symmetrical; only adults become radial.

Deeper Understanding · Germ layers and body plans

Only triploblastic animals can form a coelom, because the cavity is carved out of mesoderm itself. Diploblasts (Cnidaria, Ctenophora) have mesoglea - a non-cellular jelly - which is never homologous to mesoderm. Within triploblasts, the protostome vs deuterostome split (mouth-first vs anus-first development) separates annelids-arthropods-molluscs from echinoderms-chordates, mirroring the schizocoel vs enterocoel division. Segmentation (metamerism) is most perfect in Annelida, where every segment repeats nerves, vessels and nephridia.

Topic in Depth · Basis of classification

Classification is not stamp-collecting - each basis reveals an evolutionary leap. Organisation level shows how far cells cooperated, symmetry shows how the animal meets its environment, germ layers show developmental complexity, coelom shows how organs got private housing, segmentation shows repeated-unit engineering, and notochord announces the chordate lineage. NEET frames questions by asking which single basis separates two named phyla.
NEET Snapshot:
  • Cellular (Porifera) - tissue (Cnidaria) - organ (Platyhelminthes) - organ-system (rest).
  • Radial suits drifting/feeding all around; bilateral suits directed movement with a head.
  • Coelom + notochord questions carry the highest strike rate in this chapter.
Which basis splits whom?
PointDetail
Porifera vs CnidariaCellular vs tissue organisation
Cnidaria vs PlatyhelminthesDiploblastic vs triploblastic
Platyhelminthes vs AnnelidaAcoelomate vs coelomate
Chordates vs allNotochord present
NEET Practice MCQ
1

Which basis of classification separates Platyhelminthes from Annelida?

Solution: Both are triploblastic organ-system animals; flatworms are acoelomate while annelids are schizocoelomate.

Chapter 1 · Topic 2

Symmetry

Animal KingdomDiversity & OrganisationStep-by-step Topic
Asymmetrical animals (most sponges) cannot be divided into equal halves by any plane. Radial symmetry (cnidarians, ctenophores, adult echinoderms) gives identical halves around a central axis - ideal for sessile or drifting animals that meet food from all sides. Bilateral symmetry (all higher phyla, plus echinoderm larvae) gives one plane of division with distinct head and tail ends, driving cephalisation - concentration of brain and sense organs upfront for active, directional movement.
NEET facts:
  • Echinoderm larvae bilateral, adults radial - metamorphosis switches symmetry.
  • Ctenophores are technically biradial (two planes), counted as radial in NCERT.

Topic in Depth · Symmetry

Symmetry is the geometry of survival. Asymmetry (sponges) means no axis - food arrives randomly, so no head pays off. Radial symmetry (cnidarians, adult echinoderms) means every direction is equal - tentacles in a ring catch prey from any side. Bilateral symmetry means one privileged direction - front gets the brain, sense organs and mouth (cephalisation), rear gets waste exit, enabling fast purposeful hunting and escape.
NEET Snapshot:
  • Sponges: asymmetrical (a few secondarily radial).
  • Echinoderm larvae bilateral, adults radial - metamorphosis flips geometry.
  • Ctenophores: biradial, counted as radial in NCERT.
Remember
  • Ask "which way does it move?" - drifters go radial, hunters go bilateral.
NEET Practice MCQ
1

An animal whose larvae are bilaterally symmetrical but adults are radially symmetrical belongs to

Solution: Echinoderm larvae are bilateral; only adults become radial - a classic metamorphosis question.
Back to Index
Index / Diversity & Organisation
Chapter 1 · Topic 3

Germ layers

Animal KingdomDiversity & OrganisationStep-by-step Topic
Diploblastic animals (Cnidaria, Ctenophora) build from ectoderm + endoderm only, with non-cellular mesoglea between - mesoglea is jelly, never mesoderm. Triploblastic animals add true mesoderm, which forms muscles, blood, kidneys, gonads and the body cavity lining. Every phylum from Platyhelminthes upward is triploblastic. Ectoderm always makes skin + nervous system, endoderm makes the gut lining and associated glands, mesoderm makes everything in between.
Common mistakes: calling mesoglea a germ layer - it is not a layer at all.

Topic in Depth · Germ layers

Germ layers are the embryo three construction crews. Ectoderm builds the outer world interface (skin, nervous system), endoderm builds the inner tube (gut lining and its glands), mesoderm builds everything structural between (muscle, blood, bone, kidneys, gonads, coelom lining). Diploblasts never evolved mesoderm - their middle is acellular mesoglea jelly. Every advance from flatworms upward (excretion, circulation, skeletons) is mesoderm story.
NEET Snapshot:
  • Ectoderm: skin + nerves. Endoderm: gut + liver/pancreas. Mesoderm: all the rest.
  • Mesoglea is not a germ layer - never call cnidarians triploblastic.
Layer fates
PointDetail
Skin, brain, spinal cordEctoderm
Gut lining, liver, lungsEndoderm
Muscle, blood, bone, kidney, gonadsMesoderm
NEET Practice MCQ
1

Notochord is derived from which germ layer?

Solution: The notochord is a mesodermal rod - a favourite one-line trap against ectoderm options.
Back to Index
Index / Diversity & Organisation
Chapter 1 · Topic 4

Coelom

Animal KingdomDiversity & OrganisationStep-by-step Topic
The coelom is a mesoderm-lined body cavity housing visceral organs. Acoelomates (Platyhelminthes) have none - mesoderm fills the space. Pseudocoelomates (Aschelminthes) have a cavity not fully lined by mesoderm. True coelomates split by origin: schizocoelomates (annelids, arthropods, molluscs - cavity from splitting mesoderm) and enterocoelomates (echinoderms, chordates - cavity from gut pouches). Coelom cushions organs, carries fluids and gives space for organ growth.
NEET facts:
  • Schizocoel = protostomes; enterocoel = deuterostomes.
  • Nematode pseudocoelom acts as a hydrostatic skeleton.

Topic in Depth · Coelom

The coelom is a mesoderm-lined private cavity that frees organs from the body wall - gut can churn, gonads can swell, hearts can beat without squeezing neighbours. Its origin story splits the animal kingdom: schizocoel (mesoderm splits open - protostomes: annelids, arthropods, molluscs) versus enterocoel (gut pouches pinch off - deuterostomes: echinoderms, chordates). Pseudocoelomates kept a halfway house - fluid-filled but unlined - still useful as a hydrostatic skeleton.
NEET Snapshot:
  • Acoelomate: flatworms. Pseudocoelomate: roundworms.
  • Schizocoel: annelid/arthropod/mollusc. Enterocoel: echinoderm/chordate.
Remember
  • Coelom origin predicts development: mouth-first (protostome) pairs with schizocoel.
NEET Practice MCQ
1

Enterocoelomate phyla are

Solution: Echinoderms and chordates form the coelom from gut pouches (enterocoely) - the deuterostome signature.
Back to Index
Index / Diversity & Organisation
Chapter 1 · Topic 5

Segmentation

Animal KingdomDiversity & OrganisationStep-by-step Topic
Metamerism is serial repetition of body segments, most perfect in Annelida where nerves, blood vessels and nephridia repeat in every segment. Arthropods show fused, specialised segments (head, thorax, abdomen - tagmatisation). Chordates retain segmentation in muscles, vertebrae and nerves. Tapeworm proglottids look segmented but are merely repeated reproductive units (pseudosegmentation), not true metamerism.

Topic in Depth · Segmentation

Segmentation (metamerism) is mass production: repeat one body unit with its own nerves, vessels and excretory organs, and damage to one segment need not kill the animal. Annelids show textbook metamerism; arthropods fused segments into specialised tagmata (head-thorax-abdomen) for division of labour; vertebrates kept it internally (vertebrae, ribs, spinal nerves, muscle blocks). Tapeworm proglottids only look segmented - repeated reproductive units without true serial organs is pseudosegmentation.
NEET Snapshot:
  • True metamerism: Annelida (external + internal).
  • Tagmatisation: fused functional regions in arthropods.
  • Proglottids are not true segments.
NEET Practice MCQ
1

True metameric segmentation is best exhibited by

Solution: Nereis (annelid) repeats nerves, vessels and nephridia per segment; tapeworm repeats only reproductive units.
Back to Index
Index / Diversity & Organisation
Chapter 1 · Topic 6

Notochord

Animal KingdomDiversity & OrganisationStep-by-step Topic
The notochord is a flexible mesoderm-derived skeletal rod on the dorsal side that defines Chordata, along with the dorsal hollow nerve cord, pharyngeal gill slits and post-anal tail. In protochordates it persists lifelong; in vertebrates it is replaced by the vertebral column (only remnants remain as intervertebral disc cores). Animals with a notochord only in the larval tail (Urochordata) still qualify as chordates.

Topic in Depth · Notochord

The notochord is a stiff yet flexible mesodermal rod that gave early chordates an internal axis for muscle attachment - swimming by side-to-side undulation instead of jelly pulsing. In protochordates it persists for life (Amphioxus swims on it); in vertebrates it is largely replaced by the vertebral column, lingering only as intervertebral disc cores. Its dorsal position (below the nerve cord, above the gut) is fixed anatomy - any diagram question checks exactly this stacking.
NEET Snapshot:
  • Mesodermal rod, dorsal, flexible - the chordate badge.
  • Replaced by vertebrae in vertebrates; persists in Amphioxus.
Fig: Dorsal-to-ventral stacking: nerve cord, notochord, gut - draw it in this order.
NEET Practice MCQ
1

In vertebrates the embryonic notochord is mainly replaced by

Solution: Vertebrae replace the notochord; remnants persist as disc nuclei pulposi.
Back to Index
Index / Diversity & Organisation
Chapter 1 · Topic 7

Porifera

Animal KingdomDiversity & OrganisationStep-by-step Topic
Porifera (Sycon, Spongilla, Euspongia): cellular-level organisation, asymmetrical, with a water canal system (ostia in, osculum out) driving food capture and gas exchange. Skeleton of spicules or spongin fibres; special choanocytes (collar cells) create currents. Hermaphrodite with internal fertilisation and indirect development (amphiblastula larva). Sponges show the highest regeneration - tiny fragments regrow whole sponges.
NEET facts:
  • Bath sponge = Euspongia; freshwater sponge = Spongilla.

Related Diagram · Sponge canal system

Fig: Water enters through ostia, choanocyte collars trap food, waste exits the osculum.

Topic in Depth · Porifera

Sponges are the minimal animal: no tissues, no symmetry, no gut - just pores, canals and beating collar cells running a water pump. A large sponge filters thousands of litres daily, trapping bacteria and detritus on choanocyte collars. Their skeleton (spicules of silica/calcium or spongin protein) is taxonomy basis, and their totipotent archaeocytes regenerate whole sponges from fragments - the simplest stem-cell system in nature.
NEET Snapshot:
  • Canal system: ostia in, spongocoel, osculum out.
  • Skeleton: spicules or spongin. Cells: choanocytes, amoebocytes.
Remember
  • Spongilla (freshwater) vs Euspongia (bath sponge) - habitat-tagged examples.
NEET Practice MCQ
1

Collar cells (choanocytes) occur in

Solution: Choanocytes line sponge canals and drive water currents - exclusive poriferan flag.
Back to Index
Index / Diversity & Organisation
Chapter 1 · Topic 8

Cnidaria

Animal KingdomDiversity & OrganisationStep-by-step Topic
Cnidaria (Hydra, Aurelia, Adamsia, Physalia): tissue-level, radial, diploblastic with cnidoblasts bearing stinging nematocysts for defence and prey capture. Single gastrovascular opening serves as mouth and anus. Many show metagenesis - alternation of asexual polyp and sexual medusa (Obelia), though both phases are diploid. Corals secrete calcium carbonate reefs; Physalia (Portuguese man-of-war) is a colony, not one animal.

Related Diagram · Obelia metagenesis

Fig: Asexual polyp buds off sexual medusae; larvae settle back into polyps - both phases diploid.

Topic in Depth · Cnidaria

Cnidarians weaponised a single cell: the cnidoblast fires a harpoon-like nematocyst in microseconds - among the fastest cellular mechanisms known - injecting venom for prey capture and defence. Two body forms (sessile polyp, free medusa) alternate in metagenesis, though both are diploid so it is not true alternation of generations. Coral polyps engineer entire reef ecosystems by stacking calcium carbonate skeletons over millennia.
NEET Snapshot:
  • Cnidoblast + nematocyst = phylum badge.
  • Polyp (asexual) alternates with medusa (sexual) in Obelia.
NEET Practice MCQ
1

Metagenesis in Obelia involves alternation of

Solution: Both phases are diploid, so it only mimics plant alternation of generations.
Back to Index
Index / Diversity & Organisation
Chapter 1 · Topic 9

Ctenophora

Animal KingdomDiversity & OrganisationStep-by-step Topic
Ctenophora (Pleurobrachia, Ctenoplana - comb jellies): biradial symmetry, diploblastic, with 8 external rows of ciliated comb plates for swimming and sticky colloblasts for prey capture. Exclusively marine, transparent, and bioluminescent. Digestion is both extracellular and intracellular, and excretion/sex organs are absent - reproduction is sexual with indirect development.

Topic in Depth · Ctenophora

Comb jellies are the ocean transparent oarsmen: eight ciliary comb rows beat in rainbow waves for swimming - the largest cilia-powered animals alive. Instead of stinging cells they deploy sticky colloblasts on tentacles, and many species glow via bioluminescence (defence/startle display). Biradial symmetry, complete gut with anal pores, and absence of excretory and reproductive ducts complete a body plan found nowhere else.
NEET Snapshot:
  • 8 comb plates + colloblasts + bioluminescence = Ctenophora.
  • Exclusively marine: Pleurobrachia, Ctenoplana.
NEET Practice MCQ
1

An exclusively marine, biradially symmetrical animal with 8 ciliated comb plates is a

Solution: Comb plates plus colloblasts diagnose Ctenophora (comb jellies/sea walnuts).
Back to Index
Index / Diversity & Organisation
Chapter 1 · Topic 10

Platyhelminthes

Animal KingdomDiversity & OrganisationStep-by-step Topic
Platyhelminthes (Taenia, Fasciola, Planaria): dorsoventrally flat, triploblastic but acoelomate, organ-level organisation. Excretion by flame cells (solenocytes); mostly parasitic with hooks and suckers (tapeworm proglottids are egg factories). Planaria shows remarkable regeneration. No circulatory or respiratory systems - diffusion suffices for flat bodies.
Common mistakes: flame cells = flatworms, never roundworms.

Topic in Depth · Platyhelminthes

Flatworms are the first triploblasts - and their flatness is engineering: without circulation or lungs, every cell must sit near the surface for diffusion, so the body stays ribbon-thin. Free-living Planaria regenerates from tiny pieces (neoblast stem cells) and learns simple tasks; parasitic flukes and tapeworms traded gut and senses for hooks, suckers and astronomical egg output. Flame cells flicker to flush wastes - the excretory signature of the phylum.
NEET Snapshot:
  • Acoelomate, unsegmented (proglottids are pseudo), flame cells.
  • Planaria: regeneration model. Taenia: hooks + suckers + proglottids.
Remember
  • Flat body = diffusion-only transport - shape follows physiology.
NEET Practice MCQ
1

Flame cells perform excretion in

Solution: Flame cells (solenocytes) are the platyhelminth excretory organs; annelids use nephridia.
Back to Index
Index / Diversity & Organisation
Chapter 1 · Topic 11

Aschelminthes

Animal KingdomDiversity & OrganisationStep-by-step Topic
Aschelminthes (Ascaris, Wuchereria, Ancylostoma): cylindrical body, pseudocoelom, complete alimentary canal with muscular pharynx. Sexes separate with females longer than males. Ascaris (intestinal roundworm) spreads via contaminated food; Wuchereria causes elephantiasis via Culex mosquito; Ancylostoma (hookworm) causes anaemia by sucking intestinal blood.

Topic in Depth · Aschelminthes

Roundworms added two upgrades over flatworms: a complete one-way gut (mouth to anus, with muscular pharynx for sucking) and a fluid-filled pseudocoelom doubling as a hydrostatic skeleton for thrashing movement. Separate sexes with visible dimorphism (longer females) enabled specialised parasitism: Ascaris in intestines, hookworms drinking blood, Wuchereria clogging lymphatics via mosquito vectors. Their tough cuticle resists host enzymes - moulted periodically like arthropod armour.
NEET Snapshot:
  • Pseudocoelom + complete gut + cuticle = roundworm plan.
  • Wuchereria needs Culex; Ascaris spreads by contaminated food.
NEET Practice MCQ
1

Elephantiasis is caused by

Solution: Wuchereria (filarial worm) blocks lymph vessels; Culex mosquito is its vector.
Back to Index
Index / Diversity & Organisation
Chapter 1 · Topic 12

Annelida

Animal KingdomDiversity & OrganisationStep-by-step Topic
Annelida (Nereis, Pheretima, Hirudinaria): true metameric segmentation, closed circulation, nephridia for excretion. Nereis is marine, dioecious with parapodia; earthworm (Pheretima) is terrestrial hermaphrodite and a farmer friend (soil aeration); leech (Hirudinaria) is a blood-sucking ectoparasite whose saliva contains anticoagulant hirudin.

Topic in Depth · Annelida

Annelids perfected the segmented tube-within-a-tube: outer muscular wall, inner complete gut, true coelom between, repeated nephridia per segment, closed blood with haemoglobin in plasma. Nereis adds parapodia with setae for swimming and crawling; earthworms (hermaphrodite, clitellum for cocoons) aerate soil and are farmer friends; leeches traded locomotion for blood-feeding with hirudin anticoagulant - still used in microsurgery to keep vessels open.
NEET Snapshot:
  • Metamerism + closed circulation + nephridia = annelid trio.
  • Nereis dioecious; earthworm and leech hermaphrodite.
NEET Practice MCQ
1

Hirudin, an anticoagulant, is obtained from

Solution: Leech (Hirudinaria) saliva contains hirudin that keeps sucked blood flowing.
Back to Index
Index / Diversity & Organisation
Chapter 1 · Topic 13

Arthropoda

Animal KingdomDiversity & OrganisationStep-by-step Topic
Arthropoda - the largest phylum (over two-thirds of known species): jointed appendages, chitinous exoskeleton (moulted as ecdysis), open circulation, excretion by Malpighian tubules (insects), green glands or coxal glands. Body in head-thorax-abdomen; examples Apis, Bombyx, Anopheles, and Limulus the living-fossil king crab. Flight + metamorphosis + waterproof cuticle explain their dominance on land and air.
NEET facts:
  • Silverfish is an insect; starfish, cuttlefish, devilfish are not fishes.

Topic in Depth · Arthropoda

Arthropods conquered land, air and water with four inventions: jointed appendages (legs, wings, mouthparts, antennae from one serial plan), waterproof chitinous exoskeleton (moulted as ecdysis for growth), tracheal or gill breathing plus Malpighian tubule water conservation, and metamorphosis separating larval feeding from adult reproduction niches. Insects alone outnumber all other animal species combined - flight plus complete metamorphosis is evolution most successful product line.
NEET Snapshot:
  • Jointed legs + chitin + open circulation + Malpighian tubules.
  • Limulus (king crab) = living fossil, not a true crab.
Excretory match
PointDetail
InsectsMalpighian tubules
CrustaceansGreen glands
ArachnidsCoxal glands
NEET Practice MCQ
1

The largest phylum of Animalia is

Solution: Arthropods hold over two-thirds of described species, mostly insects.
Back to Index
Index / Diversity & Organisation
Chapter 1 · Topic 14

Mollusca

Animal KingdomDiversity & OrganisationStep-by-step Topic
Mollusca - second largest phylum: soft unsegmented body with mantle (secretes calcareous shell), muscular foot and rasping radula (absent in bivalves and octopus). Open circulation except closed in cephalopods. Examples: Pila, Unio, Octopus, Sepia, Dentalium. Pearl oyster Pinctada coats irritants with nacre to form pearls; Octopus shows the most advanced invertebrate brain and camera eyes.

Topic in Depth · Mollusca

Molluscs run a soft-body business model: muscular foot for movement, visceral hump holding organs, and a mantling fold that secretes the calcareous shell plus the mantle cavity housing gills. The rasping radula (except bivalves) scrapes food; cephalopods upgraded everything - closed circulation, camera eyes, large brains, jet propulsion and ink defence - becoming the smartest invertebrates. Pearl formation is mantle defence: nacre layers entombing an irritant over years.
NEET Snapshot:
  • Mantle + foot + radula = mollusc trio.
  • Second-largest phylum; Octopus most intelligent invertebrate.
NEET Practice MCQ
1

Radula, the rasping organ, is found in

Chapter 1 · Topic 15

Echinodermata

Animal KingdomDiversity & OrganisationStep-by-step Topic
Echinodermata (Asterias, Echinus, Holothuria, Ophiura): spiny skin of calcareous ossicles, water vascular system with tube feet for locomotion and feeding, radial adults from bilateral larvae. Exclusively marine, enterocoelomate - their embryos link them to chordates. Remarkable regeneration: a single arm with central disc regrows a whole starfish.

Topic in Depth · Echinodermata

Echinoderms are deuterostome rebels: bilateral larvae settle and rebuild into radial adults with a unique hydraulic machine - the water vascular system with tube feet that grip, walk and pry open bivalves. Calcareous ossicles armour the skin (spines in sea urchins, soft in sea cucumbers which eject sticky Cuvierian tubules when threatened). Their embryos (enterocoel, radial cleavage) whisper their chordate kinship louder than adult bodies suggest.
NEET Snapshot:
  • Water vascular system + tube feet = echinoderm badge.
  • All marine; Asterias can regrow from one arm plus disc.
Remember
  • Larva bilateral, adult radial - plus enterocoelomate embryos = chordate cousins.
NEET Practice MCQ
1

Water vascular system is characteristic of

Solution: Tube feet run on hydraulics unique to echinoderms - locomotion, feeding and respiration.
Back to Index
Index / Diversity & Organisation
Chapter 1 · Topic 16

Hemichordata

Animal KingdomDiversity & OrganisationStep-by-step Topic
Hemichordata (Balanoglossus - acorn worm, Saccoglossus): worm-like marine animals once grouped with chordates because of the stomochord (a buccal-tube outgrowth, not a true notochord), plus pharyngeal gill slits. Excretion by the proboscis gland. Modern classification keeps them as a separate invertebrate phylum - the classic example of a group promoted then demoted by embryology.

Topic in Depth · Hemichordata

Hemichordates (acorn worms like Balanoglossus) were exiled from Chordata when embryology showed their stomochord is a buccal-tube stiffener, not a true notochord. They keep pharyngeal gill slits and a dorsal nerve cord though - halfway-house anatomy that still illuminates chordate origins. Excretion via the proboscis gland; body in proboscis-collar-trunk regions; marine burrowers eating sediment.
NEET Snapshot:
  • Stomochord is not a notochord - the demotion reason.
  • Balanoglossus: proboscis gland excretion, gill slits present.
NEET Practice MCQ
1

Balanoglossus belongs to

Solution: Acorn worms are hemichordates - worm-like marine animals with a stomochord, not a true notochord.

Chapter 1 · Topic 17

Chordata

Animal KingdomDiversity & OrganisationStep-by-step Topic
Chordate hallmarks: notochord, dorsal hollow nerve cord, pharyngeal gill slits, post-anal tail - all four need appear only at some life stage. Urochordata (Ascidia, Salpa, Doliolum) keep the tail only in larvae; Cephalochordata (Branchiostoma/Amphioxus) keep all four lifelong and are the textbook primitive chordate. Vertebrata replaces the notochord with a vertebral column and adds a tripartite brain, paired sense organs and a ventral heart.

Related Diagram · Four chordate hallmarks

Fig: Notochord, dorsal nerve cord, gill slits and post-anal tail - present at least in the embryo.

Topic in Depth · Chordata

Chordata membership needs all four hallmarks at some life stage: notochord, dorsal hollow nerve cord, pharyngeal slits, post-anal tail. Urochordates (sea squirts) show them only as larvae and reabsorb the tail at metamorphosis; cephalochordates (Amphioxus) keep all four for life and filter-feed with an endostyle (thyroid ancestor). Vertebrates then added the cranium, vertebrae, paired appendages and a chambered ventral heart - the upgrades behind 60,000+ species.
NEET Snapshot:
  • 4 hallmarks, needed only transiently in urochordates.
  • Endostyle of Amphioxus = evolutionary thyroid.
NEET Practice MCQ
1

Which animal retains all four chordate characters throughout life?

Solution: Amphioxus (Branchiostoma, Cephalochordata) keeps notochord, nerve cord, slits and tail lifelong.

Index / Diversity & Organisation
Chapter 1 · Topic 18

Vertebrates

Animal KingdomDiversity & OrganisationStep-by-step Topic
Chordate hallmarks: notochord, dorsal hollow nerve cord, pharyngeal gill slits, post-anal tail. Protochordates — Urochordata (Ascidia, Salpa, Doliolum) and Cephalochordata (Branchiostoma/Amphioxus). Vertebrates: Fishes — scales, 2-chambered heart, cold-blooded (e.g. Scoliodon, Pristis, Trygon with poison sting, Exocoetus flying fish, Hippocampus sea-horse); Amphibia — 3-chambered heart, moist skin, external fertilisation (Rana, Bufo, Salamandra, Ichthyophis limbless); Reptilia — dry cornified skin, creeping gait, incomplete 4-chambered heart except crocodile (complete 4 chambers) (Chameleon, Calotes, Hemidactylus, poisonous Naja, Viper); Aves — feathers, hollow pneumatic bones, air sacs supplement respiration, 4-chambered heart, warm-blooded (Corvus, Columba, Psittacula, flightless Struthio); Mammalia — hair, mammary glands, diaphragm, external ears (pinnae), 4-chambered heart; egg-laying Ornithorhynchus (platypus), pouched Macropus (kangaroo), flying Pteropus, aquatic Balaenoptera (blue whale — largest animal), Delphinus.

A warm-blooded vertebrate lays eggs yet suckles its young. Identify it and its group.

Answer: Platypus (Ornithorhynchus) — an egg-laying mammal (prototherian).

Deeper Understanding · Heart evolution across vertebrates

Heart chambers track lifestyle: fishes manage with 2 chambers (single circulation through gills), amphibians and most reptiles with 3 chambers (mixing tolerated because skin also breathes), and crocodilians, birds and mammals with complete 4 chambers (no mixing - supports warm-blooded metabolism and flight). Incomplete double circulation in reptiles works because of intracardiac shunting. Warm-bloodedness appears independently in Aves and Mammalia, both with 4-chambered hearts, air-efficient lungs (air sacs vs alveoli) and insulation (feathers vs hair).

Topic in Depth · Vertebrates

Vertebrates layered innovations class by class: jawless fishes (lamprey suckers) to jawed fishes (scales, 2-chambered heart, fins), amphibians (limbs, lungs plus skin breathing, 3-chambered heart), reptiles (dry cornified skin, shelled eggs freeing them from water, mostly 3-chambered hearts), birds (feathers, air sacs, 4-chambered hearts, flight) and mammals (hair, mammary glands, diaphragm, 4-chambered hearts). Each step solved one constraint - desiccation, then locomotion, then energy supply for endothermy.
NEET Snapshot:
  • Fishes 2, amphibians 3, reptiles 3 (crocodile 4), birds/mammals 4 chambers.
  • Egg-laying mammals: platypus. Pouched: kangaroo. Largest animal: blue whale.
Heart chambers
PointDetail
Fishes2 (single circulation)
Amphibians3
Most reptiles3 (incomplete double)
Crocodile, birds, mammals4 (complete double)
NEET Practice MCQ
1

Crocodile differs from other reptiles in having a

Solution: Crocodilians alone among living reptiles have complete 4-chambered hearts like birds and mammals.

Chapter 2 · Step-by-step

Structural Organisation in Animals

Four tissue types and the complete cockroach - morphology plus all organ systems.

Structural Organisation in AnimalsDiversity & OrganisationChapter Introduction

Subtopics in this Chapter

1. Animal tissues2. Epithelial tissue3. Connective tissue4. Muscular tissue5. Nervous tissue6. Cockroach7. Digestive system8. Circulatory system9. Respiratory system10. Nervous system11. Reproductive system

Deep Dive · Tissues in detail

Epithelium always sits on a basement membrane and is avascular (no blood vessels - nourished by diffusion). Simple epithelium is one cell thick and lines absorptive/secretory surfaces: squamous where diffusion happens (alveoli, Bowman's capsule, endothelium of vessels), cuboidal where secretion/absorption happens (kidney tubules, glands), columnar where heavy absorption happens (stomach, intestine - often brush-bordered with microvilli), and ciliated where fluid must be moved (fallopian tubes move the ovum, bronchioles move mucus). Compound (stratified) epithelium is multilayered and protective - skin, buccal cavity, vagina.
Connective tissue - cells and matrix
TissueCells / fibresLocation & job
Areolar (loose)Fibroblasts, macrophages, mast cellsBeneath skin; binds skin to muscle, repair
AdiposeFat-filled adipocytesUnder skin; insulation + energy store
TendonDense white collagen, inelasticMuscle to bone
LigamentYellow elastin, elasticBone to bone
CartilageChondrocytes in lacunaeNose tip, ear, joints - pliable support
BoneOsteocytes, Haversian canalsRigid support, calcium store
BloodRBC/WBC/platelets in plasmaFluid transport tissue, no fibres
Fig: Simple epithelia - shape tells function: flat for diffusion, cube for secretion, tall for absorption, cilia for movement.
Muscle in one glance: skeletal fibres are long, cylindrical, multinucleate and striated (voluntary); smooth fibres are spindle-shaped, uninucleate, non-striated (involuntary - gut, vessels, iris); cardiac fibres are branched, uninucleate, striated with intercalated discs (involuntary, rhythmic). Neuroglia exceeds half the volume of neural tissue - neurons alone do not fill the brain.
NEET Practice MCQ
1

Tendon differs from ligament in that tendon

Solution: Tendon = dense white collagen fibres, muscle to bone, inelastic. Ligament = yellow elastin fibres, bone to bone, elastic.

Fig: One osteon - bone strength comes from layered mineral around a living vascular core.

Chapter 2 · Topic 1

Animal tissues

Structural Organisation in AnimalsDiversity & OrganisationStep-by-step Topic
All complex animals build from just four basic tissues: epithelial (covering), connective (binding), muscular (movement) and neural (control). Tissues organise into organs, organs into organ systems, systems into the organism - the frog and human chapters that follow are applications of this hierarchy. Epithelia are avascular and sit on basement membranes; connective tissue has spaced cells in secreted matrix; muscle converts ATP to force; neurons trade stability for speed.

Topic in Depth · Animal tissues

Four tissues build every animal: epithelium covers and secretes, connective tissue binds and transports, muscle contracts, nerves signal. Epithelia are avascular sheets on basement membranes; connective tissue spreads cells through secreted matrix (fluid in blood, mineralised in bone); muscle packs contractile proteins; neural tissue trades division for permanent wiring. Organs mix tissues (stomach: epithelium + muscle + nerves + connective), systems mix organs - hierarchy from cells to organism.
NEET Snapshot:
  • 4 tissues - epithelial, connective, muscular, neural.
  • Most complex functions need all four cooperating in one organ.
NEET Practice MCQ
1

Which tissue is avascular and always rests on a basement membrane?

Solution: Epithelia lack vessels (nourished by diffusion) and anchor to basement membranes.

Index / Diversity & Organisation
Chapter 2 · Topic 2

Epithelial tissue

Structural Organisation in AnimalsDiversity & OrganisationStep-by-step Topic
Sheets of closely packed cells on a basement membrane. Simple squamous — alveoli, Bowman's capsule, blood vessel endothelium; cuboidal — kidney tubules, glands; columnar — stomach and intestinal lining (brush-bordered absorptive cells); ciliated — fallopian tubes, bronchioles (move mucus/ova); glandular — unicellular (goblet cells) and multicellular glands. Compound epithelium (multilayered) covers dry surfaces like skin for protection. Cell junctions: tight junctions stop leakage, adhering junctions cement neighbours, gap junctions let ions and small molecules pass for coordinated beating.
NEET facts:
  • Inner cheek lining and vagina are compound (stratified) epithelium, not simple.
  • All complex animals have only four basic tissue types: epithelial, connective, muscular, neural.

Deeper Understanding · Glands and junctions

Glands are just specialised epithelia: unicellular goblet cells vs multicellular salivary glands; exocrine glands keep ducts (saliva, sweat) while endocrine glands lose them (thyroid, pituitary). Cell junctions divide labour precisely: tight junctions seal (gut lining, blood-brain barrier), adhering/desmosomes rivet cells against stress (skin, heart), and gap junctions wire cells electrically together (cardiac muscle, smooth muscle) for synchronous beating.

Topic in Depth · Epithelial tissue

Epithelial shape is destiny: flat squamous cells minimise diffusion distance (alveoli, Bowman capsule, endothelium), cubes suit secretion (glands, kidney tubules), tall columnar cells maximise absorption (intestine, often with microvilli brush borders and mucus goblet cells), cilia add conveyor motion (trachea moves mucus up, tubes move ova down). Where wear is heavy, epithelium stacks into protective compound layers (skin, oesophagus, vagina). Glands are epithelia that folded inward - exocrine keeps ducts, endocrine loses them.
NEET Snapshot:
  • Simple (one layer): absorption/secretion. Compound (many): protection.
  • Ciliated: fallopian tubes + bronchioles. Glandular: goblet (unicellular) + glands.
Remember
  • Brush border = microvilli absorption boost. Keratinised = waterproof skin.
NEET Practice MCQ
1

Brush-bordered columnar epithelium lines the

Solution: Microvilli-topped columnar cells maximise nutrient absorption in the small intestine.
Back to Index
Index / Diversity & Organisation
Chapter 2 · Topic 3

Connective tissue

Structural Organisation in AnimalsDiversity & OrganisationStep-by-step Topic
Cells spaced in a secreted matrix. Areolar (loose, beneath skin — fibroblasts, macrophages, mast cells), adipose (fat storage, insulation), tendon (dense regular white fibres — muscle to bone), ligament (yellow elastin fibres — bone to bone), cartilage (chondrocytes in lacunae; solid yet pliable matrix), bone (osteocytes in lacunae, Haversian canals, rich in calcium salts + collagen), blood (fluid matrix without fibres).

Tendon and ligament look similar. How do their fibres and attachments differ?

Answer: Tendon = dense white collagen fibres joining muscle to bone (inelastic); ligament = yellow elastin fibres joining bone to bone (elastic).

Deeper Understanding · Bone vs cartilage matrix

Cartilage matrix is solid yet pliable (chondrin) with chondrocytes in lacunae and no blood vessels - which is why cartilage heals slowly. Bone matrix is hard (calcium salts + collagen) and richly vascular through Haversian canals, each canal surrounded by concentric lamellae of osteocytes - a cut bone bleeds but repairs fast. Marrow inside long bones makes blood cells (red marrow) or stores fat (yellow marrow). Tendons and ligaments are both dense connective tissue; only the fibre type (white collagen vs yellow elastin) differs.

Topic in Depth · Connective tissue

Connective tissue is cells plus the matrix they secrete: loose areolar packing (beneath skin, with fibroblasts-macrophages-mast cells for repair and defence), adipose energy vaults, dense tendons (white collagen, inelastic, muscle-to-bone) and ligaments (yellow elastin, stretchy, bone-to-bone), cartilage (chondrocytes in lacunae, avascular, slow healing) and bone (osteocytes, Haversian canals, fast healing), plus fluid blood. The matrix - not the cells - decides each type mechanical character.
NEET Snapshot:
  • Tendon: white collagen, muscle-bone. Ligament: yellow elastin, bone-bone.
  • Cartilage avascular (slow repair); bone vascular (fast repair).
NEET Practice MCQ
1

Haversian canals occur in

Solution: Bone osteons centre on Haversian canals carrying vessels and nerves; cartilage has none.
Back to Index
Index / Diversity & Organisation
Chapter 2 · Topic 4

Muscular tissue

Structural Organisation in AnimalsDiversity & OrganisationStep-by-step Topic
Skeletal — long cylindrical multinucleate fibres with dark/light bands (striated), voluntary, attached by tendons; smooth — spindle-shaped uninucleate fibres without striations, involuntary (gut wall, blood vessels, iris); cardiac — branched uninucleate fibres with intercalated discs, striated yet involuntary, rhythmic contraction of the heart.

Deeper Understanding · Why cardiac muscle never tires

Cardiac fibres combine skeletal features (striations, powerful) with smooth features (involuntary, single nucleus) and add two unique ones: branching and intercalated discs packed with gap junctions. The discs spread each SA-node impulse across the whole atrium in milliseconds, so the heart contracts as one unit. Rich mitochondria (about 25% of cell volume) and myoglobin supply aerobic endurance - cardiac muscle essentially cannot fatigue under normal oxygen supply.

Topic in Depth · Muscular tissue

Muscle fibres are protein machines: skeletal fibres (long, cylindrical, multinucleate, striated, voluntary - attached by tendons, fatigue-prone), smooth fibres (spindle, uninucleate, non-striated, involuntary - gut walls, vessels, iris, fatigue-resistant), cardiac fibres (branched, uninucleate, striated with intercalated discs - involuntary, rhythmic, near-unfatiguable). Red fibres (myoglobin-rich, aerobic, slow posture work) versus white fibres (fast glycolytic sprints) tune each muscle to its duty.
NEET Snapshot:
  • Skeletal: voluntary + striated. Smooth: involuntary + plain. Cardiac: involuntary + striated + discs.
  • Intercalated discs with gap junctions synchronise the heartbeat.
NEET Practice MCQ
1

Intercalated discs are found in

Solution: Discs electrically couple cardiac fibres so the heart contracts as one unit.
Back to Index
Index / Diversity & Organisation
Chapter 2 · Topic 5

Nervous tissue

Structural Organisation in AnimalsDiversity & OrganisationStep-by-step Topic
Neurons (cell body with Nissl granules, dendrites in, single axon out) are supported by neuroglia forming over half of neural tissue volume - astrocytes, oligodendrocytes, microglia and ependymal cells. Myelinated axons (Schwann cells in PNS, nodes of Ranvier between) conduct far faster than bare ones. Unlike most tissues, mature neurons barely divide - brain injuries heal poorly, and glial scars block regrowth.

Topic in Depth · Nervous tissue

Neurons are extreme specialists: meter-long axons in legs, thousands of dendritic inputs in cortex, zero division after maturity - supported by glia that outnumber and out-volume them (astrocytes feed and seal the blood-brain barrier, oligodendrocytes/Schwann cells myelinate, microglia defend, ependymal cells move CSF). Myelin (white matter) versus cell bodies (grey matter) colours the brain; nodes of Ranvier enable 100 m/s saltatory jumps versus 1 m/s bare-fibre crawl.
NEET Snapshot:
  • Neurons signal; glia support, feed, insulate and defend.
  • Grey = cell bodies. White = myelinated tracts.
Remember
  • Mature neurons barely divide - CNS injuries heal poorly; PNS can regenerate.
NEET Practice MCQ
1

Myelin sheath in the peripheral nervous system is formed by

Solution: Schwann cells wrap PNS axons (one cell per segment); oligodendrocytes do the CNS equivalent.

Index / Diversity & Organisation
Chapter 2 · Topic 6

Cockroach

Structural Organisation in AnimalsDiversity & OrganisationStep-by-step Topic
Periplaneta americana (American cockroach): nocturnal omnivore with three body regions - head (compound eyes, antennae, biting-chewing mouthparts), thorax (3 segments; 3 leg pairs, 2 wing pairs - forewings as leathery tegmina), abdomen (10 segments; anal cerci, gonapophyses). Brown chitinous exoskeleton of hardened sclerites joined by soft arthrodial membrane. Hardy tracheal breathing and open circulation let it survive weeks without its head.
Fig: Three tagmata - sensory head, locomotor thorax, visceral abdomen.

Topic in Depth · Cockroach

Periplaneta americana is the nocturnal survivor: flattened brown body slipping into crevices, biting-chewing mouthparts eating anything organic, leathery tegmina covering membranous hindwings, three leg pairs plus antennae and cerci sensing every vibration. Open circulation plus tracheal breathing slashes energy needs - surviving a month headless (spiracles breathe, ganglia reflex-walk) until dehydration wins. Sclerite armour with flexible arthrodial membranes balances protection and mobility.
NEET Snapshot:
  • Head (sense) - thorax (move) - abdomen (viscera).
  • Tegmina protect; hindwings fly. Cerci sense rear threats.
NEET Practice MCQ
1

Tegmina in cockroach are

Solution: Leathery forewings (tegmina) shield the membranous flight wings beneath.

Index / Diversity & Organisation
Chapter 2 · Topic 7

Digestive system

Structural Organisation in AnimalsDiversity & OrganisationStep-by-step Topic
Cockroach gut in order: mouth - pharynx - oesophagus - crop (food storage) - gizzard (grinding teeth) - midgut/mesenteron lined with hepatic caecae (digestive enzymes at the foregut-midgut junction) - hindgut (ileum, colon, rectum). Malpighian tubules open at the midgut-hindgut junction (excretion, not digestion). Salivary glands pour into the mouth; the whole foregut and hindgut are cuticle-lined (ectodermal), only the midgut is endodermal.

Related Diagram · Cockroach gut regions

Fig: Store in crop, grind in gizzard, digest in midgut, absorb water in hindgut.

Topic in Depth · Digestive system

Cockroach gut is a processing line: mouthparts shred, saliva moistens, oesophagus delivers to the expandable crop warehouse, the toothed gizzard mill grinds, midgut (with hepatic caecae pouring enzymes at the entry) digests and absorbs, hindgut reclaims water before dry pellets exit. Foregut and hindgut wear cuticle (moulted with skin); only midgut is absorptive endoderm. Malpighian tubules dock exactly at the midgut-hindgut border - digestion ends where excretion begins.
NEET Snapshot:
  • Crop stores, gizzard grinds, midgut digests, hindgut dries.
  • Hepatic caecae ring the midgut entry like enzyme taps.
NEET Practice MCQ
1

Hepatic caecae in cockroach open into the

Solution: Digestive enzyme tubules ring the anterior midgut at the foregut-midgut junction.
Back to Index
Index / Diversity & Organisation
Chapter 2 · Topic 8

Circulatory system

Structural Organisation in AnimalsDiversity & OrganisationStep-by-step Topic
Open circulation: colourless haemolymph (no haemoglobin, no oxygen transport - tracheae do that) bathes tissues directly in sinuses. The dorsal heart is a 13-chambered tube (10 abdominal + 3 thoracic) with segmental ostia (valved inlets) and alary muscles that pump it. Blood flows forward into the head sinus and percolates back - low pressure, slow, but sufficient for a small tracheate animal.

Related Diagram · 13-chambered dorsal heart

Fig: Open system: valved ostia admit haemolymph, alary muscles drive it headward.

Topic in Depth · Circulatory system

Open circulation suits small tracheate bodies: a 13-chambered dorsal heart (10 abdominal + 3 thoracic) sucks haemolymph through segmental ostia and pumps it forward into head sinuses, from where it percolates back through tissue spaces - no capillaries, no highways, just flooding and draining. Alary musclesPulse each chamber; colourless haemolymph carries nutrients and wastes but almost no oxygen (tracheae deliver that). Low pressure, low cost, perfectly adequate.
NEET Snapshot:
  • Open: sinuses, not vessels. 13 heart chambers with ostia.
  • Haemolymph has no haemoglobin - breathing bypasses blood.
Remember
  • Cockroach blood is colourless because oxygen travels by air tubes, not blood.
NEET Practice MCQ
1

Cockroach heart has how many chambers?

Solution: Ten abdominal plus three thoracic chambers pump haemolymph forward through ostia.
Back to Index
Index / Diversity & Organisation
Chapter 2 · Topic 9

Respiratory system

Structural Organisation in AnimalsDiversity & OrganisationStep-by-step Topic
Cockroach breathes through a tracheal system: 10 pairs of lateral spiracles (2 thoracic + 8 abdominal, guarded by valves) open into branching tracheae ending in fluid-filled tracheoles that touch every cell. Oxygen diffuses directly to tissues - no blood pigment, no lungs needed. Spiracles close to prevent water loss, opening the system only during activity; this is why insects dominate dry land.

Related Diagram · Tracheal breathing

Fig: Spiracles admit air into branching tracheae ending in fluid-filled tracheoles at each cell.

Topic in Depth · Respiratory system

Ten spiracle pairs (2 thoracic + 8 abdominal) with closable valves open into branching tracheae ending in fluid-filled tracheoles touching every cell - air delivered door-to-door, no blood middleman. Active insects ventilate by abdominal pumping; resting ones use diffusion plus discontinuous gas exchange (spiracles cycling open/closed) to hoard water. This waterproof breathing is why insects rule deserts that defeat lung-breathers.
NEET Snapshot:
  • Spiracles (10 pairs) - tracheae - tracheoles (fluid-tipped).
  • Valves + discontinuous exchange = desert-proof water saving.
NEET Practice MCQ
1

In cockroach, gaseous exchange occurs directly at the level of

Solution: Tracheoles deliver air to individual cells; spiracles are just gated entrances.
Back to Index
Index / Diversity & Organisation
Chapter 2 · Topic 10

Nervous system

Structural Organisation in AnimalsDiversity & OrganisationStep-by-step Topic
Cockroach nervous system: a dorsal brain (supra-oesophageal ganglion), a circum-oesophageal nerve ring, and a double ventral nerve cord with 3 thoracic + 6 abdominal ganglia - fused in the abdomen (segments 7-10 share ganglia). Thoracic ganglia independently drive legs and wings, so a headless cockroach still walks reflexively for weeks. Compound eyes (mosaic vision, more sensitive at night) plus antennae (smell, touch) make it a nocturnal escape specialist.

Topic in Depth · Nervous system

Cockroach control is decentralised: dorsal brain (learning, vision, antennae) linked by a circum-oesophageal ring to a double ventral cord with 3 thoracic + 6 abdominal ganglia - each ganglion a local reflex computer for its segment. Thoracic ganglia drive legs/wings independently, so decapitated bodies walk, groom and right themselves for weeks. Compound eyes give mosaic night vision; antennae smell and touch; anal cerci trigger lightning escape sprints.
NEET Snapshot:
  • Brain + nerve ring + 3 thoracic + 6 abdominal ganglia.
  • Segmental ganglia = local reflex autonomy (headless walking).
NEET Practice MCQ
1

The ventral nerve cord of cockroach carries

Solution: Three thoracic plus six abdominal ganglia (posterior segments share) run segmental reflexes.

Index / Diversity & Organisation
Chapter 2 · Topic 11

Reproductive system

Structural Organisation in AnimalsDiversity & OrganisationStep-by-step Topic
Cockroach sexes separate with clear dimorphism. Male: paired testes, mushroom (utricular) gland, ejaculatory duct - spermatophores transferred via asymmetrical genitalia. Female: paired ovaries, spermathecae (sperm storage), colleterial glands secreting the ootheca - a purse-like egg case holding about 16 fertilised eggs, carried at the abdomen tip then deposited. Development is paurometabolous (egg to nymph to adult, no pupa).

Topic in Depth · Reproductive system

Cockroach sexes are separate and dimorphic: males wield asymmetrical genitalia with mushroom (utricular) glands packaging spermatophores; females carry paired ovaries, spermathecae storing sperm, and colleterial glands building the purse-like ootheca (~16 eggs) glued in crevices. Development is paurometabolous - nymphs hatch as mini-adults, moulting through instars without pupae. One ootheca per mating cycle keeps fecundity high with minimal parental cost.
NEET Snapshot:
  • Male: testes + mushroom gland + spermatophores.
  • Female: ovaries + spermathecae + ootheca (16 eggs). Paurometabolous development.
Remember
  • Ootheca = portable nursery; spermatheca = sperm bank between matings.
NEET Practice MCQ
1

Ootheca of cockroach contains about

Solution: Each purse-like ootheca holds roughly 16 fertilised eggs in two neat rows.

Human Physiology - Digestion and Absorption

Alimentary canal, glands, enzymes for every nutrient, absorption and disorders.

Human Physiology - Digestion and AbsorptionHuman PhysiologyChapter Introduction

Subtopics in this Chapter

1. Human digestive system2. Digestive glands3. Digestive enzymes4. Digestion of carbohydrates5. Digestion of proteins6. Digestion of fats7. Absorption8. Assimilation9. Digestive disorders

Deep Dive · Digestion master tables

Digestive glands - what each pours and where it acts
GlandSecretion & enzymesAction (note the pH)
Salivary (3 pairs)Ptyalin (salivary amylase), lysozymeStarch to maltose at pH 6.8; lysozyme kills bacteria
Gastric glandsHCl, pepsinogen, mucus, intrinsic factorHCl activates pepsin; pepsin digests protein at pH 1.8; intrinsic factor absorbs B12
LiverBile (no enzymes)Emulsifies fats; neutralises acidic chyme with bile salts
PancreasTrypsinogen, chymotrypsin, amylase, lipaseEnterokinase activates trypsin; proteins, starch and fats digested at pH 7.8
Intestinal glandsMaltase, sucrase, lactase, peptidases, lipaseDisaccharides to glucose; peptides to amino acids
Hormonal control is the deep layer NEET loves: gastrin (stomach) stimulates gastric juice; secretin (duodenum) triggers bicarbonate-rich pancreatic juice to neutralise acid; CCK (cholecystokinin) contracts the gall bladder and releases pancreatic enzymes; GIP slows gastric emptying. Absorption recap: glucose and amino acids enter blood capillaries, while fatty acids + glycerol rebuild into chylomicrons and enter lacteals (lymph) - fat never goes directly to blood from the villus.
Fig: Food path - mouth, oesophagus, stomach, small intestine (digestion + absorption), large intestine (water absorption). Liver and pancreas are helper glands.

1

Enterokinase, secreted by the intestinal mucosa, functions to

Solution: Enterokinase activates trypsinogen to trypsin; trypsin then activates the other pancreatic proenzymes - a cascade.

Chapter 3 · Topic 1

Human digestive system

Human Physiology - Digestion and AbsorptionHuman PhysiologyStep-by-step Topic
Mouth → buccal cavity → pharynx → oesophagus → stomach → small intestine (duodenum, jejunum, ileum) → large intestine (caecum with vermiform appendix, colon, rectum) → anus. Human teeth are thecodont (in sockets), diphyodont (milk + permanent sets) and heterodont — dental formula 2123/2123 (incisors, canines, premolars, molars per half-jaw). The stomach's four parts (cardiac, fundus, body, pyloric) churn food with HCl and pepsin; the small intestine is the main digestive-absorptive organ with villi and microvilli.
NEET facts:
  • Wisdom teeth are the third molars; canines tear, molars grind.
  • Goblet cells secrete mucus protecting the gut wall from acid and enzymes.

Deeper Understanding · Teeth formula and gut movement

Dental formula 2123/2123 means per half-jaw: 2 incisors + 1 canine + 2 premolars + 3 molars = 8 teeth, times 4 halves = 32 permanent teeth (milk set is 2102 = 20). Food moves by peristalsis - wave-like smooth-muscle contraction behind the bolus and relaxation ahead - controlled by the enteric nervous system, so digestion continues even during sleep. Sphincters (cardiac/pyloric in stomach, ileocaecal, anal) act as one-way gates between chambers.

Topic in Depth · Human digestive system

The gut is a 8 to 10 metre one-way tube where each chamber prepares food for the next: mouth shreds and moistens, oesophagus transits by peristalsis, stomach churns in acid, small intestine digests plus absorbs, large intestine reclaims water. Accessory glands (salivary, gastric, liver, pancreas, intestinal) pour in at exact stations. One-way flow plus sphincter gates prevents back-mixing - vomiting and reflux are the painful exceptions that prove the rule.
NEET Snapshot:
  • Order: mouth - oesophagus - stomach - small intestine - large intestine - anus.
  • Small intestine (duodenum, jejunum, ileum) does most digestion and absorption.
  • Dental formula 2123/2123 = 32 permanent teeth; wisdom teeth are third molars.
Remember
  • Peristalsis runs the whole journey - wave behind, relaxation ahead.
NEET Practice MCQ
1

The longest part of the alimentary canal is the

Solution: The small intestine (about 6 metres) provides the surface and time for digestion plus absorption.

Chapter 3 · Topic 2

Digestive glands

Human Physiology - Digestion and AbsorptionHuman PhysiologyStep-by-step Topic
Saliva (3 pairs of salivary glands): ptyalin/salivary amylase digests starch at pH 6.8; lysozyme kills bacteria. Gastric juice: HCl (acid medium, kills germs, activates pepsinogen → pepsin for proteins), mucus, Castle's intrinsic factor (B12 absorption). Pancreatic juice: trypsinogen → trypsin (activated by enterokinase), chymotrypsin, carboxypeptidase, pancreatic amylase and lipase. Bile (liver — largest gland; stored in gall bladder): no enzymes, but emulsifies fats and neutralises acidic chyme with bile salts.

Why doesn't the stomach digest itself although it secretes strong acid and pepsin?

Answer: A thick mucus-bicarbonate layer shields the mucosa, pepsin is secreted as inactive pepsinogen, and epithelial cells regenerate rapidly.

Deeper Understanding · Enzyme activation cascades

Digestive proteases are made inactive so they cannot digest their own gland: pepsinogen converts to pepsin only in stomach HCl, and trypsinogen converts to trypsin only when it meets enterokinase in the duodenum - then trypsin activates chymotrypsin and carboxypeptidase in a cascade. Bile contains zero enzymes; its bile salts are detergents that shatter fat globules into micelles, multiplying surface area thousands of times for lipase. This is why bile-duct obstruction causes fatty, clay-coloured stools.

Related Diagram · Digestive glands map

Fig: Five glands, five jobs - saliva starts starch, stomach starts protein, pancreas does bulk, intestine finishes.

Topic in Depth · Digestive glands

Five glands divide the chemical labour: salivary glands start starch plus kill germs (lysozyme), gastric glands deploy acid plus pepsin, the liver supplies detergent bile, the pancreas fires the broadest enzyme battery, and intestinal glands finish with brush-border enzymes. Exocrine glands keep ducts; the pancreas doubles as an endocrine islet organ. Gland failure patterns diagnose disease - no bile means fatty stools, no intrinsic factor means B12 anaemia.
NEET Snapshot:
  • Salivary: ptyalin + lysozyme. Gastric: HCl + pepsin + mucus + intrinsic factor.
  • Liver is the largest gland and makes bile with zero enzymes.
Gland outputs
PointDetail
Salivary (3 pairs)Starch to maltose; germ killing
GastricProtein to peptones in acid
LiverFat emulsification + acid neutralising
PancreasAll three nutrients at pH 7.8
IntestinalFinal disaccharide + peptide splitting
NEET Practice MCQ
1

Castle intrinsic factor, needed for B12 absorption, is secreted by

Solution: Parietal cells make both HCl and intrinsic factor; its loss causes pernicious anaemia.

Chapter 3 · Topic 3

Digestive enzymes

Human Physiology - Digestion and AbsorptionHuman PhysiologyStep-by-step Topic
Master enzyme map: carbohydrates - ptyalin (mouth, pH 6.8), pancreatic amylase (intestine), then maltase/sucrase/lactase at the brush border; proteins - pepsin (stomach, pH 1.8), then trypsin/chymotrypsin/carboxypeptidase (pancreatic) and peptidases (intestinal); fats - only pancreatic lipase after bile emulsification. Protection logic: proteases arrive as zymogens (pepsinogen, trypsinogen) and activate on site - enterokinase triggers the intestinal cascade.
NEET facts:
  • Bile has no enzymes; succus entericus completes what pancreatic juice starts.

Nutrient-wise digestion chart

NutrientMouthStomachSmall intestine
CarbohydratePtyalin → maltoseStops (acid)Pancreatic amylase → disaccharides; maltase, sucrase, lactase → glucose
ProteinNonePepsin → peptonesTrypsin/chymotrypsin → peptides; peptidases → amino acids
FatNoneLittleBile emulsifies; lipase → fatty acids + glycerol
Common mistakes: carbohydrate digestion stops in stomach acid and resumes in the intestine; fat digestion happens only in the small intestine.

Topic in Depth · Digestive enzymes

Enzymes work in pH-matched relays: salivary amylase at near-neutral pH 6.8, pepsin at acid pH 1.8, pancreatic and intestinal enzymes at alkaline pH 7.8. Proteases ship as inactive zymogens (pepsinogen, trypsinogen) and activate on site - enterokinase triggers the intestinal cascade - so glands never digest themselves. Each nutrient needs its own enzyme family; no single juice digests everything, which is why the gut uses five stations.
NEET Snapshot:
  • Amylases: starch. Proteases: protein. Lipase: fat. One family per nutrient.
  • Zymogen-to-enzyme activation protects glands from self-digestion.
NEET Practice MCQ
1

Enterokinase activates

Solution: Intestinal enterokinase converts trypsinogen to trypsin, which then activates the remaining pancreatic proenzymes.

Chapter 3 · Topic 4

Digestion of carbohydrates

Human Physiology - Digestion and AbsorptionHuman PhysiologyStep-by-step Topic
Carbohydrate digestion starts in the mouth (ptyalin converts starch to maltose, ~30% done before acid stops it in the stomach) and finishes in the small intestine: pancreatic amylase breaks remaining starch to disaccharides, then brush-border maltase, sucrase and lactase release glucose, fructose and galactose. Cellulose and most fibre escape digestion entirely (roughage for peristalsis). Lactase loss after childhood causes milk intolerance in most of the world population.

Related Diagram · Carbohydrate sites in order

Fig: Mouth starts, stomach pauses, intestine completes - enzymes matched to pH at each stop.

Topic in Depth · Digestion of carbohydrates

Starch digestion is a three-act play: mouth ptyalin clips starch to maltose (about 30 percent before stomach acid stops it), pancreatic amylase resumes in the intestine down to disaccharides, and brush-border maltase, sucrase and lactase release glucose, fructose and galactose for absorption. Cellulose escapes entirely as roughage. Lactase decline after childhood makes most of the world milk-intolerant - persistence is the mutant exception, common mainly in pastoral populations.
NEET Snapshot:
  • Mouth: starch to maltose. Intestine: disaccharides to simple sugars.
  • Lactase loss with age is normal worldwide; persistence is the exception.
NEET Practice MCQ
1

Sucrase (invertase) acts in the

Solution: Brush-border sucrase splits sucrose into glucose plus fructose at the absorptive surface.

Index / Human Physiology
Chapter 3 · Topic 5

Digestion of proteins

Human Physiology - Digestion and AbsorptionHuman PhysiologyStep-by-step Topic
Protein digestion is a relay: stomach pepsin (activated from pepsinogen by HCl) curdles milk via rennin in infants and chops proteins to peptones; pancreatic trypsin/chymotrypsin cut them to small peptides; intestinal peptidases and dipeptidases finish to amino acids. Each handoff uses a specific pH - acidic stomach, alkaline intestine - which is why antacid abuse impairs protein digestion. Castle intrinsic factor from the same gastric glands separately enables B12 absorption.

Topic in Depth · Digestion of proteins

Protein unwinding needs the harshest chemistry: stomach HCl denatures (unfolds) proteins and activates pepsin, which chops them to peptones; pancreatic trypsin, chymotrypsin and carboxypeptidase cut peptones to small peptides; intestinal peptidases finish to amino acids. Acid plus mucus balance is delicate - mucus-bicarbonate shielding plus rapid cell renewal stops the stomach digesting itself. Infants add rennin to curdle milk for longer pepsin action.
NEET Snapshot:
  • Stomach: pepsin in acid. Pancreas: trypsin team. Intestine: peptidases finish.
  • Denature first (acid), then chop (enzymes) - order matters.
Remember
  • Pepsin needs acid; antacid overuse therefore weakens protein digestion.
NEET Practice MCQ
1

Protein digestion begins in the

Solution: Pepsin starts protein breakdown in stomach acid; mouth and intestine handle other nutrients first.
Back to Index
Index / Human Physiology
Chapter 3 · Topic 6

Digestion of fats

Human Physiology - Digestion and AbsorptionHuman PhysiologyStep-by-step Topic
Fat digestion is physics before chemistry: bile salts emulsify globules into micelles, multiplying surface area; pancreatic lipase then splits triglycerides into fatty acids + monoglycerides. These rebuild into triglycerides inside gut cells, get packed with protein into chylomicrons, and exit via lacteals into lymph - the only nutrient that reaches blood via lymph first. Bile-duct obstruction therefore causes fatty, foul, clay-coloured stools (steatorrhoea).

Related Diagram · Fat route: micelle to lacteal

Fig: Emulsify, digest, rebuild, lymph - fat is the only nutrient exiting via lacteals.

Topic in Depth · Digestion of fats

Fat digestion is physics before chemistry: water-insoluble globules expose too little surface, so bile salts first emulsify them into micelles - multiplying surface area thousands of times - and only then does pancreatic lipase split triglycerides into fatty acids plus monoglycerides. Products rebuild into triglycerides inside gut cells, get packed with protein into chylomicrons, and exit via lacteals into lymph - the only nutrient routed through lymph first. Bile-duct blockage therefore means fatty, clay-coloured stools.
NEET Snapshot:
  • Emulsify (bile) first, digest (lipase) second, export via lymph.
  • Chylomicrons enter lacteals, not blood capillaries.
NEET Practice MCQ
1

Bile salts help fat digestion by

Solution: Bile is a detergent, not an enzyme - it shatters globules so lipase can reach them.
Back to Index
Index / Human Physiology
Chapter 3 · Topic 7

Absorption

Human Physiology - Digestion and AbsorptionHuman PhysiologyStep-by-step Topic
Final products (glucose, amino acids) pass into blood capillaries of villi, while fatty acids and glycerol enter lacteals (lymph) as chylomicrons. Absorption is active and passive; water and minerals absorb along the whole gut, maximally in the large intestine. Assimilation is the use of absorbed nutrients in tissues — excess glucose stored as glycogen in the liver. Disorders: kwashiorkar (protein lack with oedema) vs marasmus (protein + energy lack, no oedema), jaundice, constipation.
Common mistakes: bile contains no digestive enzymes — a favourite assertion-reason trap.

Deeper Understanding · Protein-energy malnutrition

Kwashiorkor (protein lack with adequate calories): oedema, pot belly, reddish hair, stunted growth - classically after a second child is born and the first is weaned off protein-rich milk. Marasmus (protein + energy lack): no oedema, matchstick limbs, old-man face. Assimilation means tissues actually use absorbed nutrients: excess glucose becomes glycogen (liver, muscle), excess amino acids are deaminated (urea out), excess fat stays fat. Constipation, indigestion and piles are lifestyle disorders of the same tract.

Topic in Depth · Absorption

Absorption is selective transport, not passive soaking: glucose and amino acids ride active carriers into blood capillaries of villi and microvilli (huge surface, rich blood flow), while fats take the lymphatic lacteal route as chylomicrons. Water and minerals absorb along the whole gut, maximally in the large intestine. Alcohol and some drugs absorb directly through the stomach lining - which is why they act fast and why ulcers worsen with drinking.
NEET Snapshot:
  • Villi + microvilli multiply surface hundreds of times.
  • Blood route: sugars, amino acids. Lymph route: fats.
Absorption routes
PointDetail
Glucose, amino acidsBlood capillaries to liver
Fatty acids, glycerolLacteals as chylomicrons
Water, mineralsWhole gut, mainly large intestine
NEET Practice MCQ
1

Villi are absent in the

Solution: Villi maximise small-intestine absorption; the colon absorbs water through flat mucosa instead.

Index / Human Physiology
Chapter 3 · Topic 8

Assimilation

Human Physiology - Digestion and AbsorptionHuman PhysiologyStep-by-step Topic
Assimilation is tissues actually using absorbed nutrients: glucose oxidised for ATP or stored as glycogen (liver, muscle) and fat; amino acids built into proteins or deaminated (liver converts ammonia to urea); fatty acids oxidised or stored in adipose. Hormones route the traffic - insulin drives storage after meals, glucagon mobilises stores between meals. Egestion (faeces out) is not excretion (metabolic waste out) - undigested fibre was never inside body cells at all.

Topic in Depth · Assimilation

Assimilation puts absorbed nutrients to work: glucose oxidised for ATP, stored as glycogen (liver, muscle) or converted to fat; amino acids built into proteins (or deaminated to urea when surplus); fatty acids oxidised or stored in adipose. Insulin drives post-meal storage while glucagon mobilises reserves between meals - the push-pull keeping blood sugar steady. Egestion (faeces out) differs fundamentally from excretion (metabolic waste out): undigested fibre never entered body cells at all.
NEET Snapshot:
  • Use it (oxidise), store it (glycogen, fat), or dump nitrogen (urea).
  • Insulin stores; glucagon mobilises - steady blood sugar needs both.
NEET Practice MCQ
1

Excess amino acids in the body are mainly

Solution: Surplus amino acids lose ammonia (urea cycle) and their skeletons burn as fuel - protein is not stored.

Chapter 3 · Topic 9

Digestive disorders

Human Physiology - Digestion and AbsorptionHuman PhysiologyStep-by-step Topic
Jaundice (bile pigments in blood - yellow eyes, liver fault), constipation (excess water absorption - needs fibre + fluids), indigestion (incomplete digestion, enzyme/antacid relief), piles/haemorrhoids (swollen anal veins from straining), diarrhoea (under-absorption - ORS replaces water + salts), vomiting (reverse peristalsis via medullary centre). Protein-energy malnutrition: kwashiorkor (protein lack, oedema) vs marasmus (total energy lack, no oedema).

Topic in Depth · Digestive disorders

Gut disorders map to failed steps: jaundice (liver cannot clear bilirubin - yellow eyes), constipation (excess water absorption - needs fibre plus fluids), piles (strained anal veins), diarrhoea (under-absorption - ORS replaces water plus salts), vomiting (reverse peristalsis from the medullary centre), indigestion (incomplete digestion). Protein-energy malnutrition splits by symptom: kwashiorkor (protein lack with oedema) versus marasmus (total energy lack, no oedema).
NEET Snapshot:
  • Jaundice = liver fault. ORS = water plus salts for diarrhoea.
  • Kwashiorkor has oedema; marasmus does not.
Remember
  • Match disorder to failed step: bile (fat stools), enzymes (indigestion), absorption (diarrhoea).
NEET Practice MCQ
1

Kwashiorkor differs from marasmus in showing

Solution: Kwashiorkor is protein deficiency with oedema and pot belly; marasmus is total energy lack without oedema.

Chapter 4 · Step-by-step

Breathing and Exchange of Gases

Lungs, breathing mechanics, volumes, gas transport and regulation.

Breathing and Exchange of GasesHuman PhysiologyChapter Introduction

Subtopics in this Chapter

1. Respiratory system2. Lungs3. Mechanism of breathing4. Inspiration5. Expiration6. Lung volumes7. Lung capacities8. Gas exchange9. Oxygen transport10. CO2 transport11. Respiratory regulation12. Respiratory disorders

Deep Dive · Breathing numbers and transport

Capacities are sums, not new measurements: Vital Capacity = TV + IRV + ERV (3400-4800 mL); Inspiratory Capacity = TV + IRV; Functional Residual Capacity = ERV + RV; Total Lung Capacity = VC + RV (about 5800 mL). NEET repeatedly asks which volumes include Residual Volume - only FRC and TLC do; VC never includes RV. During inspiration the diaphragm flattens and ribs lift (external intercostals), thoracic volume rises and air flows in; expiration at rest is passive recoil.
Gas transport - the exact percentages
GasAs carbamino / oxyhaemoglobinDissolved / bicarbonate
Oxygen~97% as oxyhaemoglobin~3% dissolved in plasma
Carbon dioxide~20-25% as carbaminohaemoglobin~70% as bicarbonates, ~7% dissolved
Bohr effect: high CO2, H+, temperature and 2,3-BPG shift the oxygen dissociation curve right - haemoglobin unloads O2 exactly in respiring tissues. Haldane effect: oxygenation of blood in lungs displaces CO2 from haemoglobin, aiding exhalation. Carbonic anhydrase in RBCs converts CO2 + water to carbonic acid, which splits into H+ and bicarbonate - the chloride shift keeps electrical balance.
Fig: Exchange across the respiratory membrane - O2 diffuses into blood, CO2 diffuses out, both down partial-pressure gradients.
Regulation centres
  • Medulla holds the rhythm centre; pons holds the pneumotaxic centre; chemosensitive area tracks CO2 and H+, not O2.
  • Breathing rate in a healthy adult is 12-16 per minute.
NEET Practice MCQ
1

About 97% of oxygen is transported in blood as

Solution: 97% rides as oxyhaemoglobin; only ~3% dissolves in plasma. For CO2 the major route is bicarbonate (~70%).

Fig: Four volumes stack into capacities - the grey residual block is invisible to spirometry.

Chapter 4 · Topic 1

Respiratory system

Breathing and Exchange of GasesHuman PhysiologyStep-by-step Topic
Nostrils → nasal passage → pharynx → larynx (sound box) → trachea (C-shaped cartilaginous rings) → bronchi → bronchioles → alveoli (thin squamous walls, rich capillaries — the exchange surface). The lungs sit in the thoracic chamber formed by ribs, sternum, diaphragm and dome-shaped muscles; pleural fluid lubricates the pleura.
NEET facts:
  • Sound is produced by the larynx, not the pharynx.
  • Surfactant (lecithin) in alveoli lowers surface tension and prevents collapse.

Deeper Understanding · Surfactant and pleura

Alveoli would collapse under surface tension (Laplace law: smaller bubble, higher collapsing pressure) without surfactant - a lecithin-rich secretion of pneumocytes that lowers surface tension. Premature babies lacking surfactant develop respiratory distress syndrome. The double-layered pleura with lubricating pleural fluid lets lungs glide against the chest wall; inflammation (pleurisy) makes every breath painful. The trachea resists collapse during inhalation suction because of its C-shaped cartilage rings (open ends face the oesophagus so food can pass).

Topic in Depth · Respiratory system

The airway is a protected funnel: nostrils filter and warm air, pharynx shares with food (epiglottis guards the larynx during swallowing), larynx (sound box) plus C-ringed trachea stay open under suction, bronchi branch into bronchioles ending in 300 million alveoli with 100 square metres of thin, vascular exchange surface. Pleural fluid lets lungs glide; surfactant keeps alveoli open. Every narrowing or blockage - asthma, mucus, water - directly cuts the oxygen supply line.
NEET Snapshot:
  • Path: nostril - pharynx - larynx - trachea - bronchi - bronchioles - alveoli.
  • Alveoli: thin squamous walls plus capillaries plus surfactant.
NEET Practice MCQ
1

Sound is produced in humans by the

Solution: Vocal cords in the larynx (sound box) vibrate; the pharynx only conducts air and food.
Back to Index
Index / Human Physiology
Chapter 4 · Topic 2

Lungs

Breathing and Exchange of GasesHuman PhysiologyStep-by-step Topic
Two lungs sit in the airtight thoracic chamber (ribs, sternum, diaphragm, dome muscles): the right has 3 lobes, the left 2 (space for the heart). Each lung is wrapped in double pleura with lubricating pleural fluid. Bronchi branch into bronchioles ending in alveoli - about 300 million per lung with ~100 sq m surface, walled by thin squamous cells over dense capillaries plus Amoebiasis-cleaning macrophages. Surfactant (lecithin) secreted by pneumocytes prevents alveolar collapse.

Related Diagram · Bronchial tree

Fig: One trachea, two bronchi, branching bronchioles - ending in 300 million alveoli.

Topic in Depth · Lungs

Right lung (3 lobes) is bigger than left (2 lobes, sharing space with the heart). Both sit in the airtight thoracic chamber sealed by ribs, sternum, diaphragm and intercostal muscles - airtightness is essential because breathing runs on pressure differences, and a puncture (pneumothorax) collapses the lung. Pulmonary arteries bring deoxygenated blood (rare oxygen-poor arteries) and pulmonary veins return oxygenated blood - the only reversed vessels in the body.
NEET Snapshot:
  • Right 3 lobes, left 2 lobes. Airtight thorax drives pressure breathing.
  • Pulmonary artery carries deoxygenated blood - the great vessel exception.
NEET Practice MCQ
1

The left human lung has

Solution: Two lobes leave room for the heart; the right lung has three.
