Index / Human Physiology
Chapter 4 · Topic 3

Mechanism of breathing

Breathing and Exchange of GasesHuman PhysiologyStep-by-step Topic
Inspiration (active): diaphragm contracts and flattens + external intercostal muscles lift ribs → thoracic volume rises → pulmonary pressure falls below atmospheric → air rushes in. Expiration (passive at rest): muscles relax, volume falls, air flows out; forced expiration uses abdominal and internal intercostal muscles. Breathing rate in a healthy adult ≈ 12–16 per minute.

Deeper Understanding · Muscles of breathing

Quiet inspiration is active (diaphragm + external intercostals) but quiet expiration is passive elastic recoil - no muscle work. Forced breathing recruits help: abdominal muscles and internal intercostals for forceful expiration (coughing, blowing), and neck/shoulder muscles for gasping inspiration. The diaphragm is driven by the phrenic nerve; hiccups are its involuntary spasms. Rate 12-16/min is set by the medullary rhythm centre and fine-tuned by the pontine pneumotaxic centre.

Topic in Depth · Mechanism of breathing

Breathing is applied Boyle law: bigger thoracic volume means lower pulmonary pressure, so air flows in; smaller volume pushes air out. Inspiration actively expands the thorax (diaphragm flattens, ribs lift); quiet expiration is passive elastic recoil; forced breathing recruits abdominal and neck muscles. Rate 12 to 16 per minute balances oxygen delivery against muscular work - exercise raises both depth and rate through CO2 feedback.
NEET Snapshot:
  • In = active expansion, pressure falls. Out (quiet) = passive recoil.
  • Diaphragm plus external intercostals power normal inspiration.
Remember
  • Air always flows down its pressure gradient - muscles change volume, physics moves air.
NEET Practice MCQ
1

Quiet expiration is brought about by

Solution: At rest, expiration needs no muscle - stretched lungs simply recoil and push air out.
Back to Index
Index / Human Physiology
Chapter 4 · Topic 4

Inspiration

Breathing and Exchange of GasesHuman PhysiologyStep-by-step Topic
Inspiration is active: the diaphragm contracts and flattens while external intercostal muscles lift the ribs up and out - thoracic volume rises in all three dimensions, pulmonary pressure drops ~2-3 mmHg below atmosphere, and air rushes in down the gradient. Deep (forced) inspiration additionally recruits neck muscles (sternocleidomastoid, scalenes). The whole process is driven by the phrenic nerve to the diaphragm - its irritation causes hiccups.

Related Diagram · Inspiration mechanics

Fig: Diaphragm down plus ribs up triples the volume gain - pressure falls, air rushes in.

Topic in Depth · Inspiration

Inspiration deepens in stages: diaphragm contraction (flattening down), external intercostal lifting (ribs up and out), and in forced breaths neck-muscle assistance (sternocleidomastoid, scalenes). Each stage adds volume in a different dimension - vertical, lateral, then accessory. The phrenic nerve drives the diaphragm; its irritation causes hiccups. Deeper inspiration recruits more alveoli, improving oxygen uptake per breath during exercise.
NEET Snapshot:
  • Diaphragm down + ribs up/out = three-dimensional expansion.
  • Phrenic nerve drives the diaphragm; forced inspiration adds neck muscles.
NEET Practice MCQ
1

During inspiration, the diaphragm

Solution: Contracted diaphragm flattens downward, expanding thoracic volume and dropping pressure.
Back to Index
Index / Human Physiology
Chapter 4 · Topic 5

Expiration

Breathing and Exchange of GasesHuman PhysiologyStep-by-step Topic
Quiet expiration is passive: muscles relax, elastic lungs recoil, thoracic volume falls, pressure exceeds atmosphere and air flows out - zero energy spent. Forced expiration (blowing, coughing, sneezing) recruits abdominal muscles plus internal intercostals to squeeze the thorax harder; cough adds a closed-glottis pressure build-up released explosively to clear mucus. This is why spirometry distinguishes quiet tidal breathing from forced vital-capacity manoeuvres.

Topic in Depth · Expiration

Expiration has two gears: quiet (passive recoil, zero energy) and forced (abdominal plus internal intercostal squeeze, used in blowing, coughing, sneezing). Cough adds drama - deep inspiration, closed glottis, explosive release - generating air blasts that clear mucus at near-sonic speeds. Sneezing routes the blast through the nose instead. Wheezing signals narrowed airways forcing air through; prolonged expiration characterises asthma attacks.
NEET Snapshot:
  • Quiet out: passive. Forced out: abdominal plus internal intercostals.
  • Cough = closed-glottis pressure blast for clearing airways.
NEET Practice MCQ
1

Forced expiration involves contraction of

Solution: Squeezing the abdomen and pulling ribs down forcefully expels air beyond passive recoil.
Back to Index
Index / Human Physiology
Chapter 4 · Topic 6

Lung volumes

Breathing and Exchange of GasesHuman PhysiologyStep-by-step Topic
VolumeValue (mL)
Tidal volume (TV)500
Inspiratory reserve (IRV)2500–3000
Expiratory reserve (ERV)1000–1100
Residual volume (RV)1100–1200
Vital capacity (TV + IRV + ERV)3400–4800
Total lung capacity≈ 5800

Deeper Understanding · Using the volume numbers

From the table, derive anything: Inspiratory Capacity = TV + IRV (~3500 mL), Expiratory Capacity = TV + ERV (~1600 mL), Functional Residual Capacity = ERV + RV (~2300 mL), Total Lung Capacity = VC + RV (~5800 mL). Residual volume can never be measured by spirometry - it is the air left after maximal exhalation, keeping alveoli open. In emphysema, destroyed alveolar walls trap air: RV rises while VC falls, so the chest becomes barrel-shaped.

Topic in Depth · Lung volumes

Four primary volumes never change meaning: tidal (500 mL, each quiet breath), inspiratory reserve (2500 to 3000, extra inhale), expiratory reserve (1000 to 1100, extra exhale), residual (1100 to 1200, never leaves). Residual volume is the unsung hero - keeping alveoli open between breaths and allowing continuous gas exchange even at exhalation pause. Spirometry measures the first three directly; residual volume needs gas-dilution tricks since it never exits.
NEET Snapshot:
  • TV 500, IRV 2500-3000, ERV 1000-1100, RV 1100-1200 mL.
  • RV keeps alveoli open and exchange continuous.
Volume jobs
PointDetail
TidalNormal breath size
IRVExtra inhalation reserve
ERVExtra exhalation reserve
RVPermanent alveolar air

Solution: About 1.1 to 1.2 litres stays back after maximal exhalation, keeping alveoli inflated.

Chapter 4 · Topic 7

Lung capacities

Breathing and Exchange of GasesHuman PhysiologyStep-by-step Topic
Capacities are sums of volumes: Vital Capacity = TV + IRV + ERV (3400-4800 mL, the spirometer maximum); Inspiratory Capacity = TV + IRV; Functional Residual Capacity = ERV + RV (air left after normal exhalation); Total Lung Capacity = VC + RV (~5800 mL). Only FRC and TLC include Residual Volume - VC never does. Emphysema raises RV (trapped air) while shrinking VC, the classic barrel chest.

Topic in Depth · Lung capacities

Capacities are sums with clinical meaning: vital capacity (TV+IRV+ERV, 3400 to 4800 mL - the fitness number athletes maximise), inspiratory capacity (TV+IRV), functional residual capacity (ERV+RV - resting lung air), total lung capacity (VC+RV, about 5800 mL). Only FRC and TLC include residual volume - vital capacity never does (favourite trap). Emphysema raises RV while shrinking VC; fibrosis shrinks everything including TLC.
NEET Snapshot:
  • VC = TV+IRV+ERV. TLC = VC+RV. FRC = ERV+RV.
  • VC excludes RV - the most-tested capacity distinction.
NEET Practice MCQ
1

Vital capacity equals

Solution: VC is the maximum exhalable air after maximal inhalation - residual volume stays behind.

Chapter 4 · Topic 8

Gas exchange

Breathing and Exchange of GasesHuman PhysiologyStep-by-step Topic
Exchange at alveoli and tissues occurs by diffusion down partial-pressure gradients across the thin basement-membrane barrier. O₂ travels mainly as oxyhaemoglobin (~97%); the sigmoid dissociation curve shifts right (unloading) with high CO₂, H⁺, temperature and 2,3-BPG — the Bohr effect. CO₂ travels as bicarbonates (~70%) via carbonic anhydrase in RBCs, carbaminohaemoglobin (~20–25%) and dissolved (~7%). The Haldane effect aids CO₂ loading in oxygen-poor tissues.

Why does the oxygen dissociation curve shift right in actively respiring muscle?

Answer: High CO₂ and H⁺ lower haemoglobin's O₂ affinity (Bohr effect), unloading oxygen exactly where metabolism is fastest.

Deeper Understanding · Chloride shift and 2,3-BPG

When CO2 enters RBCs, carbonic anhydrase makes H+ + bicarbonate; H+ is buffered by haemoglobin while bicarbonate exits in exchange for Cl- (chloride shift) to preserve charge balance - reversed in the lungs. 2,3-BPG (made in glycolysis) sits in haemoglobin central cavity and stabilises deoxy form: high-altitude dwellers and anaemic patients make more of it, shifting the curve right to unload extra O2. Foetal haemoglobin (HbF) binds 2,3-BPG weakly, so it pulls O2 across the placenta from maternal blood.

Related Diagram · Partial pressure gradients

Fig: Gases always diffuse down their own partial-pressure slopes - no pumping needed.

Topic in Depth · Gas exchange

Exchange needs only thinness and gradients: alveolar walls (squamous), capillary endothelium and fused basement membrane total under a micrometre, while partial-pressure differences (alveolar pO2 104 vs venous 40; tissue pCO2 45 vs arterial 40) push O2 in and CO2 out. Surface area (~100 sq m), thinness and ventilation-perfusion matching decide efficiency - pneumonia (fluid), emphysema (lost walls) and clots (no perfusion) each break one pillar.
NEET Snapshot:
  • Thin barrier + pressure gradients = diffusion exchange.
  • Alveolar pO2 104, pCO2 40 mmHg drive the flow.
Remember
  • Exchange fails by three routes: thick wall, lost area, or no blood flow.
NEET Practice MCQ
1

Gaseous exchange in alveoli occurs by

Solution: O2 and CO2 simply diffuse across the thin respiratory membrane down their gradients.

Chapter 4 · Topic 9

Oxygen transport

Breathing and Exchange of GasesHuman PhysiologyStep-by-step Topic
About 97% of oxygen rides as oxyhaemoglobin inside RBCs (only ~3% dissolves in plasma). Each haemoglobin binds 4 O2 cooperatively - the first oxygen eases the next three, producing the sigmoid dissociation curve. In respiring tissues, high CO2, H+, temperature and 2,3-BPG shift the curve right (Bohr effect), unloading O2 exactly where metabolism runs hottest. Myoglobin in muscles holds one O2 with even higher affinity as a local reserve.

Topic in Depth · Oxygen transport

Haemoglobin is a four-seat cooperative carrier: each bound O2 eases the next (sigmoid curve), loading fully in lungs (pO2 104) and unloading where needed. The Bohr effect (high CO2, H+, temperature, 2,3-BPG shifts the curve right) targets delivery to respiring tissues automatically. Myoglobin holds one O2 tighter as muscle reserve; foetal haemoglobin out-pulls maternal across the placenta. About 97 percent rides haemoglobin; 3 percent dissolves in plasma.
NEET Snapshot:
  • 97 percent as oxyhaemoglobin; sigmoid cooperative binding.
  • Bohr shift unloads O2 in active tissues automatically.
NEET Practice MCQ
1

Bohr effect describes

Solution: Acidic, CO2-rich, warm tissues lower haemoglobin affinity, releasing O2 where needed most.

Index / Human Physiology
Chapter 4 · Topic 10

CO2 transport

Breathing and Exchange of GasesHuman PhysiologyStep-by-step Topic
CO2 travels three ways: ~70% as bicarbonates (carbonic anhydrase in RBCs makes H2CO3, splitting to H+ + HCO3-), ~20-25% as carbaminohaemoglobin, ~7% dissolved. The chloride shift swaps exiting bicarbonate for entering Cl- to balance charge. In the lungs the Haldane effect reverses everything: oxygenation kicks CO2 off haemoglobin for exhalation. This is why venous blood carries more bicarbonate than arterial blood.

Topic in Depth · CO2 transport

CO2 rides mainly as bicarbonate (~70 percent): carbonic anhydrase in RBCs converts CO2 plus water to carbonic acid, splitting into H+ (buffered by haemoglobin) and bicarbonate (exported to plasma). Chloride shift (Cl- entering as HCO3- exits) preserves charge balance. Carbaminohaemoglobin carries ~20 to 25 percent; dissolved CO2 about 7 percent. In lungs the Haldane effect reverses everything - oxygenation displaces CO2 for exhalation.
NEET Snapshot:
  • 70 percent bicarbonate, 20-25 percent carbamino, 7 percent dissolved.
  • Chloride shift balances charge; Haldane effect unloads CO2 in lungs.
NEET Practice MCQ
1

Most CO2 is transported in blood as

Solution: About 70 percent travels as plasma bicarbonate via RBC carbonic anhydrase chemistry.

Chapter 4 · Topic 11

Respiratory regulation

Breathing and Exchange of GasesHuman PhysiologyStep-by-step Topic
Breathing rhythm originates in the medulla (inspiratory/expiratory groups), smoothed by the pons pneumotaxic centre (limits inspiration depth). A medullary chemosensitive area tracks CSF pH set by blood CO2 - rising CO2 (not falling O2) is the main drive to breathe faster and deeper. Peripheral carotid/aortic bodies sense O2 only in emergencies (high altitude, suffocation). Voluntary override (holding breath) works only until CO2 forces automatic control back.

Topic in Depth · Respiratory regulation

Breathing rhythm is born in the medulla (inspiratory/expiratory groups), smoothed by the pontine pneumotaxic centre (limits depth), and driven by CO2 via the chemosensitive area (CSF pH) - rising CO2, not falling O2, is the main urge to breathe. Carotid and aortic bodies monitor O2 only in emergencies. Voluntary cortex control (holding breath, speaking, singing) overrides temporarily until CO2 forces automatic control back - which is why breath-holding has hard limits.
NEET Snapshot:
  • Medulla sets rhythm; pons smooths; CO2/pH drives rate.
  • O2 sensors matter only in emergencies like high altitude.
Remember
  • Hyperventilate (blow off CO2) and the urge to breathe fades - the CO2-drive proof.
NEET Practice MCQ
1

The main stimulus for increased breathing rate is

Solution: Medullary chemosensors track CO2-driven acidity; oxygen matters little except in crises.

Chapter 4 · Topic 12

Respiratory disorders

Breathing and Exchange of GasesHuman PhysiologyStep-by-step Topic
Asthma — bronchial inflammation with wheezing; emphysema — alveolar walls damaged (smoking), reducing surface area; occupational lung diseases — silicosis/asbestosis from dust (fibrosis). Regulation: rhythm centre in the medulla, pneumotaxic centre in the pons, chemosensitive area responds to CO₂/H⁺.
Common mistakes: vital capacity excludes residual volume — students often add RV into VC.

Deeper Understanding · Asthma vs emphysema vs fibrosis

Asthma is reversible bronchial inflammation (wheezing, antihistamine/bronchodilator relief); emphysema is irreversible alveolar destruction (smoking - surface area permanently lost); occupational fibrosis (silicosis, asbestosis) stiffens lungs with scar tissue after years of dust exposure. At high altitude, low atmospheric pO2 triggers deeper breathing within hours and extra RBC production (erythropoietin) within days - acclimatisation that athletes exploit by training high and competing low.

Topic in Depth · Respiratory disorders

Asthma (reversible bronchial inflammation - wheezing, bronchodilator relief), emphysema (irreversible alveolar destruction from smoking - lost surface, barrel chest), occupational fibrosis (silicosis, asbestosis - scar-stiffened lungs from years of dust) form the classic trio: one reversible, one destructive, one scarring. Pneumonia and TB are infections, not these structural diseases. High-altitude acclimatisation (deeper breathing, then erythropoietin-driven extra RBCs) shows healthy lungs adapting the same variables diseases break.
NEET Snapshot:
  • Asthma: reversible spasm. Emphysema: destroyed walls. Fibrosis: scarred stiffness.
  • Smoking causes emphysema; dust causes fibrosis - match cause to disease.
NEET Practice MCQ
1

Emphysema results mainly from

Solution: Destroyed alveolar septa cut surface area permanently and trap air (raised RV).

Chapter 5 · Step-by-step

Body Fluids and Circulation

Blood cells, groups, clotting, heart, ECG and double circulation.

Body Fluids and CirculationHuman PhysiologyChapter Introduction

Subtopics in this Chapter

1. Blood2. Plasma3. RBC4. WBC5. Platelets6. Blood groups7. Rh factor8. Blood clotting9. Lymph10. Heart11. Cardiac cycle12. ECG13. Cardiac output14. Blood pressure15. Double circulation16. Circulatory disorders

Deep Dive · Blood groups and clotting cascade

ABO logic: antigens sit on RBCs, antibodies float in plasma - and a person never carries an antibody against their own antigen. Group A has antigen A + antibody b; B has antigen B + antibody a; AB has both antigens + no antibodies (universal recipient); O has no antigens + both antibodies (universal donor as packed cells). Rh+ blood can receive Rh- but Rh- should receive Rh- only. In erythroblastosis foetalis, an Rh- mother carrying an Rh+ baby makes anti-Rh antibodies during the first delivery; these cross the placenta in the second Rh+ pregnancy and destroy foetal RBCs - prevented by anti-Rh (RhoGAM) injection right after the first birth.
Clotting cascade in order
Injury exposes tissue thrombokinase (thromboplastin) - with Ca2+ ions it converts inactive prothrombin to thrombin; thrombin converts soluble fibrinogen to fibrin threads that trap blood cells into a clot. Vitamin K is needed to synthesise prothrombin and clotting factors in the liver - which is why newborns (sterile gut, no bacterial vitamin K yet) receive a vitamin K shot. Serum is simply plasma minus fibrinogen and clotting factors.
Fig: Double circulation - right heart pumps to lungs (pulmonary loop), left heart pumps to the body (systemic loop). Blue = deoxygenated, red = oxygenated.
Numbers to tattoo in memory
  • RBC life 120 days, destroyed in spleen; WBC differential: neutrophils 60-65, lymphocytes 20-25, monocytes 6-8, eosinophils 2-3, basophils 0.5-1.
  • Cardiac cycle 0.8 s; stroke volume 70 mL; cardiac output about 5000 mL/min; BP 120/80 mmHg; ECG: P = atrial depolarisation, QRS = ventricular depolarisation, T = ventricular repolarisation.
NEET Practice MCQ
1

A person with O- blood can safely donate red cells to

Solution: O- red cells carry no A, B or Rh antigens, so no recipient antibody can attack them - universal donor. (For plasma the rule reverses.)

Related Diagram · Reading an ECG

Fig: One heartbeat on paper - small P, spike QRS, dome T. Enlarged QRS means thickened ventricles.

Chapter 5 · Topic 1

Blood

Body Fluids and CirculationHuman PhysiologyStep-by-step Topic
Plasma (55% of blood): water, proteins (fibrinogen, albumins, globulins), minerals, glucose, hormones. RBCs: biconcave, no nucleus in mammals (except camel/llama), haemoglobin-rich, lifespan 120 days, destroyed in the spleen ("graveyard of RBCs"). WBCs: neutrophils 60–65% (phagocytic), lymphocytes 20–25% (immunity), monocytes 6–8% (largest, phagocytic), eosinophils 2–3% (allergy, parasites), basophils 0.5–1% (histamine). Platelets release thrombokinase → prothrombin → thrombin → fibrinogen → fibrin clot; Ca²⁺ and vitamin K are essential.
NEET facts:
  • Rise in WBC count = leukaemia/leucocytosis; fall in platelets = thrombocytopenia.
  • Serum is plasma without clotting factors.

Deeper Understanding · Erythropoiesis and WBC roles

RBCs are manufactured in red bone marrow under the hormone erythropoietin, released by kidneys when oxygen runs low - which is why kidney failure causes anaemia and why athletes misuse EPO. Each WBC has a signature job: neutrophils are first-responder phagocytes (pus is dead neutrophils), lymphocytes build immunity, monocytes become giant tissue macrophages, eosinophils attack parasites and mediate allergy, basophils release histamine and heparin. A differential count therefore diagnoses: high neutrophils suggest bacterial infection, high eosinophils suggest allergy or worms.

Topic in Depth · Blood

Blood is a fluid tissue with three jobs: transport (O2, nutrients, hormones, wastes), regulation (pH, temperature, water balance) and protection (clotting, immunity). Plasma (55 percent) carries dissolved cargo; formed elements (45 percent) do the specialised work - RBCs haul gases, WBCs fight, platelets plug leaks. About 5 litres circulate, making a full round roughly every minute at rest. pH holds at 7.4 via bicarbonate buffering - small drifts are emergencies.
NEET Snapshot:
  • 55 percent plasma, 45 percent cells. About 5 litres, pH 7.4.
  • Transport plus regulation plus protection = blood triple role.
NEET Practice MCQ
1

pH of human blood is about

Solution: Slightly alkaline 7.4, held by bicarbonate and protein buffers.

Index / Human Physiology
Chapter 5 · Topic 2

Plasma

Body Fluids and CirculationHuman PhysiologyStep-by-step Topic
Plasma (55% of blood) is 90-92% water carrying proteins - fibrinogen (clotting), albumins (osmotic balance), globulins (antibodies) - plus glucose, amino acids, lipids, hormones, gases, salts and wastes. It distributes heat, buffers pH with bicarbonates, and transports everything cells need or discard. Serum is plasma minus fibrinogen and clotting factors - the fluid left after blood clots in a test tube.

Topic in Depth · Plasma

Plasma is 90 to 92 percent water plus the working dissolved fraction: fibrinogen (clotting), albumins (osmotic pressure holding fluid in vessels), globulins (antibodies plus carriers), plus glucose, amino acids, lipids, hormones, gases, salts and urea. It distributes heat evenly (no hot organs), buffers pH, and delivers everything while collecting wastes. Serum is simply plasma minus clotting factors - the fluid left after a clot forms in the tube.
NEET Snapshot:
  • 90-92 percent water; proteins: fibrinogen, albumin, globulins.
  • Serum = plasma minus clotting factors.
Remember
  • Albumin loss (kidney disease, starvation) causes oedema - fluid leaks out.
NEET Practice MCQ
1

Serum differs from plasma in lacking

Solution: Clotting consumes fibrinogen, so leftover serum cannot clot again.

Chapter 5 · Topic 3

RBC

Body Fluids and CirculationHuman PhysiologyStep-by-step Topic
Human RBCs are biconcave discs without nucleus or mitochondria (mammalian maturity loses both - except camel/llama) - maximum haemoglobin space plus flexibility to squeeze through capillaries. Count ~5 million/mm3, lifespan 120 days, destroyed in the spleen (RBC graveyard). Made in red marrow under erythropoietin from kidneys. Haemoglobin (4 haem + globin) carries 4 O2; deficiency causes anaemia (iron, B12 or folate lack).

Topic in Depth · RBC

Red cells are optimisation marvels: biconcave discs (maximum surface, squeezable through capillaries), no nucleus or mitochondria at maturity (mammals - except camel/llama), packed with 270 million haemoglobin molecules each. Count ~5 million per cubic mm, lifespan 120 days, recycled in the spleen; production in red marrow driven by kidney erythropoietin when oxygen runs low. Iron, B12 or folate lack each causes its own anaemia type.
NEET Snapshot:
  • Biconcave, anucleate, 120-day life, spleen-recycled.
  • Erythropoietin from kidneys drives marrow production.
Anaemia types
PointDetail
Iron lackSmall pale RBCs
B12/folate lackLarge immature RBCs
Kidney failureLow erythropoietin output
NEET Practice MCQ
1

RBCs are destroyed mainly in the

Solution: The spleen (RBC graveyard) removes 120-day-old cells; marrow makes replacements.

Chapter 5 · Topic 4

WBC

Body Fluids and CirculationHuman PhysiologyStep-by-step Topic
WBCs (~6000-8000/mm3) are nucleated soldiers in fixed proportions - the DLC: neutrophils 60-65% (phagocytic first responders; pus is their corpses), lymphocytes 20-25% (B-cells make antibodies, T-cells kill), monocytes 6-8% (largest, become tissue macrophages), eosinophils 2-3% (parasites, allergy), basophils 0.5-1% (histamine, heparin). Rising total count (leucocytosis) signals infection; leukaemia is their cancer - the classic DLC-shift diagnosis question.

Topic in Depth · WBC

White cells are the mobile army in fixed proportions (DLC): neutrophils 60-65 (phagocytic first responders - pus is their corpses), lymphocytes 20-25 (B-cells make antibodies, T-cells kill), monocytes 6-8 (largest, become tissue macrophages), eosinophils 2-3 (parasites, allergy), basophils 0.5-1 (histamine, heparin). Total ~6000-8000 per cubic mm; shifts diagnose - neutrophils up means bacterial infection, eosinophils up means allergy or worms, blasts mean leukaemia.
NEET Snapshot:
  • DLC order: Neutrophils, Lymphocytes, Monocytes, Eosinophils, Basophils.
  • Pus = dead neutrophils. Leukaemia = WBC cancer.
NEET Practice MCQ
1

The most abundant WBCs are

Solution: Neutrophils form 60-65 percent - the phagocytic front line of innate defence.

Index / Human Physiology
Chapter 5 · Topic 5

Platelets

Body Fluids and CirculationHuman PhysiologyStep-by-step Topic
Platelets (1.5-3.5 lakh/mm3) are cell fragments from megakaryocytes that plug leaks: they stick to exposed collagen, release clotting factors (thrombokinase), and contract the clot. Low counts (thrombocytopenia, as in dengue) cause bleeding spots and slow clotting. Aspirin irreversibly blocks platelet aggregation - small daily doses prevent heart-attack clots but raise bleeding risk, the exact trade-off asked in reasoning questions.

Topic in Depth · Platelets

Platelets are megakaryocyte fragments (1.5 to 3.5 lakh per cubic mm) that patrol for leaks: exposed collagen triggers sticking, shape change and chemical alarms (ADP, thromboxane), piling a temporary plug while the fibrin cascade reinforces it. Low counts (thrombocytopenia - dengue classic) cause spots and bleeding; aspirin blocks aggregation to prevent heart-attack clots at the cost of bleeding risk. No nucleus, 8-10 day life, spleen-cleared like RBCs.
NEET Snapshot:
  • Plug leaks fast; fibrin reinforces slow - two-stage sealing.
  • Dengue drops platelets; aspirin blocks their clumping.
Remember
  • Platelet plug is temporary; fibrin mesh makes it permanent.
NEET Practice MCQ
1

Platelets are cell fragments of

Solution: Giant marrow megakaryocytes shed platelets into blood - no nucleus, short life.

Index / Human Physiology
Chapter 5 · Topic 6

Blood groups

Body Fluids and CirculationHuman PhysiologyStep-by-step Topic
ABO grouping (Landsteiner) depends on antigens A/B on RBCs and antibodies a/b in plasma; Rh factor (Rhesus monkey) decides positive/negative. Universal donor O⁻, universal recipient AB⁺. Erythroblastosis foetalis: Rh⁻ mother carrying Rh⁺ baby — second pregnancy risks antibody attack on foetal RBCs; prevented with anti-Rh (RhoGAM) injection. Coagulation cascade needs thrombokinase, Ca²⁺, prothrombin, fibrinogen and vitamin K.

A person with AB⁺ blood needs transfusion. Which groups can donate, and why is AB⁺ the universal recipient?

Answer: Anyone (no anti-A/anti-B antibodies, Rh⁺ accepts all) — AB⁺ plasma lacks a/b antibodies so no donor RBCs are agglutinated.

Deeper Understanding · Agglutination and cross-matching

Transfusion reactions are agglutination: recipient antibodies clump donor RBC antigens, blocking capillaries. Because anti-A and anti-B are naturally occurring IgM antibodies, even a first wrong transfusion reacts - unlike Rh, where the first Rh+ transfusion only sensitises and the second causes destruction (same logic as erythroblastosis foetalis). Blood is therefore cross-matched (donor cells + recipient serum mixed on a slide) before every transfusion despite ABO grouping. Platelet-rich plasma additionally needs calcium ions, which is why blood-bank bags contain citrate to chelate Ca2+ and prevent clotting in storage.

Related Diagram · ABO antigens vs antibodies

Fig: Antigens on cells decide the group; opposite antibodies in plasma decide compatibility.

Topic in Depth · Blood groups

ABO grouping is antigen-antibody logic: A carries antigen A plus antibody b; B carries B plus a; AB carries both antigens with no antibodies (universal recipient); O carries none with both antibodies (universal donor cells). A person never makes antibodies against their own antigens - mismatch agglutinates (clumps) donor cells and blocks capillaries. Cross-matching (donor cells plus recipient serum on a slide) double-checks every transfusion despite grouping.
NEET Snapshot:
  • Own antigen means no matching antibody - the compatibility rule.
  • O- donates to all; AB+ receives from all.
NEET Practice MCQ
1

A person with AB blood group can receive blood from

Solution: AB plasma has neither anti-A nor anti-B, so no donor cells get attacked - universal recipient.

Index / Human Physiology
Chapter 5 · Topic 7

Rh factor

Body Fluids and CirculationHuman PhysiologyStep-by-step Topic
Rh factor (Rhesus monkey discovery) is a separate antigen system: Rh+ blood carries it, Rh- lacks it, with no natural antibodies - first Rh+ transfusion into Rh- only sensitises, the second destroys. Universal donor is O-, universal recipient AB+. In erythroblastosis foetalis, an Rh- mother first sensitised at delivery attacks her second Rh+ baby - prevented by anti-Rh (RhoGAM) injection after the first birth.

Topic in Depth · Rh factor

Rh factor (Rhesus monkey discovery) adds plus/minus to ABO: Rh+ carries the D antigen, Rh- lacks it, and crucially nobody has natural anti-Rh antibodies - first Rh+ exposure only sensitises, the second destroys. This timing drives erythroblastosis foetalis: an Rh- mother sensitised at first Rh+ delivery attacks her second Rh+ baby - prevented by anti-Rh (RhoGAM) injection mopping up foetal cells before sensitisation. Transfusion follows the same second-exposure rule.
NEET Snapshot:
  • No natural anti-Rh - sensitisation first, reaction second.
  • RhoGAM after first birth protects the second baby.
NEET Practice MCQ
1

Erythroblastosis foetalis occurs when the mother is

Solution: Maternal anti-Rh from first delivery crosses the placenta in later Rh+ pregnancies.

Index / Human Physiology
Chapter 5 · Topic 8

Blood clotting

Body Fluids and CirculationHuman PhysiologyStep-by-step Topic
Clotting cascade: vessel injury exposes thrombokinase - with Ca2+ ions converts prothrombin to thrombin - thrombin converts soluble fibrinogen to fibrin threads that trap cells into a clot, later retracted by platelets. Vitamin K (gut bacteria + diet) builds prothrombin and factors in the liver - newborns get a vitamin K shot since their gut is sterile. Haemophilia (X-linked factor VIII/IX lack) breaks this chain from inside.

Related Diagram · Clotting cascade

Fig: Thrombokinase plus calcium converts prothrombin to thrombin, thrombin converts fibrinogen to fibrin.

Topic in Depth · Blood clotting

Clotting is a triggered cascade, not instant glue: injury exposes thrombokinase, which with Ca2+ ions converts prothrombin to thrombin, which converts soluble fibrinogen to fibrin threads that trap cells - then platelets contract the mesh. Vitamin K builds prothrombin and factors in the liver (newborns get a shot; gut bacteria supply adults). Haemophilia breaks the chain from inside (factor VIII/IX lack); citrate bags prevent it outside by chelating calcium.
NEET Snapshot:
  • Thrombokinase - Ca2+ - prothrombin - thrombin - fibrinogen - fibrin.
  • Vitamin K makes factors; calcium enables the cascade.
Cascade order
PointDetail
TriggerThrombokinase + Ca2+
Step 1Prothrombin to thrombin
Step 2Fibrinogen to fibrin mesh
FinishPlatelets retract the clot

1

Vitamin K is needed for synthesis of

Solution: Liver-made clotting factors need vitamin K - deficiency or warfarin overdose bleeds.

Index / Human Physiology
Chapter 5 · Topic 9

Lymph

Body Fluids and CirculationHuman PhysiologyStep-by-step Topic
Lymph is interstitial fluid drained back to blood: tissue fluid bathes cells, most re-enters capillaries, the rest enters blind lymph capillaries (more permeable than blood capillaries) and returns via lymphatics to the subclavian veins. Along the way lymph nodes filter pathogens (swollen glands in infection) and mature lymphocytes. Lacteals (intestinal lymph vessels) absorb fats as chylomicrons - the milky route that bypasses the liver first pass.

Topic in Depth · Lymph

Lymph is the drainage return: blood pressure filters fluid into tissues, most re-enters capillaries, and the remainder enters blind, extra-permeable lymph capillaries - flowing through node filters (which trap pathogens and mature lymphocytes) back to subclavian veins. Intestinal lacteals add fat cargo as chylomicrons, making post-meal lymph milky. Swollen nodes signal active filtering; blocked vessels (filariasis) balloon limbs with trapped fluid.
NEET Snapshot:
  • Tissue fluid - lymph capillaries - nodes - subclavian veins.
  • Lacteals carry fats; nodes filter germs.
Remember
  • Lymph moves on muscle squeezes plus valves - no central pump.
NEET Practice MCQ
1

Lymph differs from blood in lacking

Solution: Lymph carries lymphocytes in plasma-like fluid but no red cells or platelets.
Back to Index
Index / Human Physiology
Chapter 5 · Topic 10

Heart

Body Fluids and CirculationHuman PhysiologyStep-by-step Topic
Four chambers with tricuspid (right) and bicuspid/mitral (left) AV valves plus semilunar valves. Double circulation: pulmonary (right ventricle → lungs → left atrium) + systemic (left ventricle → body → right atrium); hepatic portal vein carries nutrients to the liver. Special tissues: SA node (pacemaker) → AV node → Bundle of His → Purkinje fibres. Lymph drains interstitial fluid back to blood and absorbs fats through lacteals.
Fig: Human heart — right side (RA/RV) pumps deoxygenated blood to lungs; left side (LA/LV) pumps oxygenated blood to the body. LV wall is thickest.
Common mistakes: the left ventricle wall is thickest (pumps to the whole body); coronary circulation nourishes the heart wall itself.

Deeper Understanding · Valves, sounds and portal systems

Valve names encode position: tricuspid (three flaps, right AV), bicuspid/mitral (two flaps, left AV, shaped like a bishop mitre), plus two semilunar valves (aortic, pulmonary). LUBB marks AV closure at ventricular systole start; DUP marks semilunar closure at diastole start - murmurs are faulty valves leaking sound. A portal system is any vein breaking into capillaries twice: hepatic portal (gut to liver, nutrients processed first), renal portal (frog/fish), and hypophyseal portal (hypothalamus to pituitary, releasing factors delivered directly). Lymph finally rejoins blood at the subclavian veins.

Topic in Depth · Heart

The heart is two side-by-side double pumps in one muscle wall: right atrium-ventricle push to lungs (tricuspid valve), left push to the body (bicuspid/mitral valve), with semilunar valves guarding artery exits. The left ventricle wall runs thickest (whole-body pressure duty); coronary vessels feed the wall itself. SA node (pacemaker) fires, AV node delays 0.1 s for atrial emptying, then Bundle of His plus Purkinje fibres flash the ventricles - wiring that makes 100,000 coordinated beats daily.
NEET Snapshot:
  • Right = lungs. Left = body. Left ventricle thickest wall.
  • SA node fires; AV node delays; His-Purkinje distribute.
NEET Practice MCQ
1

The thickest heart wall belongs to the

Solution: Left ventricle generates whole-body systemic pressure - muscle matches workload.
Back to Index
Index / Human Physiology
Chapter 5 · Topic 11

Cardiac cycle

Body Fluids and CirculationHuman PhysiologyStep-by-step Topic
One cycle lasts 0.8 s: atrial systole 0.1 s (ventricles fill), ventricular systole 0.3 s (AV valves shut - LUBB; blood ejects through semilunar valves), joint diastole 0.4 s (all chambers relax; semilunar shut - DUP). SA node fires ~72/min; each beat ejects stroke volume ~70 mL. The 0.1 s AV-nodal delay is essential - ventricles must finish filling before they contract.

Topic in Depth · Cardiac cycle

Each 0.8-second cycle stages filling then ejection: atrial systole (0.1 s) tops up ventricles, ventricular systole (0.3 s) slams AV valves shut (LUBB) and ejects through semilunar valves, joint diastole (0.4 s) relaxes everything as semilunar valves click shut (DUP). Stroke volume (~70 mL) times rate (~72) gives ~5 litres per minute - the entire blood volume each minute. Exercise shortens mainly diastole, trading filling time for speed.
NEET Snapshot:
  • 0.8 s = 0.1 atrial + 0.3 ventricular + 0.4 joint diastole.
  • LUBB = AV shut. DUP = semilunar shut.
NEET Practice MCQ
1

LUBB sound is produced by closure of

Solution: Ventricular systole snaps AV valves shut first (LUBB); DUP follows at diastole onset.
Back to Index
Index / Human Physiology
Chapter 5 · Topic 12

ECG

Body Fluids and CirculationHuman PhysiologyStep-by-step Topic
ECG waves: P = atrial depolarisation, QRS complex = ventricular depolarisation (large spike, masks atrial repolarisation), T = ventricular repolarisation. Intervals diagnose: long P-R means AV block, elevated S-T means infarction in progress, enlarged QRS means ventricular hypertrophy. Einthoven standard limb leads record this electrical story - the heart speaks volts before millilitres.

Topic in Depth · ECG

ECG traces the heart electrical story: P wave (atrial depolarisation), QRS spike (ventricular depolarisation, hiding atrial recovery), T dome (ventricular repolarisation). Intervals diagnose precisely - long P-R means AV block, lifted S-T means infarction in progress, tall QRS means thickened ventricles. Einthoven limb leads standardise the recording; the trace detects electrical faults long before pump failure shows.
NEET Snapshot:
  • P = atria fire. QRS = ventricles fire. T = ventricles recover.
  • S-T lift signals heart attack in progress.
Remember
  • Atrial repolarisation hides inside QRS - never look for a separate wave.
NEET Practice MCQ
1

QRS complex in ECG represents

Solution: The sharp spike is massive ventricular muscle depolarising at once.

Index / Human Physiology
Chapter 5 · Topic 13

Cardiac output

Body Fluids and CirculationHuman PhysiologyStep-by-step Topic
Cardiac output = heart rate x stroke volume = 72 x 70 mL = ~5040 mL/min (about 5 litres - the entire blood volume each minute). Exercise raises it 4-5 fold, mostly by heart rate (up to ~180) plus stronger ejection. Athletes achieve the same resting output with fewer, bigger beats (resting bradycardia ~50/min). Cardiac index divides output by body surface for fair comparison across body sizes.

Topic in Depth · Cardiac output

Cardiac output (rate times stroke volume, ~5 L/min at rest) is the circulation master number - brain, kidneys and muscles all negotiate shares of it. Exercise multiplies it 4 to 5 times via faster rate plus stronger ejection; athletes reach resting output with fewer, bigger beats (bradycardia ~50/min). Cardiac index (output per body surface) compares fairly across sizes; falling output with rising heart rate warns of failing stroke volume.
NEET Snapshot:
  • Output = 72 beats x 70 mL = ~5 litres per minute.
  • Exercise raises rate first, stroke volume second.
NEET Practice MCQ
1

If heart rate is 72/min and stroke volume 70 mL, cardiac output is

Solution: 72 x 70 = 5040 mL/min - roughly the whole blood volume each minute.

Chapter 5 · Topic 14

Blood pressure

Body Fluids and CirculationHuman PhysiologyStep-by-step Topic
Blood pressure 120/80 mmHg means systolic (ventricular ejection peak) over diastolic (relaxation floor), measured by sphygmomanometer at the brachial artery with Korotkoff sounds. Pressure falls along the tree: aorta ~100 mean, capillaries ~30 (exchange zone), veins ~10 - valves and muscle pumps return blood uphill. Persistent 140/90+ is hypertension, straining heart, brain, kidneys and eyes silently for years.

Topic in Depth · Blood pressure

Blood pressure reads ejection over relaxation: systolic ~120 (ventricular peak) over diastolic ~80 (arterial recoil floor), measured by sphygmomanometer listening for Korotkoff sounds at the brachial artery. Pressure steps down the tree - aorta ~100 mean, capillaries ~30 (exchange zone), veins ~10 (valves plus muscle pumps push uphill). Persistent 140/90+ defines hypertension, silently straining heart, brain, kidneys and eyes for years.
NEET Snapshot:
  • 120/80 normal; 140/90+ hypertension.
  • Capillary mid-pressure enables two-way exchange.
NEET Practice MCQ
1

Diastolic pressure records pressure during

Solution: Relaxed-ventricle arterial recoil sets the diastolic floor (~80 mmHg).

Chapter 5 · Topic 15

Double circulation

Body Fluids and CirculationHuman PhysiologyStep-by-step Topic
Double circulation sends blood through the heart twice per loop: pulmonary circuit (right ventricle to lungs to left atrium - picks up O2) plus systemic circuit (left ventricle to body to right atrium - delivers O2). Special detours: hepatic portal vein (gut nutrients processed by liver first), coronary circulation (heart feeding itself), and foetal shunts (foramen ovale, ductus arteriosus) that close at birth.

Topic in Depth · Double circulation

Double circulation routes blood through the heart twice per loop: pulmonary circuit (right ventricle - lungs - left atrium) oxygenates, systemic circuit (left ventricle - body - right atrium) delivers - fully separated streams in birds and mammals (no mixing, maximum efficiency). Detours serve special organs: hepatic portal (gut to liver processing first), coronary (heart feeding itself), renal (kidney filtering), and foetal shunts (foramen ovale, ductus arteriosus) that close at first breath.
NEET Snapshot:
  • Two loops, one pump each side, zero mixing in mammals.
  • Hepatic portal processes nutrients before general release.
Remember
  • Double circulation plus 4 chambers equals warm-blooded energy budgets.
NEET Practice MCQ
1

Hepatic portal vein carries blood from

Solution: Gut nutrients detour through the liver for processing before joining general circulation.

Chapter 5 · Topic 16

Circulatory disorders

Body Fluids and CirculationHuman PhysiologyStep-by-step Topic
Hypertension (140/90+, silent vessel damage), atherosclerosis/CAD (cholesterol plaques narrow coronaries), angina (O2-shortage chest pain on exertion, relieved by rest/nitroglycerin), heart failure (weak pump, congestion - not sudden death), cardiac arrest (stopped heart, CPR window), myocardial infarction (blocked artery kills muscle - S-T elevation, troponin leak). Lifestyle triad - diet, exercise, no smoking - prevents most of these.

Topic in Depth · Circulatory disorders

Circulatory failures split by mechanism: hypertension (140/90+, silent vessel strain), atherosclerosis/CAD (cholesterol plaques narrow coronaries), angina (exertion chest pain from O2 shortage, rest-relieved), heart failure (weak pump with congestion - not sudden death), cardiac arrest (stopped pump - CPR window), myocardial infarction (blocked artery kills muscle - S-T lift plus troponin leak). Diet, exercise and no smoking prevent most; stents, bypass and clot-busters rescue the rest.
NEET Snapshot:
  • Angina warns; infarction kills muscle; arrest stops everything.
  • Troponin leak plus S-T lift diagnoses heart attack.
NEET Practice MCQ
1

Angina pectoris is chest pain due to

Solution: Narrowed coronaries starve working heart muscle - rest restores balance until blockage completes.

Chapter 6 · Step-by-step

Excretory Products and Elimination

Nitrogenous wastes, nephron, urine formation, ADH and dialysis.

Excretory Products and EliminationHuman PhysiologyChapter Introduction

Subtopics in this Chapter

1. Nitrogenous wastes2. Kidney3. Nephron4. Ultrafiltration5. Reabsorption6. Secretion7. Counter-current mechanism8. ADH9. Osmoregulation10. Dialysis11. Kidney disorders

Deep Dive · Segment-wise urine formation

What each nephron segment does
SegmentProcessSubstances moved
Glomerulus + Bowman capsuleUltrafiltration (GFR ~125 mL/min, ~180 L/day)Water, ions, glucose, amino acids filtered; cells and plasma proteins stay back
PCTReabsorption (bulk, ~70%)Nearly all glucose, amino acids, 70% Na+ and water reclaimed
Descending limb of HenleWater exitsPermeable to water, not salt - filtrate concentrates
Ascending limb of HenleSalt exitsNaCl pumped out, impermeable to water - builds medullary gradient to 1200 mOsm/L

Counter-current multiplier: filtrate flowing down and up the loop in opposite directions, plus opposite blood flow in the vasa recta, multiplies a small transverse gradient into a huge corticomedullary gradient (300 to 1200 mOsm/L). The collecting duct passes through this salty medulla, so when ADH (vasopressin) inserts aquaporins, water rushes out and urine concentrates. No ADH (diabetes insipidus) means large volumes of dilute urine. RAAS defends blood pressure: low BP releases renin, generating angiotensin II that triggers aldosterone (Na+ and water reabsorption); ANF from stretched atria opposes RAAS.
Fig: The loop builds a salty medulla; the collecting duct uses it to concentrate urine only when ADH is present.

Chapter 6 · Topic 1

Nitrogenous wastes

Excretory Products and EliminationHuman PhysiologyStep-by-step Topic
Protein metabolism releases toxic ammonia; animals handle it three ways: ammonotelic (aquatic invertebrates, bony fishes - flush dilute ammonia directly), ureotelic (mammals, amphibians - liver converts ammonia to less-toxic urea via the ornithine cycle, kidneys excrete it), uricotelic (birds, reptiles, insects - nearly solid uric acid pellets saving water for eggs and flight). Mode follows habitat water, not ancestry - tadpoles are ammonotelic, adult frogs ureotelic.

Topic in Depth · Nitrogenous wastes

Protein breakdown releases ammonia - brutally toxic, so animals pay to convert or flush it: ammonotelic animals (aquatic invertebrates, bony fishes) flush dilute ammonia directly through gills and skin; ureotelic animals (mammals, amphibians) spend ATP converting it to safer urea via the liver ornithine cycle; uricotelic animals (birds, reptiles, insects) spend more making near-solid uric acid that needs almost no water. Mode follows habitat water, not ancestry - the tadpole-to-frog switch proves it.
NEET Snapshot:
  • Ammonia (toxic, water-rich) - urea (medium) - uric acid (safe, water-poor).
  • Liver makes urea; kidney excretes it. Birds save water for shelled eggs.
Excretion modes
PointDetail
AmmonotelicAquatic invertebrates, bony fishes
UreotelicMammals, amphibians, sharks
UricotelicBirds, reptiles, insects, land snails

1

Uricotelic animals excrete nitrogen mainly as

Solution: Birds, reptiles and insects conserve water by excreting semi-solid uric acid pellets.

Chapter 6 · Topic 2

Kidney

Excretory Products and EliminationHuman PhysiologyStep-by-step Topic
Bean-shaped, retroperitoneal kidneys (10-12 cm) filter blood continuously: outer cortex (glomeruli + convoluted tubules), inner medulla (pyramids + loops of Henle), funnel pelvis draining to ureter, bladder, urethra. Each kidney holds ~1 million nephrons plus vasa recta capillaries. Renal artery delivers ~1200 mL/min (20% of cardiac output) - the kidneys are small but receive the richest blood supply per gram after the heart itself.

Topic in Depth · Kidney

Each bean-shaped kidney packs a million nephrons into cortex (filtering glomeruli plus convoluted tubules) and medulla (pyramids of loops plus collecting ducts), draining through pelvis to ureter, bladder and urethra. Positioned retroperitoneally against the back wall with the right slightly lower (liver above), each receives ~600 mL/min - filtering the entire plasma volume dozens of times daily. Cortex reddish-granular (vessels), medulla striped (parallel tubules) - structure visible to the naked eye.
NEET Snapshot:
  • Cortex filters; medulla concentrates; pelvis collects.
  • One million nephrons per kidney; right kidney slightly lower.
NEET Practice MCQ
1

Bowman capsule is part of the

Solution: Glomerulus plus Bowman capsule form the Malpighian body where filtration begins.

Index / Human Physiology
Chapter 6 · Topic 3

Nephron

Excretory Products and EliminationHuman PhysiologyStep-by-step Topic
Bean-shaped kidneys (retroperitoneal) filter blood: cortex → medulla (pyramids) → pelvis → ureter → bladder → urethra. Each kidney has ~1 million nephrons. A nephron = Malpighian body (glomerulus + Bowman's capsule) → PCT → Loop of Henle (descending + ascending limbs) → DCT → collecting duct. Cortical nephrons (majority) vs juxtamedullary nephrons (long loops, concentration).
NEET facts:
  • Glomerulus is a tuft of capillaries formed by the afferent arteriole; efferent arteriole is narrower, building filtration pressure.
  • Vasa recta (peritubular capillaries around Henle's loop) runs the counter-current multiplier.

Deeper Understanding · Juxtaglomerular apparatus

Where the DCT passes between its own glomerular arterioles sits the JGA: macula densa cells (salt sensors) plus JG cells (renin secreting, modified smooth muscle). Falling filtrate NaCl or low BP triggers renin, launching RAAS - the kidney literally tastes its own filtrate to regulate body pressure. The afferent arteriole is wider than the efferent, and this bottleneck builds the high glomerular pressure (~60 mmHg) that forces ultrafiltration against osmotic pull. Podocytes wrap capillaries with slit diaphragms that form the final molecular sieve.

Related Diagram · Juxtaglomerular apparatus

Fig: Where the tubule touches its own glomerulus, salt sensors command renin release.

Topic in Depth · Nephron

The nephron is a segmented assembly line: Malpighian body (glomerulus plus Bowman capsule) filters, PCT reclaims bulk nutrients, loop of Henle builds the concentration gradient, DCT fine-tunes ions, collecting duct (shared by many nephrons) obeys ADH for final water control. Cortical nephrons (majority, short loops) do bulk filtering; juxtamedullary nephrons (long loops deep in medulla) build concentration power. Blood shadows every segment - glomerular capillaries, peritubular network, vasa recta loops.
NEET Snapshot:
  • Segments in order: glomerulus - PCT - Henle - DCT - collecting duct.
  • Cortical filters; juxtamedullary concentrates.
Remember
  • Trace any drop: capsule to PCT to loop to DCT to duct to pelvis.
NEET Practice MCQ
1

Henle loop is found in the

Solution: Glomeruli sit in cortex while loops dip into medulla - the arrangement enabling gradients.
Back to Index
Index / Human Physiology
Chapter 6 · Topic 4

Ultrafiltration

Excretory Products and EliminationHuman PhysiologyStep-by-step Topic
Ultrafiltration: ~180 L/day filtered at GFR ≈ 125 mL/min; cells and plasma proteins stay back. Reabsorption (PCT): nearly all glucose, amino acids, 70% Na⁺ and water reclaimed. Loop of Henle: descending limb passes water out; ascending limb pumps NaCl out (impermeable to water) — building medullary osmolarity up to 1200 mOsm/L. Secretion (DCT + collecting duct): K⁺, H⁺, ammonia, drugs and creatinine dumped in. Final urine ≈ 1.5 L/day.
Fig: Nephron — filtration in the Malpighian body, reabsorption in PCT, concentration by the Loop of Henle, fine-tuning in DCT and collecting duct.

Glucose appears in urine although GFR is normal. Which nephron process has failed?

Answer: Selective reabsorption in the PCT — the glucose transport maximum is exceeded (as in diabetes mellitus) or PCT function is impaired.

Deeper Understanding · Transport maximum and threshold

PCT reabsorption is carrier-mediated, so it saturates: glucose transport maximum (TmG ~375 mg/min) is exceeded when blood sugar crosses the renal threshold (~180 mg/dL), spilling glucose into urine (glycosuria) and dragging water with it (osmotic diuresis - why diabetics urinate frequently). Amino acids, phosphate and sulphate each have their own maxima. Creatinine is filtered but neither reabsorbed nor secreted significantly, making creatinine clearance the clinical measure of GFR.

Related Diagram · Filtration barrier layers

Fig: Blood crosses endothelium, basement membrane and podocyte slits - cells and proteins stay back.

Topic in Depth · Ultrafiltration

Ultrafiltration is pressure-driven sieving: wide afferent plus narrow efferent arteriole builds ~60 mmHg glomerular pressure, forcing water and small solutes through three filters (fenestrated endothelium, basement membrane, podocyte slits) while cells and plasma proteins stay back. GFR (~125 mL/min, ~180 L/day) is autoregulated - myogenic constriction plus tubuloglomerular feedback via the JGA hold it steady across blood-pressure swings. Net filtration survives only because hydrostatic pressure beats osmotic pull.
NEET Snapshot:
  • GFR 125 mL/min; 180 litres filtered daily; 1.5 litres excreted.
  • Three barriers: endothelium, basement membrane, podocyte slits.
NEET Practice MCQ
1

The effective filtration pressure in glomeruli results mainly from

Solution: Wide-inlet, narrow-outlet arterioles pressurise the tuft to force filtration.
Back to Index
Index / Human Physiology
Chapter 6 · Topic 5

Reabsorption

Excretory Products and EliminationHuman PhysiologyStep-by-step Topic
Reabsorption (mostly PCT) reclaims nearly all glucose and amino acids plus ~70% of Na+ and water via active transport, cotransport and osmosis - the filtrate shrinks from 180 L/day toward 1.5 L. Each substance has a transport maximum (TmG ~375 mg/min for glucose; blood threshold ~180 mg/dL) beyond which it spills into urine. The descending Henle limb passes water out; the ascending limb pumps NaCl out but blocks water - together building the medullary gradient.

Topic in Depth · Reabsorption

Reabsorption reclaims what filtration spilled: PCT actively recovers nearly all glucose and amino acids plus ~70 percent of sodium and water; descending Henle limb leaks water out; DCT plus collecting duct fine-tune sodium, potassium and pH under aldosterone. Each carrier saturates at a transport maximum - glucose TmG ~375 mg/min, blood threshold ~180 mg/dL - beyond which valuable cargo spills (diabetic glycosuria). Reabsorption is recovery; secretion is disposal; together they compose urine deliberately.
NEET Snapshot:
  • PCT reclaims bulk; DCT fine-tunes; thresholds cap each substance.
  • Glucose fully reabsorbed until blood crosses ~180 mg/dL.
Segment reclaim
PointDetail
PCTGlucose, amino acids, 70 percent Na+ and water
Henle descendingWater out
Henle ascendingNaCl out, water blocked
DCT/collecting ductNa+, K+, H+ tuning
NEET Practice MCQ
1

Glucose is reabsorbed from filtrate mainly in the

Solution: PCT carriers reclaim all filtered glucose until the transport maximum saturates.

Index / Human Physiology
Chapter 6 · Topic 6

Secretion

Excretory Products and EliminationHuman PhysiologyStep-by-step Topic
Tubular secretion (DCT + collecting duct) dumps what filtration missed: K+, H+, ammonia, creatinine, drugs and toxins move from blood into filtrate. This fine-tunes pH (H+ secreted in acidosis) and potassium (aldosterone boosts K+ secretion while saving Na+). Unlike reabsorption (recovery), secretion is disposal - together they make urine composition independent of the fixed filter, the kidney real intelligence.

Topic in Depth · Secretion

Tubular secretion dumps what filtration missed or what balance demands: potassium, hydrogen ions, ammonia, creatinine, drugs, pigments and toxins move from peritubular blood into DCT and collecting-duct fluid. It fine-tunes pH (extra H+ secreted in acidosis, bicarbonate reclaimed) and potassium (aldosterone boosts K+ secretion while saving Na+). Unlike filtration (fixed sieve) and reabsorption (recovery), secretion is active disposal - the kidney intelligence that customises urine.
NEET Snapshot:
  • Secreted: K+, H+, ammonia, creatinine, drugs.
  • Secretion disposes; reabsorption recovers - opposite directions, one goal.
Remember
  • Acidosis raises H+ secretion; aldosterone raises K+ secretion.
NEET Practice MCQ
1

Which ion secretion rises under aldosterone action?

Solution: Aldosterone saves sodium (and water with it) while dumping potassium into urine.
Back to Index
Index / Human Physiology
Chapter 6 · Topic 7

Counter-current mechanism

Excretory Products and EliminationHuman PhysiologyStep-by-step Topic
Opposite flows in Henle's limbs and vasa recta multiply the medullary gradient, letting the collecting duct (under ADH/vasopressin) reabsorb water and excrete concentrated urine. Hormonal control: ADH (water reabsorption — diabetes insipidus when lacking), RAAS (renin → angiotensin II → aldosterone: Na⁺ and water retained, BP rises), ANF opposes RAAS. Other excretory modes: ammonotelic (aquatic invertebrates), ureotelic (mammals), uricotelic (birds, reptiles, insects — water-saving pellets).
Common mistakes: ADH acts on the collecting duct and DCT, not the glomerulus; aldosterone saves Na⁺ (water follows).

Deeper Understanding · Desert kidneys prove the gradient

Kangaroo rats (desert rodents) survive without drinking because their extra-long Henle loops build extreme medullary gradients and excrete near-solid urine - loop length directly predicts concentrating power across species. Seawater-drinking marine birds and reptiles excrete excess salt through nasal salt glands instead. In humans, ADH secretion follows blood osmolarity minute-by-minute (osmoreceptors in hypothalamus): a salty meal raises ADH within minutes, shrinking urine output - the everyday demonstration of the counter-current system.

Topic in Depth · Counter-current mechanism

Counter-current multiplication turns a small sideways difference into a huge lengthwise gradient: descending Henle limb leaks water (filtrate concentrates), ascending limb pumps salt but blocks water, and opposite-flowing vasa recta carry salt away without washing the gradient out - multiplying medullary osmolarity from 300 to 1200 mOsm/L. The collecting duct crossing this salty zone then extracts water precisely when ADH opens aquaporins. Longer loops (desert rodents) mean stronger gradients and drier urine.
NEET Snapshot:
  • Opposite flows multiply small differences into big gradients.
  • Vasa recta preserves the gradient while supplying blood.
NEET Practice MCQ
1

The counter-current mechanism operates between

Solution: Opposite flows in loop limbs plus vasa recta build the corticomedullary salt gradient.
Back to Index
Index / Human Physiology
Chapter 6 · Topic 8

ADH

Excretory Products and EliminationHuman PhysiologyStep-by-step Topic
ADH (vasopressin) from the posterior pituitary inserts aquaporin water channels into collecting-duct walls: high ADH (dehydration, salty meal, blood loss) reabsorbs water and concentrates urine; low ADH dilutes it massively. Lack causes diabetes insipidus (huge dilute output, unquenchable thirst) - unrelated to sugar diabetes except the shared symptom of polyuria. Alcohol suppresses ADH, explaining beer frequent urination.

Topic in Depth · ADH

ADH (vasopressin) is the water tap: hypothalamus osmoreceptors sense salty blood, posterior pituitary releases ADH, collecting-duct cells insert aquaporin channels, water rushes out into the salty medulla, urine concentrates. Dehydration, blood loss and salty meals raise ADH; alcohol suppresses it (beer diuresis); absence causes diabetes insipidus (huge dilute output with unquenchable thirst). ADH acts in minutes - the fastest kidney control, versus aldosterone slower salt route.
NEET Snapshot:
  • ADH inserts aquaporins; water follows the medullary gradient.
  • No ADH means diabetes insipidus - dilute floods, unrelated to sugar.
NEET Practice MCQ
1

ADH acts mainly on the

Solution: Aquaporin insertion in late tubules decides final urine concentration.
Back to Index
Index / Human Physiology
Chapter 6 · Topic 9

Osmoregulation

Excretory Products and EliminationHuman PhysiologyStep-by-step Topic
Osmoregulation balances water and salts: freshwater fishes (saltier inside) never drink and excrete dilute urine; marine fishes (less salty inside) drink seawater and excrete salt via gills. Hormonal control layers: ADH (water), aldosterone/RAAS (Na+ + water, raises BP), ANF (opposes RAAS when atria stretch). Together they hold blood osmolarity near 300 mOsm/L despite wildly varying intake.

Topic in Depth · Osmoregulation

Osmoregulation holds body fluids near 300 mOsm/L against intake swings: freshwater fishes (saltier inside) never drink, excrete dilute urine and pump salt in through gills; marine fishes (less salty inside) drink seawater, excrete salt through gills and pass scanty urine. Hormones layer control - ADH (water), RAAS/aldosterone (sodium plus water, raises BP), ANF (opposes RAAS when atria stretch). Kidneys, gills, salt glands and behaviour each cover different habitats.
NEET Snapshot:
  • Freshwater: dilute urine, salt uptake. Marine: drink water, excrete salt.
  • ADH plus RAAS plus ANF triangulate blood volume and salt.
Remember
  • Match animal to water problem: direction of salt/water threat decides strategy.
NEET Practice MCQ
1

Marine bony fishes conserve water by

Solution: They drink the sea, dump salt through gills, and pass minimal urine - opposite of freshwater fish.

Index / Human Physiology
Chapter 6 · Topic 10

Dialysis

Excretory Products and EliminationHuman PhysiologyStep-by-step Topic
Haemodialysis replaces failed kidneys: blood flows past a cellophane semipermeable membrane against dialysing fluid (plasma-like, minus urea) - urea and excess ions diffuse out down gradients while cells and proteins stay. Heparin prevents circuit clotting; sessions run ~4 hours, thrice weekly. Peritoneal dialysis uses the patient own peritoneum as the membrane. Transplant remains the definitive cure, needing immunosuppression (cyclosporin) against rejection.

Topic in Depth · Dialysis

Haemodialysis replaces failed filtration with diffusion physics: blood flows one way past cellophane, dialysing fluid (plasma-like minus urea) flows the other, and urea plus excess ions diffuse out while cells and proteins stay. Heparin stops circuit clotting; four-hour sessions run thrice weekly. Peritoneal dialysis uses the patient own peritoneum as membrane instead. Transplant cures definitively but demands lifelong immunosuppression against rejection - dialysis bridges or substitutes.
NEET Snapshot:
  • Diffusion across cellophane replaces glomerular filtration.
  • Urea out; cells and proteins stay - size-selective rescue.
NEET Practice MCQ
1

Dialysing fluid differs from plasma mainly in containing

Solution: The urea-free gradient pulls wastes out while matched ions prevent imbalance.
Back to Index
Index / Human Physiology
Chapter 6 · Topic 11

Kidney disorders

Excretory Products and EliminationHuman PhysiologyStep-by-step Topic
Kidney failure (uraemia) — excess urea in blood; treated by haemodialysis (blood filtered across cellophane against dialysing fluid) and transplant. Kidney stones (calcium oxalate crystallisation), glomerulonephritis (inflammation), pyelonephritis, cystitis. Sweat, sebum, lungs (CO₂, water vapour) and liver (bilirubin, cholesterol) are accessory excretory routes.

Deeper Understanding · Dialysis principle

Haemodialysis works purely on diffusion across a semipermeable cellophane membrane: patient blood flows one way, dialysing fluid (same composition as plasma minus urea) flows the opposite way, so urea and excess ions diffuse out while cells and proteins stay. Heparin prevents clotting in the machine. Peritoneal dialysis uses the patient own peritoneum as the membrane instead. Kidney stones are mostly calcium oxalate; small ones pass with water loading, large ones need lithotripsy (shock-wave shattering).

Topic in Depth · Kidney disorders

Kidney failures split by cause: uraemia (urea accumulation - dialysis or transplant), kidney stones (calcium oxalate crystallisation - water loading, lithotripsy shocks), glomerulonephritis (antibody-inflamed filters leaking protein and blood), pyelonephritis (bacterial pelvis infection), cystitis (bladder inflammation). Accessory excretion continues regardless - lungs blow CO2, skin sweats salts and urea, liver dumps bilirubin and cholesterol - but none replace the kidney volume control.
NEET Snapshot:
  • Uraemia needs dialysis; stones need water then shocks; infection needs antibiotics.
  • Protein or blood in urine signals filter damage.
NEET Practice MCQ
1

Kidney stones are most commonly made of

Solution: Calcium oxalate crystallises most often; big stones need lithotripsy shattering.

Index / Human Physiology
Chapter 7 · Step-by-step

Locomotion and Movement

Bones, joints, muscles, sliding-filament theory and disorders.

Locomotion and MovementHuman PhysiologyChapter Introduction

Subtopics in this Chapter

1. Types of movement2. Skeletal system3. Bones4. Joints5. Muscles6. Skeletal muscle7. Muscle contraction8. Actin9. Myosin10. Sliding filament theory11. ATP12. Muscular disorders

Deep Dive · Sarcomere mechanics

Memorise the bands: the A-band is the thick myosin filament (constant length - never changes), the I-band is thin actin on either side (shortens), the H-zone is the bare centre of the A-band (shortens/disappears), and the Z-line borders each sarcomere. During contraction actin slides over myosin without either filament shortening - so A stays constant while I and H shrink. Thin filament = actin + troponin + tropomyosin; thick filament = myosin heads with ATPase.
Cross-bridge cycle in 5 steps
1. Nerve impulse releases Ca2+ from the sarcoplasmic reticulum. 2. Ca2+ binds troponin, shifting tropomyosin off the binding sites. 3. Energised myosin heads attach to actin. 4. Power stroke pulls actin inward (ADP + Pi released). 5. Fresh ATP binds and detaches the head; ATP hydrolysis re-cocks it. Without ATP (death), heads lock attached - rigor mortis. Red fibres are myoglobin-rich, aerobic and slow; white fibres are fast and anaerobic.
Fig: Sliding filaments - Z-lines approach, I-bands shrink, A-band never changes length.
Joints + disorders
  • Ball-and-socket (shoulder, hip), hinge (knee, elbow), pivot (atlas-axis), gliding (carpals), saddle (thumb).
  • Myasthenia gravis (autoimmune, neuromuscular junction), tetany (low Ca2+, spasm), osteoporosis (low oestrogen), gout (uric acid crystals in joints).
NEET Practice MCQ
1

During muscle contraction, which band retains exactly the same length?

Solution: The A-band is the thick myosin filament itself, so its length never changes; I-bands shorten and the H-zone disappears.

Related Diagram · Synovial joint types

Fig: Shape decides movement - ball rotates all ways, hinge one way, pivot spins, flat bones glide.

Chapter 7 · Topic 1

Types of movement

Locomotion and MovementHuman PhysiologyStep-by-step Topic
Movement spans scales: amoeboid (pseudopodia - macrophages, Amoeba), ciliary (Paramecium rows, human tracheal mucus escalator, fallopian ova transport), flagellar (sperm tails, Euglena), and muscular (all vertebrate locomotion). Locomotion is movement of the whole organism; all locomotion is movement but not vice versa (tongue moves, body stays). Cilia and flagella share the 9+2 microtubule axoneme - same engine, different stroke.

Topic in Depth · Types of movement

Movement spans mechanisms: amoeboid crawling (pseudopodia - macrophages engulfing germs), ciliary beating (Paramecium rows, tracheal mucus escalator, fallopian ova transport), flagellar whipping (sperm tails, Euglena), muscular contraction (all vertebrate locomotion). All share ATP-driven protein motors on cytoskeletal tracks - cilia and flagella even share the 9+2 microtubule axoneme, differing only in stroke pattern. Locomotion moves the whole organism; movement includes stationary acts like tongue flicks and gut churning.
NEET Snapshot:
  • Amoeboid, ciliary, flagellar, muscular - four engines, one ATP fuel.
  • Cilia/flagella share 9+2 axoneme; locomotion is whole-body movement.
NEET Practice MCQ
1

Ciliary movement in humans occurs in the

Solution: Cilia sweep mucus upward in airways and ova downward in tubes - conveyor epithelia.

Index / Human Physiology
Chapter 7 · Topic 2

Skeletal system

Locomotion and MovementHuman PhysiologyStep-by-step Topic
206 bones: axial (80) — skull (8 cranial + 14 facial + 6 ear ossicles + hyoid = 29), vertebral column (33 vertebrae: 7 cervical, 12 thoracic, 5 lumbar, 5 sacral fused, 4 coccygeal fused), ribs (12 pairs; first 7 true, 8–10 false/vertebrochondral, 11–12 floating), sternum; appendicular (126) — pectoral/pelvic girdles + limbs (forelimb 30 each: humerus, radius-ulna, 8 carpals, 5 metacarpals, 14 phalanges; hindlimb 30 each with femur — longest bone, patella, tibia-fibula, 7 tarsals). Sternum + ribs + vertebrae protect heart and lungs.
NEET facts:
  • Smallest bone = stapes; longest = femur; only movable skull bone = mandible.
  • Atlas–axis joint allows the "no" nodding (pivot joint).

Deeper Understanding · Marrow and special bones

Red marrow (skull, ribs, vertebrae, pelvis ends of long bones) manufactures all blood cells; with age much converts to fatty yellow marrow, which can reconvert during severe anaemia. Sesamoid bones form inside tendons - the patella is the largest, improving the leverage of thigh muscles over the knee. Only the mandible moves in the skull; the hyoid bone floats free, anchoring the tongue. The atlas carries the skull (yes-nodding) while the axis pivot allows head rotation (no-shaking) - both named for their jobs.

Related Diagram · Axial vs appendicular skeleton

Fig: Axial trunk protects vitals; appendicular limbs move you - 80 plus 126 equals 206.

Topic in Depth · Skeletal system

The 206-bone scaffold splits 80 axial (skull 29 with ear ossicles and hyoid, vertebral column 33 across 5 regions, ribs 12 pairs in true/false/floating sets, sternum) plus 126 appendicular (girdles plus 30 bones per limb - humerus/femur, forearm/leg pairs, wrist/ankle clusters, 5 metapodials, 14 phalanges). Axial protects vitals (brain, heart, lungs); appendicular moves. Rib-sternum-vertebrae cage expands for breathing; pelvic girdle bears weight and birth passage.
NEET Snapshot:
  • Axial 80 (protect) + appendicular 126 (move) = 206.
  • Floating ribs 11-12 attach to nothing in front.

Chapter 7 · Topic 3

Bones

Locomotion and MovementHuman PhysiologyStep-by-step Topic
Bones are living composite: osteocytes in lacunae, Haversian canals carrying vessels, matrix of calcium salts + collagen for hardness with slight flex. Longest bone femur, smallest stapes; only movable skull bone is the mandible. Red marrow makes blood cells; minerals bank calcium/phosphorus released by PTH. Osteoporosis (low oestrogen, thin porous bones) vs arthritis (joint inflammation) vs gout (urate crystals) - three different tissues failing three ways.

Topic in Depth · Bones

Bones are living composite beams: osteocytes in lacunae maintain matrix of calcium salts (hardness) plus collagen (flex), Haversian canals pipe vessels through osteons, marrow makes blood (red) or stores fat (yellow). Longest femur, smallest stapes, only-movable-skull mandible, free-floating hyoid. Bones bank calcium and phosphorus released by PTH, transmit muscle force as levers, and shield marrow, brain and thoracic organs - support, movement, protection, manufacture, storage in one tissue.
NEET Snapshot:
  • Hardness from minerals, flex from collagen, life from cells and vessels.
  • Femur longest; stapes smallest; mandible only movable skull bone.
Remember
  • Bone heals fast (vascular); cartilage heals slowly (avascular).

Solution: Mineral salts give rigidity, collagen gives slight flex - composite engineering.

Chapter 7 · Topic 4

Joints

Locomotion and MovementHuman PhysiologyStep-by-step Topic
Joints by mobility: fibrous (skull sutures - immovable), cartilaginous (vertebrae, pubic symphysis - slight movement), synovial (fluid-filled, free): ball-and-socket (shoulder, hip - all directions), hinge (knee, elbow - one plane), pivot (atlas-axis - rotation), gliding (carpals - sliding), saddle (thumb - grip). Ligaments stabilise, tendons motorise, bursae lubricate - sprain (ligament), strain (muscle), dislocation (bone out of socket) injure each in turn.

Topic in Depth · Joints

Joints trade stability for mobility along a spectrum: fibrous (skull sutures - fused, zero movement), cartilaginous (vertebrae discs, pubic symphysis - slight cushioning give), synovial (fluid-filled capsules - free movement in shaped patterns: ball-and-socket shoulders/hips rotate all ways, hinge knees/elbows swing one plane, pivot atlas-axis spins the head, gliding carpals slide, saddle thumbs oppose). Ligaments stabilise, bursae lubricate, menisci cushion - sprain (ligament), dislocation (bone out), arthritis (inflamed) injure each part distinctly.
NEET Snapshot:
  • Fused, slightly movable, freely movable - three joint grades.
  • Shape decides motion: ball rotates, hinge swings, pivot spins.
NEET Practice MCQ
1

Solution: Round head in cup socket rotates in all planes - shoulder and hip only.

Chapter 7 · Topic 5

Muscles

Locomotion and MovementHuman PhysiologyStep-by-step Topic
Three muscle types, three lifestyles: skeletal (long cylindrical multinucleate striated fibres - voluntary, tendon-attached, fatigues), smooth (spindle uninucleate non-striated - involuntary gut/vessel/iris walls, never fatigues), cardiac (branched uninucleate striated with intercalated discs - involuntary rhythmic heart pump). Red (slow, myoglobin-rich, aerobic posture) vs white (fast, anaerobic sprint) fibres mix per muscle use. Muscle is ~40% of body mass.

Related Diagram · Three muscle fibres side by side

Fig: Stripes mean sarcomeres; branches plus discs mean a heart that beats as one.

Topic in Depth · Muscles

Three muscle architectures serve three masters: skeletal (long multinucleate striated fibres, voluntary, tendon-attached - powerful but fatiguing), smooth (spindle uninucleate plain fibres, involuntary - gut peristalsis, vessel tone, iris focus, tireless), cardiac (branched uninucleate striated fibres with intercalated discs - involuntary rhythmic pump, near-unfatiguable). Red slow fibres (myoglobin-rich, aerobic posture) versus white fast fibres (glycolytic sprints) tune each muscle; total muscle runs ~40 percent of body mass.
NEET Snapshot:
  • Skeletal voluntary+striped; smooth involuntary+plain; cardiac involuntary+striped+discs.
  • Red fibres endure; white fibres explode.
NEET Practice MCQ
1

Smooth muscles are

Solution: Spindle-shaped uninucleate fibres run gut, vessels and iris without conscious command.

Chapter 7 · Topic 6

Skeletal muscle

Locomotion and MovementHuman PhysiologyStep-by-step Topic
Skeletal muscle fibres are syncytia - hundreds of nuclei from fused myoblasts - packed with parallel myofibrils of alternating actin/myosin that create striations. Each fibre is wired by one motor neuron at the neuromuscular junction (acetylcholine trigger); a motor neuron plus its fibres form a motor unit - small units (eye) for precision, large units (thigh) for power. Sarcoplasmic reticulum stores the Ca2+ that starts every twitch.

Topic in Depth · Skeletal muscle

Skeletal fibres are fused syncytia - hundreds of nuclei from merged myoblasts - stuffed with parallel myofibrils whose actin-myosin banding creates striations. Each fibre answers one motor neuron at a neuromuscular junction (acetylcholine trigger); neuron plus its fibres form a motor unit - tiny (eye precision) to huge (thigh power). Sarcoplasmic reticulum warehouses the calcium that starts every twitch; mitochondria and myoglobin fund sustained work.
NEET Snapshot:
  • Multinucleate syncytia with striated myofibrils.
  • Motor unit size matches precision needs - eyes small, thighs large.
Remember
  • One neuron, many fibres per unit - recruitment grades force smoothly.
NEET Practice MCQ
1

Neuromuscular junction uses which neurotransmitter?

Solution: Motor nerve acetylcholine triggers skeletal fibres; its blockers cause paralysis.

Index / Human Physiology
Chapter 7 · Topic 7

Muscle contraction

Locomotion and MovementHuman PhysiologyStep-by-step Topic
Contraction converts nerve signal to pull: impulse releases Ca2+ from sarcoplasmic reticulum - Ca2+ binds troponin, tropomyosin shifts, myosin heads grab actin - power stroke drags thin filaments inward - fresh ATP detaches heads for the next cycle. Summed twitches build sustained tetanus (normal posture tone is partial tetanus). No ATP means no detachment - the stiffness of rigor mortis. Energy ultimately comes from creatine phosphate, then glycogen, then fats.

Related Diagram · Cross-bridge cycle in 5 steps

Fig: Calcium opens, myosin grabs, stroke pulls, ATP releases - repeat thousands of times per second.

Topic in Depth · Muscle contraction

Contraction converts nerve signal to pull in milliseconds: impulse releases sarcoplasmic Ca2+, troponin shifts tropomyosin off actin sites, energised myosin heads grab on, power stroke drags thin filaments inward, fresh ATP detaches heads for the next cycle - thousands of asynchronous cycles per fibre per second. Summed twitches fuse into sustained tetanus (normal posture tone is partial tetanus). Energy cascades creatine phosphate to glycogen to fats as effort extends.
NEET Snapshot:
  • Calcium opens, myosin pulls, ATP releases - the three-beat cycle.
  • Summed twitches fuse into smooth sustained force.
NEET Practice MCQ
1

The immediate energy source for muscle contraction is

Solution: ATP powers strokes directly; creatine phosphate instantly rebuilds ATP for seconds.

Index / Human Physiology
Chapter 7 · Topic 8

Actin

Locomotion and MovementHuman PhysiologyStep-by-step Topic
Actin (thin filament) is a double helix of globular monomers wrapped with regulatory tropomyosin strands plus troponin complexes (TnC binds Ca2+, TnT anchors tropomyosin, TnI inhibits). At rest tropomyosin physically covers myosin-binding sites; calcium-bound troponin drags it aside. Actin also builds cell cytoskeletons, microvilli and cleavage furrows - the most abundant intracellular protein, moonlighting everywhere.

Topic in Depth · Actin

Actin thin filaments are double helices of globular monomers wrapped with tropomyosin ropes plus troponin complexes (TnC calcium sensor, TnT tropomyosin anchor, TnI inhibitor). At rest tropomyosin physically covers myosin-binding sites; calcium-bound troponin drags it aside to expose them. Beyond muscle, actin builds microvilli, cleavage furrows and cell crawlers - the most abundant intracellular protein, moonlighting in every cell shape change.
NEET Snapshot:
  • Thin filament = actin helix + tropomyosin + troponin trio.
  • Calcium via troponin exposes binding sites - the contraction switch.
NEET Practice MCQ
1

Troponin binds

Solution: Ca2+ binding to TnC shifts tropomyosin off actin sites, starting contraction.
Back to Index
Index / Human Physiology
Chapter 7 · Topic 9

Myosin

Locomotion and MovementHuman PhysiologyStep-by-step Topic
Myosin (thick filament) bundles with tails packed and heads projecting - each head is an ATPase motor plus actin-binding site. Heads point outward from the bare central H-zone in both directions, which is why actin gets pulled symmetrically inward. Myosin powers more than muscle: cytokinesis rings, vesicle transport and amoeboid crawling all run on myosin motors walking actin tracks.

Topic in Depth · Myosin

Myosin thick filaments bundle tail-to-tail with heads projecting outward from a bare central H-zone - each head an ATPase motor plus actin grip. Because heads point both ways from centre, actin gets pulled symmetrically inward from both Z-lines (which is why H-zones shrink). Beyond muscle, myosin motors walk vesicles along actin tracks, pinch dividing cells (contractile ring) and drive amoeboid crawling - one motor family, many cellular jobs.
NEET Snapshot:
  • Thick filament = bundled tails + projecting ATPase heads.
  • Bidirectional heads pull both sides inward symmetrically.
Remember
  • A-band equals thick filament length - constant because myosin never shortens.
NEET Practice MCQ
1

Myosin heads possess enzymatic activity of

Solution: Head ATPase splits ATP to cock and fire each power stroke.
