Index / Human Physiology
Chapter 7 · Topic 10

Sliding filament theory

Locomotion and MovementHuman PhysiologyStep-by-step Topic
Sarcomere (Z-line to Z-line): A-band (thick myosin, constant), I-band (thin actin, shortens), H-zone (shortens). Thin filament = actin + troponin + tropomyosin; thick filament = myosin heads (cross-bridges) with ATPase. Cycle: nerve impulse → Ca²⁺ floods from sarcoplasmic reticulum → Ca²⁺ binds troponin, tropomyosin shifts → myosin heads attach → power stroke pulls actin inward (needs ATP) → fresh ATP detaches the head → repeat. ATP powers both the stroke and detachment (rigor mortis = no ATP). Red fibres (myoglobin-rich, aerobic, slow) vs white fibres (fast, anaerobic).

During contraction the H-zone disappears but the A-band is unchanged. Explain.

Answer: Actin slides over myosin without either filament shortening — overlap grows (H-zone shrinks, I-bands shorten) while thick-filament length (A-band) stays constant.

Deeper Understanding · Rigor, cramp and oxygen debt

Rigor mortis timeline is forensic: ATP exhaustion 2-6 hours after death locks cross-bridges (stiffness), and putrefaction enzymes release them after ~24-48 hours. A cramp is sustained involuntary contraction from overexcitation or salt loss; tetany is whole-body cramp-like spasm from low blood Ca2+ increasing nerve excitability. During intense sprinting, muscles work anaerobically, accumulating lactic acid; the post-exercise oxygen excess that clears it (liver converting lactate back) is the oxygen debt. Myoglobin gives red fibres their colour and oxygen reserve for sustained posture muscles.

Topic in Depth · Sliding filament theory

Huxley sliding theory: neither filament shortens - actin slides over myosin, overlap grows, so I-bands shorten, H-zones vanish, Z-lines approach, while A-bands stay exactly constant. Evidence nailed it: electron microscopy shows constant A with shrinking I during contraction. The theory unified anatomy (bands), chemistry (ATPase) and physiology (tension) - and every related MCQ reduces to asking which band changes and which does not.
NEET Snapshot:
  • Slide, do not shorten - overlap grows, A stays constant.
  • I shortens, H disappears, Z-lines approach.
Band fates
PointDetail
A-bandUnchanged (thick filament)
I-bandShortens (thin overlap grows)
H-zoneDisappears (bare centre covered)
SarcomereShortens overall
NEET Practice MCQ
1

During contraction, which remains unchanged?

Solution: A-band is the thick filament itself - overlap changes around it, never it.

Chapter 7 · Topic 11

ATP

Locomotion and MovementHuman PhysiologyStep-by-step Topic
ATP does two opposite jobs per cycle: its splitting powers the power stroke, and fresh ATP binding detaches the myosin head - energy both pulls and releases. Immediate backup is creatine phosphate (seconds), then anaerobic glycolysis (lactic acid, oxygen debt), then aerobic respiration (endurance). This ladder explains sprint vs marathon fuels - and why dead muscle without ATP locks stiff.

Topic in Depth · ATP

ATP plays two opposite roles per cycle: splitting powers the power stroke, while fresh ATP binding detaches the myosin head - energy both pulls and releases. Supply ladders with effort: creatine phosphate (seconds), anaerobic glycolysis (lactic acid, oxygen debt), aerobic respiration (endurance). No ATP means locked heads - rigor mortis stiffness that forensic timelines use (sets in 2-6 hours, releases in 1-2 days as tissues decay).
NEET Snapshot:
  • ATP splits to pull, ATP binds to release - dual role.
  • Creatine phosphate first, glycogen next, fats for endurance.
NEET Practice MCQ
1

Rigor mortis occurs because

Solution: Without ATP, cross-bridges lock attached - stiffness until decay enzymes free them.

Chapter 7 · Topic 12

Muscular disorders

Locomotion and MovementHuman PhysiologyStep-by-step Topic
Fibrous (skull sutures — immovable), cartilaginous (between vertebrae — slight movement), synovial (fluid-filled, freely movable): ball-and-socket (shoulder, hip), hinge (knee, elbow), pivot (atlas–axis), gliding (carpals), saddle (thumb). Disorders: myasthenia gravis (autoimmune block at neuromuscular junction → paralysis), tetany (low blood Ca²⁺ → sustained spasm), osteoporosis (low oestrogen, weak bones), arthritis, gout (uric acid in joints).
Common mistakes: gout is a joint disorder (sodium urate crystals), not a muscle disorder.

Deeper Understanding · Arthritis types + first aid of fractures

Osteoarthritis is wear-and-tear cartilage loss of old age (weight-bearing knees worst); rheumatoid arthritis is autoimmune synovial attack (symmetrical small joints, morning stiffness); gout is metabolic (sodium urate crystals, sudden big-toe agony, rich-food trigger). Fracture healing stages - haematoma, fibrocartilage callus, bony callus, remodelling - explain why immobilisation (plaster) must last 6-8 weeks: osteoblasts need stillness to bridge the gap. Dislocation (bone out of joint) vs sprain (ligament overstretch) vs strain (muscle overstretch) differ only in which tissue fails.

Topic in Depth · Muscular disorders

Muscle-joint failures split by tissue: myasthenia gravis (autoimmune acetylcholine-receptor block - drooping, fatigable weakness), tetany (low blood Ca2+ overexcites nerves - sustained spasms), muscular dystrophy (DMD - X-linked dystrophin lack, progressive wasting), osteoporosis (low oestrogen porous bones), osteoarthritis (age-worn cartilage), rheumatoid arthritis (autoimmune synovium), gout (urate crystals, sudden toe agony). Treatment matches tissue - calcium, immunosuppression, joint replacement, diet control respectively.
NEET Snapshot:
  • Myasthenia: receptor block. Tetany: low calcium. Dystrophy: dystrophin gene.
  • Gout hits joints (urate), not muscles - name traps.
Remember
  • Match disorder to molecule: receptors, calcium, dystrophin, urate, oestrogen.
NEET Practice MCQ
1

Myasthenia gravis results from

Solution: Antibodies destroy acetylcholine receptors - signals arrive but muscles cannot listen.

Chapter 8 · Step-by-step

Neural Control and Coordination

Neuron, impulse, synapse, brain, reflexes and sense organs.

Neural Control and CoordinationHuman PhysiologyChapter Introduction

Subtopics in this Chapter

1. Neuron2. Nervous system3. Nerve impulse4. Resting potential5. Action potential6. Synapse7. CNS8. PNS9. Brain10. Spinal cord11. Reflex action12. Reflex arc13. Sensory receptors

Deep Dive · Nerve impulse and brain map

Impulse in sequence: resting membrane at -70 mV (Na+-K+ ATPase pumps 3 Na+ out, 2 K+ in). A stimulus opens voltage-gated Na+ channels - Na+ rushes in and the membrane shoots to about +30 mV (depolarisation). Na+ channels close, K+ channels open, K+ leaves (repolarisation) with a brief hyperpolarisation overshoot. Conduction is all-or-none: weak stimuli fail, and any successful impulse has full strength. Myelinated fibres conduct by saltatory jumps between nodes of Ranvier - far faster than continuous conduction in unmyelinated fibres.
Brain parts - one-line jobs
PartJob (NEET one-liners)
CerebrumIntelligence, memory, voluntary acts; hemispheres joined by corpus callosum
ThalamusSensory relay station
HypothalamusHunger, thirst, temperature, hormones
MidbrainVisual and auditory reflexes
CerebellumBalance and coordination
PonsBridge + pneumotaxic breathing centre
MedullaRespiration, heartbeat, swallowing, vomiting, BP
Reflex arc: receptor to sensory neuron to spinal interneuron to motor neuron to effector - the brain is informed but not consulted, which is why withdrawal is instant. Autonomic split: sympathetic = emergency (dilated pupils, fast heart, dry mouth); parasympathetic = rest-and-digest (constricted pupils, slow heart, digestion on). Eye defects: myopia (elongated eyeball, concave lens), hypermetropia (short eyeball, convex lens); rods carry rhodopsin for dim light, cones pack the fovea for colour; the blind spot is where the optic nerve exits (no photoreceptors).
NEET Practice MCQ
1

The resting membrane potential of a neuron and its basis are

Solution: At rest the membrane sits near -70 mV because the Na+-K+ ATPase continuously pumps 3 Na+ out for every 2 K+ in.

Fig: One-way chemical bridge - Ca2+ in, transmitter across, enzyme cleanup behind.

Chapter 8 · Topic 1

Neuron

Neural Control and CoordinationHuman PhysiologyStep-by-step Topic
Neuron = cell body (Nissl granules), dendrites (receive) and axon (transmit; may be myelinated by Schwann cells with nodes of Ranvier). Resting potential −70 mV maintained by Na⁺–K⁺ ATPase (3 Na⁺ out, 2 K⁺ in). Stimulus opens Na⁺ channels → depolarisation (+30 mV) → K⁺ efflux → repolarisation → slight hyperpolarisation. Impulse conduction is all-or-none; myelinated fibres conduct by fast saltatory jumps.
NEET facts:
  • Fastest fibres are myelinated; unmyelinated conduction is slow and continuous.
  • Major neurotransmitters: acetylcholine, GABA (inhibitory), glutamate (excitatory), dopamine, serotonin.

Deeper Understanding · Myelin makers and speed math

CNS myelin comes from oligodendrocytes (one cell wraps many axons), PNS myelin from Schwann cells (one cell, one axon segment) - which is why peripheral nerves regenerate after injury but spinal cord does not (oligodendrocyte debris actively inhibits regrowth). Speed scales with diameter and myelin: unmyelinated C-fibres crawl at ~1 m/s (dull aching pain), myelinated A-fibres fly at ~100 m/s (sharp pain, reflexes). Multiple sclerosis is autoimmune myelin destruction - slowed, scattered signalling causing tremor and blurred vision.

Related Diagram · Structure of a neuron

Fig: Neuron — dendrites receive, cell body integrates, myelinated axon conducts fast, knobs pass the signal across synapses.

Topic in Depth · Neuron

Neurons are extreme one-way wires: dendrites collect thousands of inputs, the cell body (with Nissl granules for heavy protein synthesis) integrates, one axon transmits - myelinated by Schwann cells (PNS) or oligodendrocytes (CNS) with node gaps enabling 100 m/s saltatory jumps versus 1 m/s bare crawl. Synaptic knobs convert the electrical wave to chemical message at each handoff. Mature neurons barely divide - the brain banks its lifetime supply before birth.
NEET Snapshot:
  • Dendrites in, axon out, myelin speeds, knobs hand off.
  • Nissl granules mark heavy protein-making cell bodies.
NEET Practice MCQ
1

Nissl granules are found in the

Solution: Rough-ER clusters make proteins for the huge cell - absent from axons.

Index / Human Physiology
Chapter 8 · Topic 2

Nervous system

Neural Control and CoordinationHuman PhysiologyStep-by-step Topic
The nervous system divides by structure (CNS: brain + spinal cord; PNS: 12 cranial + 31 spinal nerve pairs) and by function (somatic voluntary vs autonomic involuntary - sympathetic emergency vs parasympathetic rest-and-digest). Information flows receptor to sensory neuron to CNS to motor neuron to effector - with reflex arcs shortcutting the brain for speed. Neurons plus glia form the tissue; myelin plus synapses set its speed and flexibility.

Topic in Depth · Nervous system

The nervous system organises by structure (CNS brain-plus-cord versus PNS 12 cranial plus 31 spinal pairs) and by command type (somatic voluntary single-neuron lines versus autonomic two-neuron chains - sympathetic emergency versus parasympathetic rest-digest). Information loops receptor-sensory-CNS-motor-effector, with spinal reflex shortcuts for speed. Myelin colour-codes tracts (white) versus processing zones (grey); meninges plus CSF armour the core.
NEET Snapshot:
  • CNS integrates; PNS connects; somatic obeys; autonomic manages.
  • Sympathetic spends energy; parasympathetic rebuilds it.
Remember
  • Two-neuron autonomic chains versus one-neuron somatic lines - count the relays.
NEET Practice MCQ
1

Sympathetic nervous system generally causes

Solution: Emergency mode dilates pupils, accelerates heart and halts digestion - fight or flight.

Index / Human Physiology
Chapter 8 · Topic 3

Nerve impulse

Neural Control and CoordinationHuman PhysiologyStep-by-step Topic
An impulse is a travelling wave of depolarisation: stimulus opens Na+ channels, membrane shoots from -70 toward +30 mV, then K+ exit repolarises it - each patch triggering the next, like dominoes. Conduction is all-or-none (stimulus must cross threshold; bigger stimuli only fire faster, never bigger) and unidirectional (the trailing patch is refractory). Myelinated fibres jump node to node (saltatory, ~100 m/s); bare fibres crawl continuously (~1 m/s).

Topic in Depth · Nerve impulse

Impulses are travelling depolarisation waves: stimulus opens Na+ gates (up to +30 mV), then K+ exit repolarises - each patch triggering the next like dominoes, strictly one-way because trailing patches rest refractory. Firing is all-or-none (threshold decides; stronger stimuli only fire faster), and myelin converts crawl to 100 m/s saltatory jumps between nodes. Speed, direction and reliability emerge from channel physics, not wiring diagrams.
NEET Snapshot:
  • Domino depolarisation, one-way by refractory trail.
  • All-or-none firing; rate codes strength, not size.
NEET Practice MCQ
1

Nerve impulse conduction is unidirectional mainly because the

Solution: Just-fired membrane cannot refire immediately, so waves advance only forward.
Back to Index
Index / Human Physiology
Chapter 8 · Topic 4

Resting potential

Neural Control and CoordinationHuman PhysiologyStep-by-step Topic
Resting potential (-70 mV) is built by the Na+-K+ ATPase pumping 3 Na+ out for every 2 K+ in (net positive loss), plus higher K+ leak permeability and trapped intracellular anions (proteins, phosphates). The result: outside positive, inside negative, poised like a loaded spring. Blocking the pump (cyanide energy failure) or the leaks (toxins) collapses the voltage - and with it, all signalling.

Topic in Depth · Resting potential

Resting -70 mV is active bookkeeping: Na+-K+ ATPase pumps 3 Na+ out per 2 K+ in (net positive loss), K+ leaks out faster than Na+ leaks in, and trapped intracellular anions (proteins, phosphates) hold the inside negative. The result is a loaded spring - poised energy awaiting stimulus. Poison the pump (energy failure), plug the leaks (toxins), or flood extracellular K+ (hyperkalaemia), and excitability collapses with the voltage.
NEET Snapshot:
  • Pump (3 out, 2 in) plus leaks plus trapped anions = -70 mV.
  • Resting voltage is stored excitability, not idleness.
Potential values
PointDetail
Resting-70 mV (pump-maintained)
Peak~+30 mV (Na+ flood)
AfterBrief hyperpolarisation, then reset
NEET Practice MCQ
1

Resting potential is maintained mainly by

Solution: The pump builds gradients; selective leaks shape the -70 mV steady state.

Index / Human Physiology
Chapter 8 · Topic 5

Action potential

Neural Control and CoordinationHuman PhysiologyStep-by-step Topic
Action potential phases: threshold crossing, rapid depolarisation to +30 mV (voltage-gated Na+ flood), repolarisation (Na+ close, K+ flood out), brief hyperpolarisation (slow K+ gates), then pump restoration. Absolute refractory period (Na+ gates locked) caps firing rate and enforces one-way travel. Local anaesthetics (lidocaine) work by plugging Na+ channels - no depolarisation, no pain signal.

Topic in Depth · Action potential

Action potentials stage four acts: threshold crossing, explosive depolarisation to +30 mV (voltage-gated Na+ flood), repolarisation (Na+ close, K+ flood out), brief hyperpolarisation (slow K+ gates), then pump restoration. Absolute refractory period (locked Na+ gates) caps firing rate and enforces one-way travel. Local anaesthetics (lidocaine) plug Na+ channels - no depolarisation, no pain signal, fully reversible blockade.
NEET Snapshot:
  • Depolarise (Na+ in), repolarise (K+ out), reset (pump).
  • Refractory period sets max firing rate and direction.
Remember
  • Na+ blockers silence nerves - anaesthesia is applied channel physics.
NEET Practice MCQ
1

Repolarisation of an excited neuron is due to

Solution: Potassium rushing out restores negativity after sodium entry peaks.
Back to Index
Index / Human Physiology
Chapter 8 · Topic 6

Synapse

Neural Control and CoordinationHuman PhysiologyStep-by-step Topic
Pre-synaptic knob → synaptic cleft (fluid-filled gap) → post-synaptic membrane. Arriving impulse opens Ca²⁺ channels; vesicles fuse and release acetylcholine, which binds receptors and starts a fresh impulse (or inhibits it). Enzyme acetylcholinesterase destroys excess transmitter so the synapse resets — the basis of one-way conduction.

Organophosphate insecticides inhibit acetylcholinesterase. What happens at synapses and why is it dangerous?

Answer: Acetylcholine accumulates, causing continuous stimulation, muscle twitching, paralysis and potentially fatal respiratory failure.

Deeper Understanding · Summation at the synapse

One EPSP rarely fires a neuron; thousands summate - spatial summation (many synapses at once) and temporal summation (rapid repeated hits). Inhibitory GABA synapses subtract via IPSPs, so firing reflects a running vote. Botulinum toxin blocks acetylcholine release (flaccid paralysis - yet used in micro-doses cosmetically), while black-widow venom dumps all vesicles at once (cramp then exhaustion). Myasthenia gravis destroys the postsynaptic receptors themselves, so the signal arrives but nobody listens.

Topic in Depth · Synapse

Synapses are one-way chemical bridges: arriving impulse opens Ca2+ channels, vesicles fuse and spill acetylcholine (or GABA/glutamate), receptors fire or inhibit the next cell, then acetylcholinesterase destroys leftover transmitter to reset. Summation tallies thousands of EPSPs against IPSPs - firing is a running vote. Botulinum blocks release (flaccid paralysis), black-widow venom dumps everything (cramp then exhaustion), organophosphates jam the cleanup enzyme - each poison maps one step.
NEET Snapshot:
  • Ca2+ in, transmitter across, enzyme cleanup - one-way triplet.
  • EPSPs excite, IPSPs inhibit; summation decides firing.
NEET Practice MCQ
1

Acetylcholine is broken down in the synaptic cleft by

Solution: Rapid enzymatic cleanup resets the synapse for the next signal within milliseconds.
Back to Index
Index / Human Physiology
Chapter 8 · Topic 7

CNS

Neural Control and CoordinationHuman PhysiologyStep-by-step Topic
CNS (brain + spinal cord) is the integration core, wrapped in three meninges (dura, arachnoid, pia) with CSF cushioning inside the subarachnoid space. Grey matter (cell bodies, cortex outside in brain) processes; white matter (myelinated tracts) connects. The spinal cord additionally hosts reflex arcs and two-way traffic - yet unlike peripheral nerves, its injured tracts barely regenerate (oligodendrocyte inhibitors + glial scars).

Topic in Depth · CNS

CNS (brain plus spinal cord) integrates everything inside triple meninges (dura, arachnoid, pia) with CSF shock-absorption and astrocyte blood-brain barrier filtering. Grey matter processes (cortical sheets, cord butterfly horns), white matter connects (myelinated tracts). The cord adds reflex autonomy and two-way traffic; unlike peripheral nerves, injured CNS tracts barely regenerate (oligodendrocyte inhibitors plus glial scars) - making brain and spine injuries uniquely serious.
NEET Snapshot:
  • Meninges plus CSF plus barrier = triple-armoured core.
  • Grey processes; white connects; cord reflexes independently.
NEET Practice MCQ
1

Cerebrospinal fluid is found in the

Solution: CSF cushions brain and cord inside the meninges and ventricles.

Chapter 8 · Topic 8

PNS

Neural Control and CoordinationHuman PhysiologyStep-by-step Topic
PNS wiring: 12 cranial nerve pairs (brain - including vagus, the wandering parasympathetic highway) + 31 spinal pairs (mixed sensory-motor). Somatic division drives voluntary skeletal muscle (single neuron from CNS); autonomic uses two-neuron chains (pre + post ganglionic) to reach smooth muscle, glands and heart - sympathetic (thoracolumbar, emergency) vs parasympathetic (craniosacral, rest-digest). Shingles (reactivated chickenpox virus in dorsal root ganglia) proves ganglia locations clinically.

Topic in Depth · PNS

PNS wiring totals 12 cranial pairs (brain - smell to vagus wandering) plus 31 spinal pairs (mixed sensory-motor at each segment). Somatic lines run single-neuron voluntary commands to skeletal muscle; autonomic chains relay through ganglia - sympathetic (thoracolumbar, emergency spend) versus parasympathetic (craniosacral, rest-digest rebuild). Shingles (chickenpox virus hiding in dorsal root ganglia) clinically proves ganglion geography.
NEET Snapshot:
  • 12 cranial + 31 spinal = 43 nerve pairs.
  • Somatic one relay; autonomic two relays with opposite goals.
Remember
  • Vagus (cranial X) carries most parasympathetic traffic - wandering regulator.
NEET Practice MCQ
1

Parasympathetic system arises from

Solution: Cranial plus sacral outflows oppose thoracolumbar sympathetic - anatomical opposites.
Back to Index
Index / Human Physiology
Chapter 8 · Topic 9

Brain

Neural Control and CoordinationHuman PhysiologyStep-by-step Topic
Forebrain: cerebrum (intelligence, memory, voluntary acts; corpus callosum joins hemispheres), thalamus (relay), hypothalamus (hunger, thirst, temperature, hormones). Midbrain: visual/auditory reflexes. Hindbrain: cerebellum (balance, coordination), pons, medulla (respiration, heartbeat, swallowing, vomiting, blood pressure). Brain coverings = cranial meninges (dura, arachnoid, pia). Spinal cord conducts two-way traffic and hosts reflex arcs.

Deeper Understanding · Meninges, CSF and injuries

Three meninges - dura (tough outer), arachnoid (webby middle), pia (delicate inner) - cushion brain and spinal cord, with cerebrospinal fluid in the subarachnoid space acting as a shock absorber and chemical buffer. A hard blow can bruise the brain against the skull opposite the impact (contrecoup injury). The blood-brain barrier (astrocyte-wrapped capillaries) blocks most drugs and pathogens - which is why brain infections and tumours are harder to treat, and why only lipid-soluble anaesthetics work fast.

Related Diagram · Side view of the human brain

Fig: Brain (side view) — cerebrum for thought and voluntary acts, cerebellum for balance, medulla for breathing and heartbeat.

Topic in Depth · Brain

Brain regions divide the workload: cerebrum (intelligence, memory, voluntary acts; corpus callosum joins hemispheres), thalamus (sensory relay), hypothalamus (hunger, thirst, temperature, hormones), midbrain (visual-auditory reflexes), cerebellum (balance, coordination), pons (bridge plus breathing tuning), medulla (respiration, heartbeat, swallowing, vomiting, BP - the vital core). Damage maps function - cerebellar injury staggers, medullary injury kills.
NEET Snapshot:
  • Forebrain thinks, midbrain reflexes, hindbrain sustains.
  • Medulla holds life-or-death centres; cerebrum holds mind.
Brain jobs
PointDetail
CerebrumThought, memory, voluntary acts
CerebellumBalance, coordination
MedullaBreathing, heartbeat, BP
HypothalamusHunger, thirst, hormones

Breathing rhythm centre is located in the

Solution: Medullary inspiratory-expiratory groups generate rhythm; pons smooths it.

Chapter 8 · Topic 10

Spinal cord

Neural Control and CoordinationHuman PhysiologyStep-by-step Topic
The spinal cord runs from medulla to first lumbar vertebra (below that, dangling roots form the cauda equina - the safe lumbar-puncture zone). Cross-section shows butterfly grey matter (dorsal sensory + ventral motor horns) inside white tracts (ascending sensation, descending commands). It conducts two-way traffic and independently runs reflexes - knee-jerk tests the L2-L4 loop without involving the brain at all.

Topic in Depth · Spinal cord

The cord runs medulla to first lumbar vertebra, ending in dangling cauda equina roots (the safe lumbar-puncture zone below). Cross-section shows butterfly grey (dorsal sensory plus ventral motor horns) inside white ascending-descending tracts. It conducts two-way traffic and independently runs reflex arcs - knee-jerk tests the L2-L4 loop without brain involvement. Cord transection paralyses everything below (paraplegia/quadriplegia by level) while reflexes may persist.
NEET Snapshot:
  • Medulla to L1, then cauda equina - puncture below L1 safely.
  • Grey butterfly processes; white tracts transport.
NEET Practice MCQ
1

Lumbar puncture for CSF sampling is done below

Solution: The cord ends at L1; floating roots below can be safely sampled around.

Index / Human Physiology
Chapter 8 · Topic 11

Reflex action

Neural Control and CoordinationHuman PhysiologyStep-by-step Topic
Reflex actions are instant, involuntary, protective responses - withdrawing from heat, blinking, coughing, pupil constriction. Simple reflexes (knee-jerk) are inborn spinal circuits; acquired/conditioned reflexes (Pavlov salivating dogs, cycling balance) are learned cerebral habits that become near-automatic. Clinicians grade reflexes to localise lesions: exaggerated means upper-motor damage, absent means lower-motor or sensory break.

Topic in Depth · Reflex action

Reflexes are instant involuntary protectors - withdrawing from heat, blinking, coughing, pupil constriction - executed by spinal or brainstem circuits without waiting for conscious decisions. Simple (inborn, knee-jerk) versus conditioned (learned to automaticity - Pavlov salivation, cycling balance) split the family. Clinicians grade reflexes to localise lesions: exaggerated means upper-motor damage, absent means lower-motor or sensory break.
NEET Snapshot:
  • Fast, involuntary, protective - brain informed, not consulted.
  • Exaggerated reflexes point up; absent ones point down the circuit.
Remember
  • Conditioned reflexes prove even learning can become reflex-fast.
NEET Practice MCQ
1

Knee-jerk reflex is an example of

Solution: Stretch receptors signal cord interneurons that fire motor neurons directly - no brain delay.

Index / Human Physiology
Chapter 8 · Topic 12

Reflex arc

Neural Control and CoordinationHuman PhysiologyStep-by-step Topic
PNS: cranial nerves (12 pairs) + spinal nerves (31 pairs); somatic (voluntary) vs autonomic — sympathetic (emergency) vs parasympathetic (rest-and-digest). Reflex arc: receptor → sensory neuron → spinal cord interneuron → motor neuron → effector (e.g. knee-jerk, pupil constriction) — fast, involuntary, protective. Eye: rods (rhodopsin, dim light) vs cones (colour, fovea); blind spot where the optic nerve exits. Ear: malleus–incus–stapes amplify sound; cochlea (hearing), semicircular canals + utricle/saccule (balance).
Fig: Eye - cornea and lens focus light on the retina (rods and cones); signals leave via the optic nerve (blind spot).
Common mistakes: sympathetic dilates pupils and accelerates heartbeat; parasympathetic does the reverse — students often swap them.

Deeper Understanding · Hearing lever + balance labyrinth

The ossicle chain (malleus-incus-stapes) is a lever plus area-ratio amplifier converting faint air vibrations into strong fluid waves in the cochlea - the stapes footplate on the oval window is the smallest bone pressing the inner ear. Loud sounds trigger the acoustic reflex (stapedius muscle stiffens the chain). Balance splits too: semicircular canals sense rotation (cristae), while utricle and saccule (otoliths - literal ear stones) sense gravity and linear acceleration - motion sickness is their disagreement with vision.

Topic in Depth · Reflex arc

Reflex arcs wire five links: receptor (detects), sensory neuron (carries in), cord interneuron (decides), motor neuron (carries out), effector (acts). Monosynaptic arcs (knee-jerk, direct sensory-to-motor) are fastest; polysynaptic arcs (withdrawal, with interneurons) allow modulation. Pupil, salivary and cough arcs route through brainstem instead - same five-link logic, cranial address. Each link is a lesion site with distinct clinical signs.
NEET Snapshot:
  • Receptor - sensory - interneuron - motor - effector.
  • Five links, each a possible lesion with its own sign.
NEET Practice MCQ
1

Correct reflex arc order is

Solution: Sense in, decide centrally, act out - the universal protective circuit.
Back to Index
Index / Human Physiology
Chapter 8 · Topic 13

Sensory receptors

Neural Control and CoordinationHuman PhysiologyStep-by-step Topic
Eye: cornea + lens focus light on the retina - rods (rhodopsin, dim light, peripheral) vs cones (colour, dense in fovea); blind spot where the optic nerve exits. Defects: myopia (long eyeball, concave lens), hypermetropia (short eyeball, convex lens), presbyopia (stiff lens with age), cataract (opaque lens). Ear: pinna collects, ossicles amplify into the cochlea (hearing), semicircular canals + otolith organs (utricle/saccule) report rotation and gravity - motion sickness is their argument with vision.

Related Diagram · Ear: hearing plus balance

Fig: Ossicles amplify sound into the cochlea; semicircular canals plus otolith organs report movement.

Topic in Depth · Sensory receptors

Eyes focus light (cornea plus lens) onto rod-cone retina - rods (rhodopsin, dim, peripheral) versus cones (colour, foveal dense) - with a blind spot at optic-nerve exit. Defects: myopia (long eyeball, concave lens), hypermetropia (short, convex), presbyopia (stiff ageing lens), cataract (opaque lens). Ears split jobs: ossicle lever (malleus-incus-stapes amplifying into cochlea for hearing) versus canals plus otolith organs (rotation plus gravity for balance) - motion sickness is their argument with vision.
NEET Snapshot:
  • Rods dim, cones colour; myopia concave, hypermetropia convex.
  • Cochlea hears; canals plus otoliths balance.

How hormones act: peptide hormones (insulin, GH) cannot enter cells - they bind surface receptors and work through second messengers like cAMP. NEET Practice MCQ
1

Which statement about calcium-regulating hormones is correct?

Solution: Calcitonin (thyroid) deposits calcium into bone, lowering blood Ca2+; PTH (parathyroid) resorbs bone, raising it - a classic opposite pair.

Chapter 9 · Topic 1

Endocrine glands

Chemical Coordination and IntegrationHuman PhysiologyStep-by-step Topic
Endocrine glands are ductless — they pour hormones directly into blood, unlike exocrine glands with ducts. The hypothalamus links the nervous and endocrine systems by releasing regulatory hormones (releasing/inhibiting factors) that command the pituitary. Hormones act only on target cells carrying matching receptors; peptide hormones bind surface receptors while steroid/thyroid hormones enter the cell and act on DNA.
NEET facts:
  • Hormones are non-nutrient, trace-amount messengers — never confuse them with enzymes (which act at the site of release).
  • Enteroendocrine cells of the gut (gastrin, secretin, CCK, GIP) coordinate digestion hormonally.

Deeper Understanding · Negative feedback - the thermostat logic

Almost every hormone axis is a negative feedback loop: rising T3/T4 suppresses hypothalamic TRH and pituitary TSH, so output falls back to normal - exactly like a thermostat switching off a heater. This single logic answers dozens of NEET questions: remove the thyroid and TSH rises (no brake); inject cortisol and ACTH falls; destroy the pituitary and all downstream glands shrink for lack of trophic signal. Positive feedback is rare - only the LH surge (estrogen exciting more LH) and oxytocin in labour (contractions exciting more oxytocin) break the rule.

Topic in Depth · Endocrine glands

Endocrine glands are ductless broadcasters - pouring hormones straight into blood for distant targets - versus exocrine glands that ship via ducts to local surfaces. Major players: hypothalamus-pituitary command, thyroid-parathyroid calcium managers, adrenal stress duo, pancreatic sugar balancers, gonadal sex drivers, plus pineal rhythm-keeper and thymus immune-trainer. Gut and heart hormones prove endocrine tissue hides outside classic glands too. Ductless plus distant plus trace-amount defines the system.
NEET Snapshot:
  • Ductless, blood-borne, trace-amount, distant-target = endocrine.
  • Hypothalamus-pituitary axis commands most downstream glands.
NEET Practice MCQ
1

Which gland is both exocrine and endocrine?

Solution: Pancreatic acini digest via ducts while islets release hormones into blood - dual role.
Back to Index
Index / Human Physiology
Chapter 9 · Topic 2

Hormones

Chemical Coordination and IntegrationHuman PhysiologyStep-by-step Topic
Hormones are non-nutrient chemical messengers secreted in trace amounts, carried by blood to distant target cells bearing matching receptors. Peptide hormones (insulin, GH, oxytocin) bind surface receptors and act via second messengers like cAMP - fast but short-lived. Steroid + thyroid hormones (cortisol, estrogen, thyroxine) enter cells, bind intracellular receptors and switch genes on - slow but long-lasting. Hormones never act where no receptor exists, which is how one blood bathes all tissues yet commands only a few.

Topic in Depth · Hormones

Hormones split by chemistry and therefore by speed: water-soluble peptides and amines (insulin, adrenaline, pituitary hormones) bind surface receptors and act in seconds-to-minutes via second messengers like cAMP; lipid-soluble steroids and thyroid hormones cross membranes, bind intracellular receptors and rewrite gene expression over hours-to-days. Target specificity comes purely from receptor presence - one blood bathes all tissues yet commands only equipped cells. Hormones are never enzymes and never nutrients.
NEET Snapshot:
  • Peptide = surface receptor, fast, cAMP. Steroid = inside receptor, slow, genetic.
  • No receptor means no response, regardless of hormone level.
Remember
  • Speed tells chemistry: seconds means peptide, days means steroid.
NEET Practice MCQ
1

Steroid hormones act through

Solution: Lipid-soluble steroids enter cells and switch genes - slow but long-lasting action.
Back to Index
Index / Human Physiology
Chapter 9 · Topic 3

Hypothalamus

Chemical Coordination and IntegrationHuman PhysiologyStep-by-step Topic
The hypothalamus is the command link between nervous and endocrine systems: neurosecretory cells release releasing and inhibiting factors (GnRH, TRH, CRH, GHRH, somatostatin, dopamine/PIH) into the hypophyseal portal vessels feeding the anterior pituitary. It also manufactures oxytocin and ADH, shipped down axons for posterior-pituitary release. Beyond hormones it runs hunger, thirst, temperature, sleep and emotions - the body true control room.

Topic in Depth · Hypothalamus

The hypothalamus is the body control room where nervous and endocrine systems merge: neurosecretory cells release regulatory factors (releasing plus inhibiting) into the hypophyseal portal feeding the anterior pituitary, and manufacture oxytocin plus ADH shipped down axons for posterior release. Beyond hormones it runs hunger, thirst, temperature, sleep, emotions and circadian rhythms - damage here unbalances weight, water, heat and mood simultaneously.
NEET Snapshot:
  • Commands pituitary via portal factors; makes oxytocin plus ADH directly.
  • Hunger, thirst, temperature, sleep - all hypothalamic.
NEET Practice MCQ
1

Hypothalamus connects to anterior pituitary through the

Solution: Releasing factors travel the short portal system - private command line, not general circulation.

Index / Human Physiology
Chapter 9 · Topic 4

Pituitary

Chemical Coordination and IntegrationHuman PhysiologyStep-by-step Topic
Anterior pituitary (adenohypophysis) secretes GH (growth hormone), TSH (thyroid), ACTH (adrenal cortex), FSH + LH (gonads — gonadotropins) and prolactin (milk production). Posterior pituitary (neurohypophysis) only stores and releases oxytocin (uterine contraction, milk ejection) and ADH/vasopressin (water reabsorption) made in the hypothalamus. GH excess: gigantism (children) and acromegaly (adults); GH lack in childhood causes dwarfism.

A tumour causes excess GH secretion starting at age 35. What condition results, and why not gigantism?

Answer: Acromegaly — after puberty the long-bone growth plates have fused, so only jaw, hands and feet enlarge instead of full-body gigantism.

Deeper Understanding · Why gigantism has an age limit

Long-bone growth happens only at open epiphyseal plates. Excess GH before puberty (plates open) grows the whole skeleton - gigantism; after puberty (plates fused) only flat bones of jaw, hands and feet can still grow - acromegaly. The same plate logic explains why oestrogen/testosterone at puberty both trigger the growth spurt and end it (they fuse the plates). Pituitary dwarfism is treatable with GH only before fusion - afterwards no hormone can lengthen bone.

Related Diagram · Pituitary hormones map

Fig: Front lobe manufactures six hormones; back lobe only stores two made upstairs.

Topic in Depth · Pituitary

The pea-sized pituitary (master gland, hanging below hypothalamus) splits duties: anterior adenohypophysis manufactures six - GH (growth), TSH (thyroid), ACTH (adrenal cortex), FSH plus LH (gonads), prolactin (milk making); posterior neurohypophysis merely stores hypothalamic oxytocin (labour, milk ejection) and ADH (water). Tumours cause over-secretion syndromes (gigantism, Cushing-like); destruction shrinks all downstream glands for lack of trophic signal.
NEET Snapshot:
  • Anterior makes 6; posterior stores 2 made upstairs.
  • Trophic hormones keep target glands alive and sized.
Trophic pairs
PointDetail
TSHThyroid
ACTHAdrenal cortex
FSH + LHGonads
GHBones, tissues generally
NEET Practice MCQ
1

Oxytocin is secreted by the

Solution: Hypothalamic neurons manufacture it; the posterior lobe only stores and releases.

Chapter 9 · Topic 5

Thyroid

Chemical Coordination and IntegrationHuman PhysiologyStep-by-step Topic
Thyroid (bilobed, largest endocrine gland): T3/T4 thyroxine (basal metabolic rate, needs iodine) + calcitonin (lowers blood Ca²⁺). Disorders: cretinism (childhood hypothyroidism — stunted, mentally retarded), myxoedema (adult hypothyroidism), simple goitre (iodine deficiency), Graves'/exophthalmic goitre (hyperthyroidism with bulging eyes), Hashimoto's (autoimmune). Parathyroids secrete PTH which raises blood calcium (bone resorption) — opposite of calcitonin. Adrenal cortex: glucocorticoids (cortisol — stress, immunity), mineralocorticoids (aldosterone — Na⁺/water), sex corticoids; failure causes Addison's disease (bronze-like pigmentation, weakness), excess causes Cushing syndrome. Adrenal medulla: adrenaline + noradrenaline — emergency fight-or-flight hormones (rapid heartbeat, dilated pupils, goosebumps).
Common mistakes: calcitonin lowers blood calcium while PTH raises it — students constantly swap the two.

Deeper Understanding · Goitre is a screaming pituitary

In iodine deficiency the thyroid cannot make T3/T4, so negative feedback disappears and the pituitary shouts louder: TSH stays high, overstimulating the gland until it visibly enlarges - a simple goitre is literally pituitary overdrive made visible. Cretinism (childhood hypothyroidism) stunts both body and brain because thyroid hormone drives neuronal myelination in infancy - replacement must start within weeks of birth. Graves disease flips the story: autoantibodies mimic TSH, forcing hyperthyroidism with bulging eyes (exophthalmos from orbital tissue swelling).

Topic in Depth · Thyroid

The butterfly thyroid (largest endocrine gland) makes iodine-dependent T3/T4 setting basal metabolic rate - growth, brain development, heat, oxygen use - plus calcitonin lowering blood calcium. Iodine lack causes goitre (TSH overdrive swelling), cretinism (childhood lack stunting body plus brain), myxoedema (adult lack slowing everything); autoantibodies mimicking TSH cause Graves hyperthyroidism with bulging eyes. Thyroid hormone needs iodine the way haemoglobin needs iron.
NEET Snapshot:
  • T3/T4 set metabolism; calcitonin lowers calcium.
  • Iodine lack = goitre; autoantibody excess = Graves disease.
NEET Practice MCQ
1

Simple goitre arises from deficiency of

Solution: No iodine means no T3/T4, so feedback-free TSH overstimulates the gland into swelling.
Back to Index
Index / Human Physiology
Chapter 9 · Topic 6

Parathyroid

Chemical Coordination and IntegrationHuman PhysiologyStep-by-step Topic
Four tiny parathyroids behind the thyroid secrete PTH (parathormone), which raises blood calcium by resorbing bone, reclaiming renal calcium and activating vitamin D for gut absorption. It exactly opposes thyroid calcitonin (which deposits calcium into bone). PTH lack (accidental removal in thyroid surgery) causes tetany - sustained muscle spasm from low blood Ca2+ overexciting nerves; excess causes bone thinning plus kidney stones.

Topic in Depth · Parathyroid

Four rice-grain parathyroids behind the thyroid secrete PTH - the blood-calcium raiser via bone resorption, renal reclaim and vitamin-D activation for gut absorption. It exactly opposes thyroid calcitonin (which deposits calcium into bone). Accidental removal during thyroid surgery drops calcium into tetany (sustained spasms from overexcitable nerves); tumours raise it into bone thinning plus kidney stones. Calcium balance is a two-hormone seesaw with PTH on the lift side.
NEET Snapshot:
  • PTH raises blood calcium; calcitonin lowers it - opposite pair.
  • Post-thyroid-surgery tetany means parathyroids were nicked.
Remember
  • Calcium seesaw: PTH up, calcitonin down - students constantly swap them.
NEET Practice MCQ
1

Tetany after thyroid surgery indicates damage to the

Solution: Lost PTH drops blood calcium, overexciting nerves into sustained muscle spasm.
Back to Index
Index / Human Physiology
Chapter 9 · Topic 7

Adrenal

Chemical Coordination and IntegrationHuman PhysiologyStep-by-step Topic
Each adrenal has two glands in one: the cortex (glucocorticoids like cortisol for stress/immunity, mineralocorticoids like aldosterone for Na+/water balance, small sex corticoids) and the medulla (adrenaline + noradrenaline for instant fight-or-flight). Cortex failure causes Addison disease (bronze pigmentation, weakness, low BP); excess causes Cushing syndrome (moon face, obesity, high sugar). Medulla removal is survivable; cortex removal is fatal without replacement.

Related Diagram · Adrenal zones

Fig: Outer cortex keeps you alive daily; inner medulla fires only in emergencies.

Topic in Depth · Adrenal

Each adrenal is two glands laminated: outer cortex (glucocorticoids like cortisol for stress metabolism plus immunity, mineralocorticoids like aldosterone for sodium-water balance, minor sex corticoids) and inner medulla (adrenaline plus noradrenaline for instant fight-or-flight). Cortex failure (Addison: bronze skin, weakness, low BP) versus excess (Cushing: moon face, obesity, high sugar) are mirror syndromes. Medulla removal is survivable; cortex removal is fatal without steroid replacement.
NEET Snapshot:
  • Cortex: slow vital steroids. Medulla: fast emergency amines.
  • Addison (failure) mirrors Cushing (excess).
NEET Practice MCQ
1

Aldosterone regulates

Solution: Mineralocorticoid aldosterone saves Na+ (water follows), defending blood volume and pressure.

Index / Human Physiology
Chapter 9 · Topic 8

Pancreas

Chemical Coordination and IntegrationHuman PhysiologyStep-by-step Topic
Pancreas (islets of Langerhans): α-cells → glucagon (raises glucose), β-cells → insulin (lowers glucose; lack causes diabetes mellitus), δ-cells → somatostatin. Gonads: testes (Leydig cells → testosterone — male secondary characters, spermatogenesis) and ovaries (estrogen — female characters; progesterone — pregnancy maintenance). Pineal → melatonin (sleep–wake rhythm); thymus → thymosin (T-lymphocyte maturation, active in the young). Diabetes insipidus (ADH lack) vs diabetes mellitus (insulin lack) is a classic distinction question.

Deeper Understanding · Insulin at the cell door

Insulin binds its surface receptor and summons Glut4 transporters from inside the cell to the membrane - doors that let glucose flood into muscle and fat. In type-2 diabetes the doors jam (insulin resistance) despite high insulin; in type-1 there is no insulin at all (autoimmune beta-cell killing). Glucagon opposes insulin by breaking liver glycogen (glycogenolysis) and making new glucose (gluconeogenesis). The delta cells somatostatin brakes both neighbours - the islet is a three-way argument, not two independent taps.

Related Diagram · Islet cells

Fig: Three cell types, three hormones - glucagon up, insulin down, somatostatin referees.

Topic in Depth · Pancreas

Pancreatic islets balance blood sugar with three cells: alpha (glucagon raises glucose via glycogen breakdown), beta (insulin lowers it via uptake plus storage), delta (somatostatin brakes both neighbours). Type-1 diabetes (autoimmune beta killing, insulin-dependent, young onset) differs fundamentally from type-2 (insulin resistance, lifestyle-linked, adult onset). Blood sugar control is push-pull - glucagon mobilises, insulin stores - with somatostatin refereeing both.
NEET Snapshot:
  • Alpha glucagon up; beta insulin down; delta somatostatin brakes.
  • Type-1 lacks insulin; type-2 resists it.
Sugar hormones
PointDetail
GlucagonRaises glucose (glycogenolysis)
InsulinLowers glucose (uptake, storage)
SomatostatinDampens both neighbours
NEET Practice MCQ
1

Insulin is secreted by pancreatic

Solution: Beta cells release insulin; alpha cells oppose with glucagon, delta cells moderate.

Index / Human Physiology
Chapter 9 · Topic 9

Pineal

Chemical Coordination and IntegrationHuman PhysiologyStep-by-step Topic
The pineal (between the cerebral hemispheres) secretes melatonin in darkness, timing sleep-wake cycles, puberty onset and seasonal rhythms - light suppresses it, which is why screens delay sleep. The thymus (behind the sternum) secretes thymosin, maturing T-lymphocytes; it is largest in childhood and shrinks after puberty, so cell-mediated immunity is literally built young. Both glands prove small size never means small importance.

Topic in Depth · Pineal

The pinecone pineal secretes melatonin in darkness - timing sleep onset, puberty gating and seasonal rhythms - with light suppressing it (screens delay sleep biochemically, not just behaviourally). The thymus behind the sternum secretes thymosin maturing T-lymphocytes, largest in childhood and shrinking after puberty - cell-mediated immunity is literally built young. Both glands prove endocrine importance needs no size: milligrams of tissue run body-wide programs.
NEET Snapshot:
  • Melatonin: darkness signal for sleep and rhythms.
  • Thymosin: childhood T-cell schooling; thymus shrinks after puberty.
NEET Practice MCQ
1

Melatonin secretion rises mainly

Solution: Darkness triggers pineal melatonin - the chemical basis of sleep hygiene advice.
Back to Index
Index / Human Physiology
Chapter 9 · Topic 10

Testes

Chemical Coordination and IntegrationHuman PhysiologyStep-by-step Topic
Testes (Leydig/interstitial cells) secrete testosterone, driving male secondary characters - facial hair, deep voice, muscle mass, spermatogenesis itself - plus aggression-linked behaviour. Secretion starts at puberty under pituitary LH (ICSH), with FSH + androgen-binding protein from Sertoli cells nursing sperm production. Falling testosterone with age (andropause) is gradual, unlike the sharp female menopause.

Topic in Depth · Testes

Testicular Leydig cells secrete testosterone driving male puberty and upkeep - facial hair, deep voice, muscle mass, spermatogenesis itself, plus libido - under pituitary LH, with FSH plus Sertoli factors nursing sperm production. Testosterone also fuses epiphyseal plates (ending height growth) and maintains bone density lifelong; gradual age-related decline (andropause) contrasts the sharp female menopause. Anabolic misuse builds muscle at the cost of shrunken testes and heart risk.
NEET Snapshot:
  • Testosterone: secondary characters plus sperm production plus bone.
  • LH drives Leydig cells; FSH plus Sertoli factors drive sperm line.
NEET Practice MCQ
1

Leydig cells secrete

Solution: Interstitial Leydig cells make testosterone under pituitary LH command.

Index / Human Physiology
Chapter 9 · Topic 11

Ovaries

Chemical Coordination and IntegrationHuman PhysiologyStep-by-step Topic
Ovaries secrete estrogen (female secondary characters, endometrium rebuilding, bone protection) and progesterone (pregnancy maintenance, secretory endometrium). The developing follicle makes estrogen; the post-ovulation corpus luteum makes progesterone - so the cycle hormones come from structures the cycle itself creates. At menopause (~50 years) follicles exhaust, estrogen crashes, and bone-loss plus hot flashes follow.

Topic in Depth · Ovaries

Ovaries run a two-hormone cycle: developing follicles secrete estrogen (female characters, endometrium rebuilding, bone protection, LH-surge trigger), then the post-ovulation corpus luteum secretes progesterone (secretory endometrium, pregnancy maintenance, breast preparation). Fertilisation rescues the corpus luteum via hCG; otherwise it dies and menstruation follows. Menopause (~50, follicle exhaustion, estrogen crash) brings hot flashes plus bone loss - treatable with managed replacement.
NEET Snapshot:
  • Estrogen builds; progesterone maintains pregnancy.
  • Follicle makes estrogen; corpus luteum makes progesterone.
Remember
  • hCG rescue of corpus luteum is what pregnancy tests detect.
NEET Practice MCQ
1

Corpus luteum secretes mainly

Solution: Luteal progesterone maintains the secretory endometrium awaiting implantation.

Index / Human Physiology
Chapter 9 · Topic 12

Hormonal regulation

Chemical Coordination and IntegrationHuman PhysiologyStep-by-step Topic
Hormonal regulation runs on three signal types: neural (sympathetic triggers adrenal medulla in seconds), hormonal (pituitary trophic hormones command downstream glands), and humoral (blood glucose directly triggers insulin/glucagon). Response speed follows the same order - adrenaline acts in seconds, insulin in minutes, thyroid hormone over days. The gut hormones (gastrin, secretin, CCK, GIP) extend regulation beyond classic glands into digestion itself.

Topic in Depth · Hormonal regulation

Hormonal control runs three signal speeds: neural (sympathetic fires adrenal medulla in seconds), hormonal (pituitary trophic hormones command glands over minutes-hours), humoral (blood glucose directly triggers islet hormones). Response duration follows chemistry - amines flash, peptides pulse, steroids persist for days. Gut hormones (gastrin, secretin, CCK, GIP) extend regulation into digestion, and heart ANF plus kidney erythropoietin prove endocrine tissue hides in working organs.
NEET Snapshot:
  • Neural seconds, hormonal minutes-hours, humoral direct sensing.
  • Duration follows chemistry: amines flash, steroids persist.
NEET Practice MCQ
1

Fastest hormonal response comes from the

Solution: Neurally-triggered adrenaline acts in seconds; steroid responses take hours to days.

Index / Human Physiology
Chapter 9 · Topic 13

Feedback mechanisms

Chemical Coordination and IntegrationHuman PhysiologyStep-by-step Topic
Negative feedback dominates: end-hormone suppresses its own command chain (T3/T4 brake TRH + TSH; cortisol brakes CRH + ACTH; testosterone brakes GnRH + LH). Breaks anywhere invert the pattern - thyroid removal raises TSH; pituitary removal shrinks all downstream glands. Positive feedback is limited to two events: estrogen triggering the LH surge for ovulation, and oxytocin amplifying labour contractions until delivery ends the loop.

Topic in Depth · Feedback mechanisms

Negative feedback dominates endocrinology: end-hormone suppresses its command chain (T3/T4 brake TRH plus TSH; cortisol brakes CRH plus ACTH; testosterone brakes GnRH plus LH), holding levels in tight bands like a thermostat. Breaks invert patterns predictably - thyroid removal raises TSH; pituitary loss shrinks downstream glands. Positive feedback is restricted to two explosive events: estrogen triggering the LH surge (ovulation) and oxytocin amplifying labour contractions until delivery ends the loop.
NEET Snapshot:
  • Negative feedback: end product brakes its bosses - the universal pattern.
  • Positive feedback only: LH surge and labour - both end in an event.
Feedback reading
PointDetail
High TSH + low T4Primary thyroid failure
Low TSH + low T4Pituitary failure
High end-hormone + low trophicNormal negative feedback working
NEET Practice MCQ
1

After thyroid removal, blood TSH level will

Solution: Lost T3/T4 removes the brake, so the pituitary shouts louder - classic feedback reading.

Chapter 9 · Topic 14

Hormonal disorders

Chemical Coordination and IntegrationHuman PhysiologyStep-by-step Topic
Disorder map: dwarfism/gigantism/acromegaly (GH timing vs plates), cretinism/myxoedema/goitre/Graves (thyroid axis), Addison/Cushing (adrenal cortex), diabetes mellitus (insulin lack) vs diabetes insipidus (ADH lack) - same polyuria symptom, opposite hormones. Diagnosis follows the feedback logic: high TSH + low T4 means primary thyroid failure; low TSH + low T4 means pituitary failure - the two patterns examiners love to swap.
Common mistakes: myxoedema (adult hypothyroidism) vs exophthalmic goitre (hyperthyroidism) - opposite ends, often confused.

Topic in Depth · Hormonal disorders

Endocrine disorders split by axis and direction: GH timing (dwarfism, gigantism, acromegaly), thyroid (cretinism, myxoedema, goitre, Graves, Hashimoto), adrenal (Addison failure vs Cushing excess), pancreas (mellitus vs insipidus - same polyuria, opposite hormones), calcium (tetany vs stones). Diagnosis follows feedback logic plus timing - childhood versus adult onset changes the same hormone into different diseases (GH excess: gigantism before plate fusion, acromegaly after).
NEET Snapshot:
  • Same hormone, different age = different disease (GH, thyroid).
  • Mellitus (insulin) vs insipidus (ADH) - compare the pair.
Remember
  • Myxoedema is hypo, Graves is hyper - opposite ends, often swapped.
NEET Practice MCQ
1

Acromegaly differs from gigantism in occurring

Solution: Fused long-bone plates restrict adult GH excess to jaw, hands and feet.

Human Reproduction

Gametogenesis, menstrual cycle, fertilisation, pregnancy and lactation.

Human ReproductionReproductionChapter Introduction

Subtopics in this Chapter

Deep Dive · Gametogenesis and the menstrual cycle

Spermatogenesis vs oogenesis

Menstrual cycle as a hormone story: days 1-5, low hormones shed the endometrium (menstruation). FSH then grows follicles; rising estrogen rebuilds the endometrium (follicular phase). The estrogen peak triggers the LH surge around day 14 - ovulation. The ruptured follicle becomes the corpus luteum, secreting progesterone that maintains the endometrium (luteal phase). Without fertilisation the corpus luteum dies, hormones crash, and the cycle restarts. After implantation the trophoblast secretes hCG, rescuing the corpus luteum - which is exactly what pregnancy tests detect.

Germ layers from gastrulation

  • Morula (8-16 cells) to blastocyst (trophoblast + inner cell mass); implantation around day 7; gestation 280 days.

Chapter 10 · Topic 1

Male reproductive system

Human ReproductionReproductionStep-by-step Topic
Testes (in the scrotum, 2–2.5°C cooler for sperm formation) contain seminiferous tubules where Sertoli cells nourish developing sperm and interstitial Leydig cells secrete testosterone. Sperm path: seminiferous tubules → rete testis → vasa efferentia → epididymis (maturation/storage) → vas deferens → ejaculatory duct → urethra. Accessory glands: seminal vesicles (fructose-rich fluid), prostate (alkaline fluid), Bulbourethral/Cowper's glands (lubrication) — together forming semen.
Fig: Sperm — acrosome enzymes digest egg coverings, mitochondria power the tail, haploid nucleus carries paternal DNA.

Deeper Understanding · Semen is mostly gland fluid

Mature sperm are a tiny fraction of semen volume: seminal vesicles contribute fructose-rich fluid (sperm fuel), the prostate adds alkaline fluid that neutralises vaginal acidity, and Cowper glands lubricate the urethra. The scrotum holds testes 2-2.5 degC below body temperature because sperm enzymes misfold at core heat - undescended testes (cryptorchidism) cause sterility unless corrected early. Sertoli cells both nourish germ cells and form the blood-testis barrier, hiding sperm (genetically foreign, haploid) from the immune system.

Related Diagram · Male reproductive tract

Fig: Sperm path: tubules to epididymis to vas deferens, joined by gland fluids at ejaculation.

Topic in Depth · Male reproductive system

Male anatomy is a cooled production-plus-delivery line: scrotal testes (2-2.5 degrees cooler for sperm enzymes) house seminiferous tubules where Sertoli cells nurse germ cells behind the blood-testis barrier and Leydig cells secrete testosterone; sperm mature and store in the epididymis, travel vasa deferentia, and exit through ejaculatory ducts plus urethra mixed with seminal-vesicle fructose fluid, prostatic alkaline fluid and Cowper lubrication - together semen. Accessory glands contribute volume; testes contribute cells plus hormones.
NEET Snapshot:
  • Cool scrotum, Sertoli nursing, Leydig testosterone, epididymis maturation.
  • Semen is mostly gland fluid; sperm are a small fraction.
NEET Practice MCQ
1

Sperms attain motility and maturation in the

Solution: Epididymal transit matures sperm and stores them until ejaculation.

Index / Reproduction
Chapter 10 · Topic 2

Female reproductive system

Human ReproductionReproductionStep-by-step Topic
Ovaries (produce ova + estrogen/progesterone) → fallopian tubes (fimbriae catch the ovum; ampulla is the fertilisation site) → uterus (endometrium, myometrium) → cervix → vagina. External genitalia + mammary glands (milk production after delivery) complete the system.
NEET facts:
  • Fertilisation occurs in the ampulla, never in the uterus.
  • The acrosome reaction + cortical reaction block polyspermy (only one sperm enters).

Deeper Understanding · Three uterine layers, three jobs

The uterus wall is a sandwich: outer perimetrium (protective covering), middle myometrium (powerful smooth muscle that performs labour contractions), inner endometrium (the layer that builds, receives the blastocyst, and sheds in menstruation). The cervix stays plugged with mucus except at ovulation (sperm entry) and labour (baby exit). Fimbriae create gentle currents sweeping the ovulated egg into the tube - ectopic pregnancy usually means a fertilised egg got stuck in this transit.

Related Diagram · Female reproductive tract

Fig: Eggs travel tubes to uterus; fertilisation happens in transit, implantation at destination.

Topic in Depth · Female reproductive system

Female anatomy centres the uterus: ovaries make ova plus estrogen-progesterone, fimbriae sweep ovulated eggs into fallopian tubes (ampulla = fertilisation site), uterus (perimetrium-myometrium-endometrium) receives the blastocyst, cervix gates entry, vagina plus external genitalia complete the tract, mammary glands await delivery orders. Uterine myometrium performs labour contractions; endometrium builds and sheds cyclically. Fimbriae currents plus tubal cilia - not swimming alone - deliver the egg.
NEET Snapshot:
  • Ovary makes, tube transports and hosts fertilisation, uterus gestates.
  • Ampulla fertilises; endometrium implants; myometrium labours.
Tube segments
PointDetail
FimbriaeCatch the ovulated egg
AmpullaFertilisation site
IsthmusTransport to uterus
UterusImplantation and gestation

Fertilisation in humans occurs in the

Solution: Sperm meet the ovum in the wide ampulla; the blastocyst later implants in the uterus.

Chapter 10 · Topic 3

Spermatogenesis

Human ReproductionReproductionStep-by-step Topic
Spermatogenesis runs continuously from puberty inside seminiferous tubules: spermatogonia (2n) divide by mitosis, one daughter becomes a primary spermatocyte - meiosis I gives 2 secondary spermatocytes, meiosis II gives 4 spermatids - then spermiogenesis reshapes them (acrosome, flagellum, shed cytoplasm) into sperm that mature further in the epididymis. Sertoli cells nurse every step; FSH + testosterone drive the line. One ejaculation releases 200-300 million sperm for odds of one.

Topic in Depth · Spermatogenesis

Spermatogenesis runs continuously from puberty: spermatogonia divide by mitosis (one daughter stays stem, one differentiates), primary spermatocytes complete meiosis I into two secondary spermatocytes, meiosis II yields four spermatids, then spermiogenesis remodels them (acrosome cap, flagellum, shed cytoplasm, condensed nucleus) into spermatozoa that finish maturing in the epididymis. FSH plus testosterone drive the line; Sertoli cells nurse every generation. Output runs hundreds of millions daily - quantity compensating a hazardous journey.
NEET Snapshot:
  • Mitosis then two meiotic divisions then remodelling = 4 sperm per spermatogonium.
  • Spermiogenesis shapes; epididymis matures.
NEET Practice MCQ
1

Spermiogenesis is the transformation of

Solution: Meiosis makes spermatids; differentiation (no division) sculpts them into sperm.

Index / Reproduction
Chapter 10 · Topic 4

Oogenesis

Human ReproductionReproductionStep-by-step Topic
Spermatogenesis (starts at puberty, continuous): spermatogonia (2n) → primary spermatocyte → meiosis I → 2 secondary spermatocytes → meiosis II → 4 spermatids → spermiogenesis (differentiation into sperm). Oogenesis (starts before birth): oogonia → primary oocytes arrested in prophase I → completes meiosis I just before ovulation (secondary oocyte arrested in metaphase II + first polar body) → meiosis II finishes only after sperm entry. One primary oocyte yields one ovum + polar bodies.
Common mistakes: the second polar body forms after fertilisation begins, not at ovulation.

Deeper Understanding · Why polar bodies exist

Oogenesis deliberately throws away cytoplasm: each division keeps almost everything in one daughter cell so the ovum stays giant (yolk + mitochondria + mRNAs for early embryo), discarding the rest as tiny polar bodies that degenerate. The first polar body forms at ovulation (meiosis I complete), the second only after a sperm enters - so an ovulated egg is technically still arrested in metaphase II, and fertilisation is what finishes maternal meiosis. Male meiosis wastes nothing because sperm need only DNA plus a motor.

Topic in Depth · Oogenesis

Oogenesis front-loads then pauses twice: oogonia multiply before birth, primary oocytes arrest in prophase I until puberty, complete meiosis I just before ovulation (secondary oocyte arrested in metaphase II plus first polar body), and finish meiosis II only after sperm entry (second polar body extruded). One primary oocyte yields one ovum plus discarded polar bodies - hoarding cytoplasm (yolk, mitochondria, maternal mRNAs) for the early embryo. Menopause ends the finite follicle stock around age 50.
NEET Snapshot:
  • Arrested prophase I till puberty, metaphase II till fertilisation.
  • One ovum per oocyte; the rest is discarded polar bodies.
Remember
  • Second polar body forms after sperm entry - not at ovulation.
NEET Practice MCQ
1

Second meiotic division in oogenesis completes

Solution: The ovulated egg waits in metaphase II; fertilisation triggers its completion.
Back to Index
Index / Reproduction
Chapter 10 · Topic 5

Menstrual cycle

Human ReproductionReproductionStep-by-step Topic
28-day cycle: menstrual phase (days 1–5) — endometrium sheds; follicular/proliferative phase — FSH grows follicles, rising estrogen rebuilds the endometrium; ovulation (~day 14) triggered by the LH surge; luteal/secretory phase — corpus luteum secretes progesterone maintaining the endometrium. Without fertilisation the corpus luteum degenerates and the cycle restarts. Menarche (first menstruation) vs menopause (~50 years, cycle stops).

In a 28-day cycle, when does ovulation occur and which hormone surge causes it?

Answer: Around day 14, caused by a sharp LH (luteinising hormone) surge from the anterior pituitary.

Deeper Understanding · Reading the cycle for contraception

The rhythm method avoids days ~10-17 (ovulation window + sperm survival 3 days + egg survival 1 day), but irregular cycles make it unreliable. Hormonal pills work by faking the luteal phase: steady progesterone-estrogen convinces the pituitary that ovulation already happened, so no LH surge occurs. Emergency pills (levonorgestrel) delay ovulation and disturb implantation but cannot abort an implanted pregnancy - a precise distinction NEET tests. Lactational amenorrhea works only with exclusive breastfeeding and within 6 months of delivery.

Topic in Depth · Menstrual cycle

The 28-day cycle is a hormone relay: menstrual phase (days 1-5, hormone crash sheds endometrium), follicular phase (FSH grows follicles, rising estrogen rebuilds lining), ovulation (~day 14, estrogen-triggered LH surge ruptures the follicle), luteal phase (corpus luteum progesterone maintains secretory lining). Without fertilisation the corpus luteum dies and the cycle restarts; with it, hCG rescues progesterone and pregnancy continues. Menarche starts it; menopause (~50) ends the follicle supply.
NEET Snapshot:
  • Bleed (low hormones), rebuild (estrogen), ovulate (LH surge), maintain (progesterone).
  • Day 14 ovulation; hCG rescue decides pregnancy versus periods.
NEET Practice MCQ
1

LH surge on about day 14 causes

Solution: The sharp luteinising-hormone peak ruptures the mature Graafian follicle.
Back to Index
Index / Reproduction
Chapter 10 · Topic 6

Ovulation

Human ReproductionReproductionStep-by-step Topic
Ovulation (~day 14) is triggered by the LH surge: the mature Graafian follicle ruptures, releasing the secondary oocyte (arrested in metaphase II) into the fimbriae funnel. Mittelschmerz (mid-cycle pain) and a slight basal-temperature rise mark it. The ruptured follicle becomes the corpus luteum secreting progesterone; fertilisation rescues it via hCG, otherwise it degenerates into corpus albicans and menstruation follows.

Topic in Depth · Ovulation

Ovulation releases the metaphase-II secondary oocyte when the LH surge ruptures the Graafian follicle wall - aided by prostaglandins and pressure - into fimbriae currents. Mittelschmerz (mid-cycle pain) plus basal-temperature rise plus cervical-mucus thinning mark it. The ruptured follicle becomes the progesterone-secreting corpus luteum; unruptured follicles become atretic. Anovulatory cycles (no egg, still bleeding) are common at menarche and perimenopause.
NEET Snapshot:
  • LH surge ruptures follicle; fimbriae catch the egg.
  • Ruptured follicle becomes corpus luteum - ovulation funds the luteal phase.
Remember
  • Pain plus temperature plus mucus change triangulate ovulation day.
NEET Practice MCQ
1

Ovulated egg is surrounded by

Solution: Zona plus follicular corona travel with the egg until sperm digest through.
Back to Index
Index / Reproduction
Chapter 10 · Topic 7

Fertilisation

Human ReproductionReproductionStep-by-step Topic
Fertilisation in the ampulla: capacitated sperm digest the corona radiata (hyaluronidase) and zona pellucida (acrosome reaction); one sperm fuses - the cortical reaction hardens the zona, permanently blocking polyspermy. Entry triggers completion of maternal meiosis II (second polar body extruded) and male/female pronuclei fuse (syngamy), restoring diploidy and fixing sex (sperm X or Y). The zygote starts cleaving during its 3-4 day tube journey.

Topic in Depth · Fertilisation

Fertilisation is a molecular obstacle course in the ampulla: capacitation primes sperm, hyaluronidase disperses corona cells, acrosome enzymes digest the zona, one sperm fuses - triggering the cortical reaction that hardens the zona against polyspermy - then pronuclei fuse (syngamy), restoring diploidy and fixing sex (sperm X or Y decides). Maternal meiosis II completes on entry. Millions start, dozens arrive, one fuses - with blocks at every step preventing multi-sperm disasters.
NEET Snapshot:
  • Capacitation, corona, acrosome, fusion, cortical block, syngamy.
  • Zona hardening after first fusion prevents polyspermy.
NEET Practice MCQ
1

Polyspermy is prevented in humans mainly by

Solution: First fusion triggers cortical granules that make the zona impenetrable to latecomers.
Back to Index
Index / Reproduction
Chapter 10 · Topic 8

Implantation

Human ReproductionReproductionStep-by-step Topic
Implantation (~day 7): the blastocyst hatches from the zona, and trophoblast cells invade the endometrium (now secretory under progesterone), digesting a nest with lytic enzymes. The trophoblast secretes hCG, rescuing the corpus luteum so progesterone continues - no hCG, no maintained pregnancy. Implantation bleeding (slight spotting) can mimic a light period. Ectopic implantation (usually tubal) cannot survive and ruptures dangerously.

Topic in Depth · Implantation

Implantation (~day 7) embeds the hatched blastocyst: trophoblast cells digest into the secretory endometrium using lytic enzymes, anchoring villi that will become the placenta, while the inner cell mass commits to the embryo. Trophoblast hCG rescues the corpus luteum - without it progesterone crashes and the lining sheds. Implantation bleeding (light spotting) can mimic a period; ectopic tubal implantation ruptures dangerously instead.
NEET Snapshot:
  • Blastocyst hatches, trophoblast invades, hCG rescues the corpus luteum.
  • Day-7 uterine embedding; tubal embedding is ectopic emergency.
NEET Practice MCQ
1

Implantation in humans occurs about

Solution: The week-old blastocyst embeds in secretory endometrium around day 7.
Back to Index
Index / Reproduction
Chapter 10 · Topic 9

Pregnancy

Human ReproductionReproductionStep-by-step Topic
Pregnancy (~280 days/40 weeks) divides into trimesters: first (organogenesis - most vulnerable to teratogens like alcohol and drugs), second (growth + detectable heartbeat/movements), third (rapid weight gain, lung maturation). hCG peaks early then falls as the placenta takes over progesterone/estrogen production by week 12. Maternal blood volume rises ~50%; the foetal heart (~140/min) and umbilical flow are monitored throughout.

Topic in Depth · Pregnancy

Pregnancy (~280 days/40 weeks from last period) divides into trimesters: first (organogenesis - most teratogen-vulnerable; alcohol, drugs, rubella injure forming organs), second (growth, detectable heartbeat and movements, anomaly scans), third (rapid weight gain, lung maturation, head-down positioning). hCG peaks early then yields to placental progesterone-estrogen by week 12; maternal blood volume rises ~50 percent; foetal heart (~140/min) plus growth scans monitor progress.
NEET Snapshot:
  • First builds organs (protect it), second grows, third gains weight.
  • hCG early, placenta takes over hormones by week 12.
Remember
  • Teratogen danger peaks in weeks 3-8 when organs form.
NEET Practice MCQ
1

Gestation period in humans averages

Solution: Forty weeks from last menstrual period - 38 weeks from fertilisation.
Back to Index
Index / Reproduction
Chapter 10 · Topic 10

Embryonic development

Human ReproductionReproductionStep-by-step Topic
Capacitated sperm meet the ovum in the ampulla; syngamy restores diploidy. Cleavage → morula (8–16 cells) → blastocyst (outer trophoblast + inner cell mass) which implants in the uterus around day 7. Trophoblast secretes hCG (pregnancy-test hormone) rescuing the corpus luteum. Embryonic development: gastrulation forms three germ layers — ectoderm (skin, nervous system), mesoderm (muscles, bones, blood, heart), endoderm (gut lining, liver, lungs). Organogenesis completes by week 12; gestation ≈ 280 days / 40 weeks.

Deeper Understanding · Twins and hCG doubling

Monozygotic (identical) twins split from one zygote (same sex, same genes); dizygotic (fraternal) twins come from two eggs + two sperms (can differ like siblings). Blood hCG roughly doubles every 48 hours in early healthy pregnancy - slow rises flag ectopic or failing pregnancies, the clinical use of the pregnancy hormone. The placenta is a selective barrier, not absolute: nutrients, gases, alcohol, many drugs and some viruses cross, but maternal and foetal blood never mix (exchange across membranes only).

Topic in Depth · Embryonic development

Development layers complexity: cleavage (rapid mitoses, morula 8-16 cells), blastocyst (trophoblast shell plus inner cell mass), gastrulation (three germ layers - ectoderm for skin-nerves, mesoderm for muscle-bone-blood-heart, endoderm for gut-liver-lungs), neurulation (neural tube plus notochord), organogenesis (heart beats week 4, limbs bud, face forms by week 12). Each stage has critical windows - thalidomide shortened limbs because it struck limb-bud weeks precisely.
NEET Snapshot:
  • Morula to blastocyst to gastrula to neurula to foetus.
  • Ectoderm covers, mesoderm moves, endoderm digests - layer fates.
Layer gifts
PointDetail
EctodermSkin, nervous system, lens
MesodermMuscle, bone, blood, heart, kidneys, gonads
EndodermGut lining, liver, pancreas, lungs
NEET Practice MCQ
1

Heart develops from which germ layer?

Solution: Mesoderm builds muscle, blood, vessels and heart - the movement middle layer.
Back to Index
Index / Reproduction
Chapter 10 · Topic 11

Placenta

Human ReproductionReproductionStep-by-step Topic
The placenta (chorionic villi + uterine tissue) is foetus lifeline and endocrine gland: transports O2/nutrients in and CO2/wastes out, and secretes hCG, estrogen, progesterone and relaxin. Maternal and foetal blood never mix - exchange crosses membranes only - yet alcohol, drugs, many medicines and some viruses cross freely (no barrier against chemistry). The umbilical cord carries two arteries (foetus to placenta) + one vein (placenta to foetus).

Topic in Depth · Placenta

The placenta (chorionic villi bathed in maternal blood sinuses plus uterine decidua) multitasks: transports O2-nutrients in and CO2-wastes out, secretes hCG-estrogen-progesterone-relaxin, and immunologically shields the half-foreign foetus. Maternal and foetal blood never mix - exchange crosses membranes only - yet alcohol, drugs, nicotine and many viruses cross freely (no barrier against chemistry). Umbilical cord carries two arteries (foetus to placenta) plus one vein (placenta to foetus).
NEET Snapshot:
  • Exchange plus hormones plus immune shield - triple organ.
  • Two arteries out, one vein in (from foetal view).
NEET Practice MCQ
1

Umbilical cord contains

Solution: Paired arteries carry foetal waste out; single vein brings oxygenated blood in.
Back to Index
Index / Reproduction
Chapter 10 · Topic 12

Parturition

Human ReproductionReproductionStep-by-step Topic
Placenta (chorionic villi + uterine tissue) supplies O₂/nutrients, removes wastes, and secretes hCG, progesterone, estrogen and relaxin — but is permeable to alcohol, drugs and some pathogens. Parturition (labour): oxytocin drives uterine contractions + relaxin loosens pelvic ligaments. Lactation: prolactin produces milk, oxytocin ejects it; first milk colostrum is rich in IgA antibodies for the newborn.
NEET facts:
  • Pregnancy test detects hCG in urine.
  • Umbilical cord has two arteries + one vein (vein carries oxygenated blood to the foetus).

Deeper Understanding · Two hormones, one feeding

Prolactin makes milk (lactation needs high prolactin + low estrogen after delivery) while oxytocin ejects it (let-down reflex triggered by suckling - a neuroendocrine loop: nipple nerves to hypothalamus to posterior pituitary). Colostrum, the first yellow milk, is low in fat but packed with IgA antibodies - the newborn gut gets passive immunity before its own system wakes. Stress and pain inhibit oxytocin, which is why calm surroundings genuinely improve breastfeeding success.

Topic in Depth · Parturition

Labour is a positive-feedback cascade: mature foetus plus falling progesterone trigger oxytocin pulses, contracting myometrium against a softening cervix (relaxin loosens pelvic ligaments), each contraction squeezing more oxytocin until delivery - dilation, expulsion, then placental afterbirth stages. Foetal cortisol initiates the sequence (lung-maturity signal); epidural anaesthesia blocks pain but can slow pushing; Caesarean bypasses the canal when risks mount. Oxytocin (Pitocin) drips induce or augment medically.
NEET Snapshot:
  • Foetal signal starts, oxytocin amplifies, cervix yields, baby then placenta.
  • Positive feedback ends only with delivery emptying the uterus.
Remember
  • Relaxin softens ligaments; oxytocin hardens contractions - complementary duo.
NEET Practice MCQ
1

Parturition is induced by

Solution: Withdrawing progesterone plus rising oxytocin flips the uterus from maintenance to expulsion.
Back to Index
Index / Reproduction
Chapter 10 · Topic 13

Lactation

Human ReproductionReproductionStep-by-step Topic
Lactation needs two hormones: prolactin manufactures milk in alveoli after delivery (high prolactin + crashed estrogen/progesterone switch production on), and oxytocin ejects it via myoepithelial contraction (let-down reflex on suckling). First milk colostrum is low-fat but rich in IgA antibodies - the newborn first immunisation. Prolactin simultaneously suppresses GnRH, spacing pregnancies during exclusive breastfeeding.

Topic in Depth · Lactation

Lactation splits manufacture from delivery: prolactin (high after delivery as estrogen-progesterone crash) makes milk in alveoli; oxytocin ejects it via myoepithelial squeeze on suckling (let-down reflex - a neuroendocrine loop from nipple nerves to hypothalamus to posterior pituitary). Colostrum (first yellow milk) delivers concentrated IgA antibodies - the newborn first immunisation. Prolactin simultaneously suppresses GnRH, spacing pregnancies during exclusive breastfeeding; stress and pain inhibit oxytocin, so calm surroundings genuinely improve feeding.
NEET Snapshot:
  • Prolactin makes; oxytocin ejects; colostrum immunises.
  • Suckling drives supply - demand sets production volume.
NEET Practice MCQ
1

Milk ejection (let-down) is caused by

Solution: Oxytocin contracts myoepithelial cells; prolactin handles manufacture upstream.

Chapter 11 · Step-by-step

Reproductive Health

Population, every contraceptive method, MTP, STIs and ART.

Reproductive HealthReproductionChapter Introduction

Subtopics in this Chapter

1. Reproductive health2. Population explosion3. Contraception4. Natural methods5. Barrier methods6. IUDs7. Hormonal methods8. Surgical methods9. MTP10. STIs11. Infertility12. IVF13. ZIFT14. GIFT

Deep Dive · Contraception and ART decoded

How each method actually blocks pregnancy: natural methods (withdrawal, rhythm, lactational amenorrhea) simply avoid sperm-egg meeting but fail often. Barrier methods (condoms, diaphragm, vaults) physically block entry - condoms alone also block STIs. IUDs (CuT, Multiload 375) release Cu2+ ions that suppress sperm motility and fertilising capacity inside the uterus. Oral pills (daily progesterone-estrogen combos, or weekly non-steroidal Saheli) suppress ovulation itself. Surgical methods (vasectomy in the vas deferens, tubectomy in fallopian tubes) block gamete transport permanently - but hormones, desire and secondary characters remain completely normal, a favourite assertion-reason trap.
ART one-liners (never interchange)
TechniqueWhat happens where
IVF-ET (test-tube baby)Fertilisation in lab; embryo transferred to uterus
ZIFTZygote transferred into fallopian tube
GIFTUnfertilised gametes placed in tube; fertilisation inside body
ICSISingle sperm injected directly into ovum
AI / IUISemen artificially introduced into uterus
Fig: Sterilisation cuts the transport tubes only - sperm/egg production and hormones continue normally.
