Chapter 11 · Topic 1

Reproductive health

Reproductive HealthReproductionStep-by-step Topic
India launched family planning in 1951; Reproductive and Child Health (RCH) programmes spread awareness on reproduction, STDs, contraception and childcare. A rapid population rise ("population explosion") strains resources — slogans like "Hum Do Hamare Do" promote the small-family norm. Sex determination and female foeticide are banned; amniocentesis legally restricted to detecting genetic disorders, not foetal sex.
NEET facts:
  • Amniocentesis analyses foetal cells from amniotic fluid for chromosomal defects (Down syndrome etc.).
  • Statutory marriage ages: 18 (female), 21 (male).

Deeper Understanding · RCH goals in numbers

Reproductive and Child Health programmes target measurable outcomes: lower IMR (infant mortality) and MMR (maternal mortality), wider immunisation, and smaller family norms. India population crossed 1.4 billion because death rate fell faster than birth rate (population momentum - even replacement fertility keeps growth going for decades). Legal guardrails: amniocentesis only for genetic defects (sex detection banned under PCPNDT), marriage age 18/21, MTP Act for regulated abortion. "Hum Do Hamare Do" promotes replacement-level fertility (~2.1 children).

Topic in Depth · Reproductive health

Reproductive health means physically plus socially healthy reproductive life - safe sex, informed contraception, STI prevention, maternal care and infertility help - delivered through RCH programmes (awareness, immunisation, safe deliveries, childcare). India family planning began in 1951 (world first); RCH widened it from population control to total care. Healthy timing (adult marriage ages 18/21), birth spacing and institutional deliveries cut maternal and infant deaths dramatically.
NEET Snapshot:
  • RCH covers awareness, contraception, STI care, maternal plus child health.
  • Family planning since 1951; legal marriage ages 18 (female) / 21 (male).
NEET Practice MCQ
1

RCH in India stands for

Solution: Reproductive and Child Health programmes bundle family welfare with maternal-child care.

Chapter 11 · Topic 2

Population explosion

Reproductive HealthReproductionStep-by-step Topic
Population explosion means death rate crashing (medicine, sanitation) while birth rate stays high - India crossed 1.4 billion on this gap. Consequences: resource strain, unemployment, slums, pressure on health and education systems. Control levers: small-family norms ("Hum Do Hamare Do"), delayed marriage, female literacy (strongest fertility suppressor statistically), accessible contraception, and RCH services - slogans work only when clinics do.

Topic in Depth · Population explosion

Population explodes when death rates crash (medicine, sanitation, food) while birth rates stay high - growth momentum continues decades even after fertility falls to replacement (~2.1 children), because huge young cohorts keep entering reproductive age. Consequences strain every system: jobs, housing, water, schools, hospitals. Control needs female literacy plus late marriage plus accessible contraception plus RCH services - slogans alone never moved any rate.
NEET Snapshot:
  • Death-rate fall minus birth-rate lag equals explosion.
  • Replacement fertility ~2.1; momentum sustains growth for decades.
Remember
  • Female literacy is statistically the strongest fertility suppressor.
NEET Practice MCQ
1

Population explosion is caused mainly by

Solution: Medicine cut deaths faster than norms cut births - the gap is the explosion.

Chapter 11 · Topic 3

Contraception

Reproductive HealthReproductionStep-by-step Topic
MethodExamplesHow it works
NaturalWithdrawal, rhythm/calendar, lactational amenorrheaAvoid fertilisation; no devices, higher failure rate
BarrierCondoms, diaphragm, cervical caps, vaultsBlock sperm entry; condoms also prevent STIs
IUDsCuT, Cu7, Multiload 375, LNG-20Cu²⁺ ions suppress sperm motility/fertilising capacity
Oral pillsMala-D, Saheli (weekly, non-steroidal)Progesterone ± estrogen suppress ovulation
SurgicalVasectomy (vas deferens), Tubectomy (fallopian tubes)Permanent; blocks gamete transport only
Common mistakes: vasectomy/tubectomy block gamete transport, not hormone production — desire and characteristics remain normal.

Deeper Understanding · Copper-T mechanism

CuT releases Cu2+ ions that are directly toxic to sperm - reducing motility and fertilising capacity - while the device itself provokes a mild sterile inflammatory reaction in the uterus hostile to implantation. It contains no hormones, works 5-10 years, and is reversible on removal. Hormone-releasing IUDs (LNG-20/Mirena) instead thicken cervical mucus and thin the endometrium. Neither IUD protects against STIs - only condoms do double duty, which is why programmes promote them for high-risk groups.

Topic in Depth · Contraception

Contraception blocks pregnancy at five possible gates: avoid gamete meeting (natural, barriers), block fertilising capacity (IUDs), prevent ovulation (hormonal), or permanently close transport (surgical). Ideal method balances effectiveness, reversibility, safety, cost and STI protection - no single method tops all five, which is why counselling matches method to couple (spacing vs limiting, young vs completed family, risk level). Perfect-use versus typical-use failure rates separate lab promise from real life.
NEET Snapshot:
  • Five gates: timing, barrier, uterus, ovulation, tubes/ducts.
  • No method leads on all five ideals - match method to need.
Method ladder
PointDetail
NaturalFree, least reliable
BarrierPlus STI cover
IUDsYears, reversible
HormonalNear-perfect with discipline
SurgicalPermanent, most reliable
NEET Practice MCQ
1

Which contraceptive also protects against STIs?

Solution: Only barrier condoms block pathogen transfer alongside sperm - unique double duty.

Chapter 11 · Topic 4

Natural methods

Reproductive HealthReproductionStep-by-step Topic
Natural methods need no devices but demand discipline: withdrawal (coitus interruptus - pre-ejaculate still carries sperm), rhythm/calendar (abstinence days ~10-17, fails with irregular cycles), lactational amenorrhea (exclusive breastfeeding, under 6 months only). Typical-use failure rates are the highest of all methods - which is exactly why exam questions contrast them with near-perfect IUDs and pills.

Related Diagram · Fertile window chart

Fig: Rhythm method maths: avoid a full week around ovulation - unreliable if cycles shift.

Topic in Depth · Natural methods

Natural methods cost nothing and need maximum discipline: withdrawal (coitus interruptus - pre-ejaculate still carries sperm, timing fails), rhythm/calendar (abstinence days ~10-17 around ovulation - collapses with irregular cycles), lactational amenorrhea (exclusive breastfeeding suppresses ovulation, valid under 6 months only). Typical-use failure runs highest of all methods - examiners contrast them against near-perfect IUDs and pills to test effectiveness ranking.
NEET Snapshot:
  • Withdrawal, rhythm, lactational amenorrhea - no devices, high failure.
  • Lactational cover needs exclusive feeding plus under 6 months.
NEET Practice MCQ
1

Lactational amenorrhea works as contraception only when

Solution: Frequent suckling suppresses ovulation briefly; supplementary feeds or age break the cover.

Chapter 11 · Topic 5

Barrier methods

Reproductive HealthReproductionStep-by-step Topic
Barrier methods physically block sperm meeting egg: male/female condoms, diaphragms, cervical caps and vaults (used with spermicidal jelly). Condoms alone add STI protection - the unique double duty no other method offers. They are cheap, non-hormonal, immediately reversible and have no systemic side effects; breakage/slippage is the main failure mode, fixed by correct use and quality checks.

Topic in Depth · Barrier methods

Barriers physically stop sperm reaching eggs: male and female condoms, diaphragms, cervical caps and vaults (used with spermicidal jelly for backup kill). Condoms alone add STI protection - the unique double duty driving their promotion in high-risk groups. Advantages stack: cheap, non-hormonal, immediately reversible, no systemic side effects. Failures come from breakage, slippage and inconsistent use - technique plus quality checks fix most of it.
NEET Snapshot:
  • Condoms, diaphragms, caps, vaults - physical blocks plus spermicide.
  • Only condoms add STI protection.
Remember
  • Barrier plus spermicide beats barrier alone - backup killing matters.
NEET Practice MCQ
1

Diaphragms and cervical caps are used with

Solution: Spermicide backs the physical barrier with chemical sperm-killing.

Chapter 11 · Topic 6

IUDs

Reproductive HealthReproductionStep-by-step Topic
IUDs sit inside the uterus for years: inert types (Lippes loop) mainly provoke a mild anti-implantation reaction; copper types (CuT, Cu7, Multiload 375) release Cu2+ ions toxic to sperm motility and fertilising capacity; hormone types (LNG-20) thicken cervical mucus and thin the endometrium. Inserted by doctors, effective 5-10 years, fertility returns on removal - ideal spacing method, though not for STI protection.

Related Diagram · Copper-T in place

Fig: A tiny T-shaped device: copper ions suppress sperm, mild reaction blocks implantation.

Topic in Depth · IUDs

IUDs guard the uterus for years: inert loops provoke mild anti-implantation reaction; copper types (CuT, Cu7, Multiload 375) release Cu2+ ions toxic to sperm motility and fertilising capacity; hormone types (LNG-20) thicken cervical mucus plus thin the endometrium. Doctor-inserted, effective 5-10 years, fertility returning on removal - ideal spacing method. They do not protect against STIs and need screening before insertion (infection risk).
NEET Snapshot:
  • Copper poisons sperm; hormone thickens mucus; inert irritates lining.
  • Years-long, reversible, doctor-inserted - best spacers.
NEET Practice MCQ
1

CuT IUD prevents pregnancy mainly by

Solution: Cu2+ toxicity plus local reaction stops fertilisation without touching hormones.

Chapter 11 · Topic 7

Hormonal methods

Reproductive HealthReproductionStep-by-step Topic
Hormonal methods (combined pills, progestin-only minipills, injectables, implants, patches) all converge on suppressing ovulation - steady steroids fake the luteal phase so no LH surge occurs - plus thickened cervical mucus and thinned endometrium as backup. Saheli (ormeloxifene) is special: non-steroidal, once-weekly, mainly anti-implantation with fewer steroidal side effects. Emergency pills (levonorgestrel) delay ovulation post-exposure but cannot disturb an implanted pregnancy.

Topic in Depth · Hormonal methods

Hormonal methods (combined pills, minipills, injectables, implants, patches) converge on suppressing ovulation - steady steroids fake the luteal phase so no LH surge fires - backed by thickened cervical mucus and thinned endometrium. Daily discipline decides effectiveness (missed pills fail); Saheli (ormeloxifene) innovates as non-steroidal weekly cover. Emergency levonorgestrel delays ovulation post-exposure but cannot disturb implanted pregnancy - timing-limited backup, not abortion.
NEET Snapshot:
  • Fake luteal phase - no LH surge, no ovulation.
  • Discipline decides pill success; Saheli goes weekly non-steroidal.
NEET Practice MCQ
1

Saheli contraceptive pill is distinctive because it is

Solution: Ormeloxifene dosed weekly avoids steroidal side effects while preventing implantation.

Chapter 11 · Topic 8

Surgical methods

Reproductive HealthReproductionStep-by-step Topic
Vasectomy (cut/cauterised vasa deferentia through a tiny scrotal nick - now no-scalpel technique) and tubectomy (cut/blocked fallopian tubes, often post-delivery) permanently block gamete transport. Hormones, desire, erection and ejaculate volume (semen minus sperm) stay normal - the most-tested clarification. Reversal surgery exists but success falls with time; couples must treat sterilisation as permanent.

Topic in Depth · Surgical methods

Sterilisation permanently closes gamete transport: vasectomy (cut or sealed vasa deferentia via tiny/no-scalpel scrotal nick) and tubectomy (cut or clipped fallopian tubes, often post-delivery). Hormones, desire, erection and ejaculate volume (semen minus sperm) stay completely normal - the most-tested clarification. Reversal exists but success fades with time; couples must consent as if permanent. Uptake rises with completed families plus incentive programmes.
NEET Snapshot:
  • Vasectomy: vas deferens. Tubectomy: fallopian tubes. Both: transport only.
  • Hormones and desire untouched - sterilisation is plumbing, not chemistry.
Remember
  • Gamete transport blocked; hormone production untouched - the assertion-reason favourite.
NEET Practice MCQ
1

After vasectomy, which remains normal?

Solution: Only sperm transport stops; Leydig testosterone and libido continue unchanged.

Chapter 11 · Topic 9

MTP

Reproductive HealthReproductionStep-by-step Topic
MTP (Medical Termination of Pregnancy) is legal regulated abortion under the MTP Act (1971, amended): permitted for contraceptive failure, rape, foetal abnormalities, or maternal health risk, within specified gestational limits with registered practitioner approval. Amniocentesis is legal only for detecting genetic defects (Down syndrome etc.) - sex determination and female foeticide are criminal under the PCPNDT Act. Safe MTP needs clinics; unsafe abortion remains a major maternal-mortality cause.

Topic in Depth · MTP

MTP (Medical Termination of Pregnancy) is legal regulated abortion under the MTP Act - permitted for contraceptive failure, rape, foetal abnormalities endangering viability, or maternal health risk, within gestational limits with registered practitioner approvals (amended to widen access). Amniocentesis stays legal only for genetic-defect detection; sex determination plus female foeticide are criminal under PCPNDT. Safe MTP needs clinics - unsafe abortion remains a top maternal killer where access fails.
NEET Snapshot:
  • MTP: legal, regulated, reason-bound abortion care.
  • Amniocentesis for defects legal; for sex selection criminal.
NEET Practice MCQ
1

MTP in India is regulated under the

Solution: The MTP Act legalises reason-bound abortion with practitioner approvals and limits.

Index / Reproduction
Chapter 11 · Topic 10

STIs

Reproductive HealthReproductionStep-by-step Topic
Sexually transmitted infections: gonorrhoea, syphilis, chlamydia, trichomoniasis (curable if detected early), plus hepatitis-B, genital herpes and HIV (managed, not cured). HIV/AIDS spreads through unprotected sex, infected blood/needles and placenta — never by touch, hugging or shared food. Diagnosis by ELISA; treatment with anti-retroviral drugs prolongs life but does not cure. Prevention: safe sex, sterile needles, screened blood.

Deeper Understanding · HIV window and ELISA logic

After infection there is a window period (weeks) when the virus exists but antibodies are not yet detectable - blood donated then tests negative yet infects, which is why donor screening plus history matters. ELISA detects anti-HIV antibodies (screening), confirmed by Western blot/PCR. HIV integrates proviral DNA into host CD4 cells and can hide latently for years - the reason anti-retroviral therapy suppresses but never cures. Safe behaviours form a hierarchy: abstinence, mutual monogamy, condoms, sterile needles, screened blood.

Topic in Depth · STIs

STIs split by curability: gonorrhoea, syphilis, chlamydia and trichomoniasis cure if caught early (antibiotics), while herpes, hepatitis-B and HIV manage but never cure (suppression plus vaccination where available). Spread runs through unprotected sex, infected blood/needles and placenta - never touch, hugging or shared food. Condoms plus sterile needles plus screened blood form the prevention triad; early testing plus partner treatment breaks chains.
NEET Snapshot:
  • Curable bacterial (early) vs manageable viral (lifelong).
  • Touch and food never spread STIs - transmission needs fluids or blood.
STI map
PointDetail
Gonorrhoea, syphilisBacterial, curable early
Herpes, HIV, hepatitis-BViral, managed not cured
TrichomoniasisProtozoan, curable
NEET Practice MCQ
1

Which STI is curable if detected early?

Solution: Bacterial syphilis yields to antibiotics; viral STIs only suppress.

Index / Reproduction
Chapter 11 · Topic 11

Infertility

Reproductive HealthReproductionStep-by-step Topic
Infertility (no conception after 2 years of unprotected sex) is treatable: IVF–ET (test-tube baby) — fertilisation outside, embryo transferred to uterus; ZIFT — zygote into fallopian tube; GIFT — gametes into tube; ICSI — single sperm injected into ovum; AI/IUI — artificial insemination. Legal adoption remains an option.

Distinguish IVF, ZIFT and GIFT in one line each.

Answer: IVF: fertilisation in lab, embryo into uterus. ZIFT: zygote transferred into fallopian tube. GIFT: unfertilised gametes placed into the tube for in-body fertilisation.

Deeper Understanding · Choosing the right ART

Match technique to defect: blocked tubes but healthy uterus - IVF-ET; very low sperm count - ICSI (one sperm, one egg, under microscope); tubes healthy but transport failing - ZIFT/GIFT; ejaculation issues only - IUI. Success per cycle is only ~30-40% and falls with maternal age, so multiple cycles are normal. Surplus frozen embryos raise ethical and legal questions (ownership on divorce, posthumous use) that laws now regulate - medicine here is inseparable from ethics, a growing NEET assertion-reason theme.

Topic in Depth · Infertility

Infertility (no conception after 2 years unprotected sex) splits causes across partners - male (low count/motility, blockages), female (ovulation failure, tubal blockage, uterine issues), combined or unexplained - so both partners test before treatment ladders climb: lifestyle plus ovulation drugs, then IUI, then IVF/ICSI, then donor or surrogacy options. Age dominates prognosis (female fertility falls sharply after 35). Legal adoption remains the parallel path programmes must mention.
NEET Snapshot:
  • Test both partners; treat stepwise from simple to advanced.
  • Female age is the steepest fertility factor.
NEET Practice MCQ
1

Infertility is clinically defined as no conception after

Solution: Two years of regular unprotected intercourse without conception triggers evaluation.

Chapter 11 · Topic 12

IVF

Reproductive HealthReproductionStep-by-step Topic
IVF-ET (test-tube baby): ovaries hyperstimulated, eggs retrieved, fertilised with sperm in the laboratory dish, and resulting embryos transferred to the uterus - the classic solution for blocked tubes. Louise Brown (1978) was the first success. Per-cycle success is ~30-40%, falling with maternal age; surplus embryos can be frozen for later attempts. Cost, multiple-pregnancy risk and ethics (embryo ownership, posthumous use) surround every cycle.

Related Diagram · IVF path

Fig: Eggs and sperm meet in glass, embryos grow days, the best returns to the uterus.

Topic in Depth · IVF

IVF-ET (test-tube baby): hyperstimulated ovaries yield eggs, lab fertilisation with processed sperm creates embryos, the best transfer to the uterus - bypassing blocked tubes entirely (Louise Brown, 1978, first success). Per-cycle success runs ~30-40 percent, falling with maternal age; surplus embryos freeze for later attempts. Multiple-pregnancy risk, cost, and embryo-ownership ethics (divorce, posthumous use) surround every cycle - medicine inseparable from law here.
NEET Snapshot:
  • Lab fertilisation plus uterine transfer bypasses blocked tubes.
  • ~30-40 percent per cycle; age dominates success.
Remember
  • IVF solves tubal blockage; ICSI solves severe male factor.
NEET Practice MCQ
1

IVF-ET is most directly indicated for

Solution: Lab fertilisation plus uterine transfer routes around impassable tubes.

Chapter 11 · Topic 13

ZIFT

Reproductive HealthReproductionStep-by-step Topic
ZIFT (zygote intra-fallopian transfer) moves the IVF zygote (up to 8-cell stage) into the fallopian tube instead of the uterus, letting natural tubal transport complete the journey - chosen when tubes are healthy but earlier steps failed. It needs laparoscopy (more invasive than uterine transfer), so usage has shrunk as uterine-transfer success improved. Conceptually it sits halfway between in-body and in-lab conception.

Topic in Depth · ZIFT

ZIFT (zygote intra-fallopian transfer) moves the IVF zygote (up to 8-cell stage) into a healthy fallopian tube via laparoscopy, letting natural tubal transport complete the journey to the uterus. It suits couples wanting more natural transit or with uterine-transfer failures, but needs at least one open tube plus invasive laparoscopy - so improving uterine-transfer success has shrunk its use. Conceptually halfway between in-body and in-lab conception.
NEET Snapshot:
  • Zygote to healthy tube; nature completes transport.
  • Needs open tube plus laparoscopy - more invasive than uterine transfer.
NEET Practice MCQ
1

In ZIFT, what is transferred into the fallopian tube?

Solution: Fertilisation happens in lab; the early zygote finishes transit naturally.

Index / Reproduction
Chapter 11 · Topic 14

GIFT

Reproductive HealthReproductionStep-by-step Topic
GIFT (gamete intra-fallopian transfer) places unfertilised eggs + sperm directly into healthy fallopian tubes, so fertilisation happens inside the body - the choice for couples with moral objections to lab fertilisation or unexplained infertility with open tubes. It demands at least one functional tube and uses laparoscopy. ICSI (single sperm injected into one egg) instead solves severe male-factor infertility; IUI simply deposits washed semen into the uterus.

Topic in Depth · GIFT

GIFT (gamete intra-fallopian transfer) places unfertilised eggs plus sperm directly into healthy tubes via laparoscopy, so fertilisation happens inside the body - the choice for unexplained infertility with open tubes or moral objections to lab fertilisation. It demands functional tubes (unlike IVF) and offers no fertilisation proof if pregnancy fails. ICSI (single sperm injected into one egg) instead conquers severe male-factor infertility; IUI simply deposits washed semen into the uterus for milder cases.
NEET Snapshot:
  • Gametes to healthy tube; fertilisation inside the body.
  • Needs open tubes; ICSI instead fixes sperm defects.
NEET Practice MCQ
1

GIFT differs from IVF in that fertilisation occurs

Solution: GIFT transfers gametes for in-body fertilisation; IVF fertilises in glass first.

Chapter 12 · Step-by-step

Principles of Inheritance and Variation

Mendel crosses, dominance, ABO, sex linkage, pedigrees and disorders.

Principles of Inheritance and VariationGenetics & EvolutionChapter Introduction

Subtopics in this Chapter

1. Mendelian inheritance2. Monohybrid cross3. Dihybrid cross4. Laws of inheritance5. Test cross6. Back cross7. Incomplete dominance8. Codominance9. Multiple alleles10. ABO blood groups11. Sex determination12. Sex-linked inheritance13. Pedigree analysis14. Genetic disorders15. Chromosomal disorders

Deep Dive · Every ratio and criss-cross inheritance

Ratio table - the most scoring page of genetics
CrossPhenotypic ratioMeaning
Monohybrid F2 (Tt x Tt)3 : 1 (genotypic 1:2:1)Law of segregation
Dihybrid F29 : 3 : 3 : 1Independent assortment; deviation signals linkage
Test cross (unknown x tt)1 : 1 means heterozygous; all dominant means homozygousReveals hidden genotype
Incomplete dominance (Rr pink)1 : 2 : 1 (same as genotypic)Dog flower, Mirabilis
CodominanceBoth traits visibleAB blood group, sickle-cell carriers
Linkage breaks Mendel's third law: genes on the same chromosome travel together (Morgan's Drosophila), so parental combinations exceed 50% and the 9:3:3:1 collapses. Crossing over in pachytene rescues variation by swapping segments; recombination frequency maps distance (1% = 1 map unit/centimorgan). Sex determination: XX-XY (humans - male heterogametic), ZZ-ZW (birds - female heterogametic), XX-XO (grasshopper), haplodiploidy (honeybees - males haploid from unfertilised eggs, so a drone has a grandfather but no father).
Fig: Gametes along the margins, zygotes inside - three boxes carry T (tall), only tt is dwarf.
Disorders cheat-sheet
  • Autosomal recessive: sickle-cell anaemia (GAG to GUG point mutation), thalassemia, cystic fibrosis, PKU, albinism.
  • Autosomal dominant: myotonic dystrophy. X-linked recessive: haemophilia, colour blindness (criss-cross: father to daughter to grandson).
  • Chromosomal: Down (trisomy 21), Turner (XO female), Klinefelter (XXY male).
NEET Practice MCQ
1

In a dihybrid cross the F2 phenotypic ratio 9:3:3:1 is obtained because of

Solution: Mendel's law of independent assortment shuffles the two gene pairs freely; tight linkage would destroy the 9:3:3:1 pattern.

Fig: Squares are males, circles females; filled means affected, half-shaded means carrier.

Chapter 12 · Topic 1

Mendelian inheritance

Principles of Inheritance and VariationGenetics & EvolutionStep-by-step Topic
Mendel (1860s, pea, 7 contrasting traits) founded genetics by counting thousands of offspring: chosen pea for self-pollination (true lines), easy emasculation/dusting for crosses, short generations and big progeny. His three laws - dominance, segregation, independent assortment - waited 35 years for chromosomes and meiosis (Sutton-Boveri theory) to supply the physical mechanism. Rediscovered in 1900 by de Vries, Correns and Tschermak simultaneously.

Topic in Depth · Mendelian inheritance

Mendel founded genetics by counting pea offspring by the thousand: self-pollination gave true lines, emasculation plus dusting made exact crosses, seven contrasting traits gave clean either-or scores, short generations plus big progeny gave ratios. Dominance (F1 uniformity), segregation (F2 3:1 reappearance), assortment (dihybrid 9:3:3:1 new combos) emerged purely from numbers - chromosomes and meiosis (Sutton-Boveri) supplied the mechanism 35 years later. Rediscovery in 1900 launched genetics.
NEET Snapshot:
  • Pea advantages: selfing, easy crosses, clear traits, big families.
  • Ratios first (1860s), mechanism later (chromosomes, meiosis).
NEET Practice MCQ
1

Mendel chose pea mainly because it

Solution: True lines, controlled crosses, binary traits and large progeny made ratios visible.
Back to Index
Index / Genetics & Evolution
Chapter 12 · Topic 2

Monohybrid cross

Principles of Inheritance and VariationGenetics & EvolutionStep-by-step Topic
Monohybrid cross (Tt x Tt): F1 all tall (dominance masks t), F2 phenotypic 3:1 with genotypic 1:2:1 (TT:Tt:tt) - the hidden recessive reappears, proving alleles separate unchanged (law of segregation: gametes carry one allele each). Punnett squares place gametes on margins and read zygotes inside; three of four boxes carry T, only tt is dwarf. Every ratio question starts here.

Topic in Depth · Monohybrid cross

Monohybrid Tt x Tt delivers the founding ratio: F1 all tall (dominance masks t), F2 three tall to one dwarf phenotypically with 1:2:1 TT:Tt:tt genotypically (segregation preserves alleles unchanged through gametes). Punnett squares place parental gametes on margins and read zygotes inside - three of four boxes carry T, only tt shows dwarf. Every ratio problem reduces to this template; dihybrid is just two monohybrids running simultaneously.
NEET Snapshot:
  • F2 3:1 phenotype, 1:2:1 genotype - segregation made visible.
  • Hidden recessive reappears unchanged - alleles do not blend.
Remember
  • Punnett margins are gametes; interiors are zygotes - read accordingly.
NEET Practice MCQ
1

Genotypic ratio of monohybrid F2 is

Solution: TT:Tt:tt = 1:2:1 underlies the 3:1 dominant appearance.
Back to Index
Index / Genetics & Evolution
Chapter 12 · Topic 3

Dihybrid cross

Principles of Inheritance and VariationGenetics & EvolutionStep-by-step Topic
Dihybrid cross (YyRr x YyRr): F2 phenotypic 9:3:3:1 - 9 round-yellow (both dominant), 3 round-green, 3 wrinkled-yellow, 1 wrinkled-green - proving genes on different chromosomes assort independently (law of independent assortment). New combinations (round-green, wrinkled-yellow) appear that neither parent showed. Tight linkage collapses this ratio toward parental types - the standard signal that assortment failed.

Linkage and crossing over

Sutton–Boveri chromosomal theory: genes sit on chromosomes, explaining Mendel. Linkage (Morgan, Drosophila): genes on the same chromosome travel together; strength falls as distance rises. 1% recombination = 1 map unit (centimorgan) — the basis of genetic maps. Crossing over (pachytene) breaks linkage and creates new combinations.
Morgan student Sturtevant built the first genetic map as an undergraduate: recombination percentages between Drosophila genes became map distances (1% = 1 map unit). Double crossovers underestimate long distances, so far-apart genes can appear unlinked (>50% recombination looks like independent assortment). Cytological proof came from maize (Creighton and McClintock) showing physical exchange of chromosome segments exactly where crossing over was predicted - genetics met microscopy.

Topic in Depth · Dihybrid cross

Dihybrid YyRr x YyRr yields 9 round-yellow : 3 round-green : 3 wrinkled-yellow : 1 wrinkled-green - two parental plus two recombinant classes proving genes on different chromosomes assort independently into gametes (YR, Yr, yR, yr equally). New combinations neither parent showed are the recombination signature. Tight linkage collapses the ratio toward parental types; crossing over partially restores recombinants - the bridge from Mendel to Morgan mapping.
NEET Snapshot:
  • 9:3:3:1 with two new recombinant classes = independent assortment.
  • Linkage destroys the ratio; crossing over partly rescues it.
NEET Practice MCQ
1

New phenotypic combinations in dihybrid F2 arise due to

Solution: Free shuffling of two gene pairs manufactures round-green and wrinkled-yellow classes.

Index / Genetics & Evolution
Chapter 12 · Topic 4

Laws of inheritance

Principles of Inheritance and VariationGenetics & EvolutionStep-by-step Topic
Mendel (pea, 7 contrasting traits): law of dominance, law of segregation (alleles separate in gametes) and law of independent assortment (dihybrid). Monohybrid F₂: phenotypic 3:1, genotypic 1:2:1; dihybrid F₂ phenotypic 9:3:3:1. Test cross (unknown × homozygous recessive) reveals genotype: 1:1 means heterozygous.
NEET facts:
  • Pea is ideal: self-pollinating, short life cycle, clear contrasting traits.
  • Failure of 9:3:3:1 signals linkage between the gene pairs.

Deeper Understanding · Mendel seven traits decoded

Mendel tracked exactly seven pea traits: stem height (tall/dwarf), flower colour (violet/white) and position (axial/terminal), pod shape (inflated/constricted) and colour (green/yellow), seed shape (round/wrinkled) and colour (yellow/green). Pea suited him perfectly: self-pollinating (true lines), easy emasculation + dusting for crosses, short generation, and large progeny for ratios. His laws waited 35 years because chromosomes and meiosis were unknown - Sutton and Boveri later supplied the physical basis (chromosomal theory).

Topic in Depth · Laws of inheritance

Three laws ladder complexity: dominance (F1 shows dominant allele only), segregation (allele pairs separate into gametes unchanged - proven by 3:1 F2 reappearance), independent assortment (different gene pairs shuffle freely - proven by 9:3:3:1 recombinants). Dominance describes expression, segregation describes transmission, assortment describes multi-gene shuffling. Exceptions refine rather than refute - incomplete dominance, codominance, linkage each reveal the law operating underneath modified expression.
NEET Snapshot:
  • Dominance (show), segregation (separate), assortment (shuffle).
  • Exceptions reveal mechanisms; laws still anchor every problem.
Law proofs
PointDetail
DominanceF1 uniformity
Segregation3:1 F2 with 1:2:1 genotypes
Independent assortment9:3:3:1 with recombinants
NEET Practice MCQ
1

Law of segregation is proven by

Solution: Recessive reappearance proves alleles separate unchanged into gametes.

Chapter 12 · Topic 5

Test cross

Principles of Inheritance and VariationGenetics & EvolutionStep-by-step Topic
Test cross (unknown dominant phenotype x homozygous recessive): all dominant offspring means the parent was homozygous; a 1:1 mix means it was heterozygous. Breeders use it to purify lines; examiners use it to test whether you can work ratios backwards. A back cross is any cross back to a parent - test cross is the special back cross to the recessive parent.

Related Diagram · Test cross reading

Fig: Cross to recessive and read the children: uniform means pure, half-half means hybrid.

Topic in Depth · Test cross

Test cross (unknown dominant phenotype crossed to homozygous recessive) reads hidden genotype from children: all dominant means parent was homozygous; 1:1 dominant-to-recessive means heterozygous. Breeders purify lines this way; examiners test reverse-ratio thinking the same way. It is the special back cross to the recessive parent - the only cross where offspring phenotypes directly spell parental genotype.
NEET Snapshot:
  • All dominant children = homozygous parent. 1:1 = heterozygous parent.
  • Test cross is back cross to the recessive parent specifically.
NEET Practice MCQ
1

A tall plant crossed to dwarf gives 1:1 tall:dwarf. Its genotype is

Solution: Equal segregation to recessive reveals heterozygosity - the test-cross verdict.

Index / Genetics & Evolution
Chapter 12 · Topic 6

Back cross

Principles of Inheritance and VariationGenetics & EvolutionStep-by-step Topic
Back cross means crossing F1 (or any progeny) back to either parent: to the dominant parent it hides genotypes (all dominant phenotypes), to the recessive parent it becomes a test cross revealing them (1:1 if heterozygous). Plant breeders backcross repeatedly to transfer one desired gene (e.g. disease resistance) into an elite variety while recovering its genome - each generation halves the donor remainder.

Topic in Depth · Back cross

Back cross means progeny crossed back to either parent: to the dominant parent all children look dominant (genotypes hidden); to the recessive parent it becomes a test cross (genotypes revealed 1:1 if heterozygous). Breeders backcross repeatedly to transfer one desired gene (disease resistance) into elite varieties - each generation halves donor genome remainder while keeping the target gene. After 6-7 backcrosses the line is ~99 percent elite plus the new trait.
NEET Snapshot:
  • Back to dominant hides; back to recessive reveals (test cross).
  • Repeated backcrossing moves one gene into elite backgrounds.
Remember
  • Test cross is a back cross; not every back cross is a test cross.
NEET Practice MCQ
1

Repeated backcrossing in plant breeding is used to

Solution: Each backcross halves unwanted donor genome while retaining the target gene.

Index / Genetics & Evolution
Chapter 12 · Topic 7

Incomplete dominance

Principles of Inheritance and VariationGenetics & EvolutionStep-by-step Topic
Complete dominance: heterozygote shows the dominant trait (Tt = tall). Incomplete dominance (dog flower/Mirabilis): F₂ 1:2:1 pink — phenotype blends. Codominance: both alleles fully expressed — AB blood group, sickle-cell heterozygotes. Multiple alleles: ABO system (Iᴬ, Iᴮ, i) — 6 genotypes, 4 phenotypes; IᴬIᴮ = codominant.

A red (RR) and white (rr) dog flower cross gives all pink (Rr); selfing gives red:pink:white in what ratio?

Answer: 1:2:1 — incomplete dominance makes the genotypic and phenotypic ratios identical.

Deeper Understanding · ABO genotypes fully listed

Three alleles (IA, IB, i) make six genotypes: IAIA + IAi = A, IBIB + IBi = B, IAIB = AB (codominant), ii = O. A cross of AxB parents can yield all four groups in children - classic pedigree puzzle. Sickle-cell heterozygotes (HbA/HbS) show codominance at molecular level (both haemoglobins made) but the trait protects against malaria - balanced polymorphism keeping a lethal allele alive. Rh blood group is independently inherited (D/d alleles), so ABO x Rh problems multiply: 4 x 2 = 8 possible phenotypes.

Topic in Depth · Incomplete dominance

Incomplete dominance blends heterozygotes between parents: dog-flower red x white gives all pink F1, and F2 runs 1 red : 2 pink : 1 white - phenotypic ratio identical to genotypic (1:2:1) because no allele fully masks. Snapdragons (Mirabilis) are the textbook; Andalusian fowl and Tay-Sachs carriers show similar intermediacy. It refines dominance (expression blends) without touching segregation (alleles still separate) - laws intact, appearances intermediate.
NEET Snapshot:
  • F2 1:2:1 both ways - phenotype mirrors genotype.
  • Blending is expression-level; alleles stay discrete underneath.
NEET Practice MCQ
1

Pink F1 from red x white dog-flower selfed gives F2 ratio

Solution: Incomplete dominance makes phenotypic and genotypic ratios identical at 1:2:1.
Back to Index
Index / Genetics & Evolution
Chapter 12 · Topic 8

Codominance

Principles of Inheritance and VariationGenetics & EvolutionStep-by-step Topic
Codominance expresses both alleles fully in the heterozygote: AB blood group (IAIB makes both antigens), sickle-cell carriers (both HbA and HbS haemoglobins detectable), roan cattle (red + white hairs intermingled). Unlike incomplete dominance there is no blending - both products sit side by side. At molecular level most heterozygotes are codominant (both proteins made); dominance is often just the visible-trait description.

Topic in Depth · Codominance

Codominance expresses both alleles fully side by side: AB blood (both antigens on RBCs), sickle-cell carriers (both HbA and HbS haemoglobins detectable), roan cattle (red plus white hairs intermingled, not blended pink). Unlike incomplete dominance there is no intermediate - both products sit visibly together. Molecularly most heterozygotes are codominant (both proteins made); dominance versus codominance often just describes which level examiners inspect.
NEET Snapshot:
  • Both alleles fully visible - AB blood, sickle carriers, roan coat.
  • No blending: products coexist, not merge.
NEET Practice MCQ
1

AB blood group illustrates

Solution: IAIB red cells carry both A and B antigens simultaneously and fully.
Back to Index
Index / Genetics & Evolution
Chapter 12 · Topic 9

Multiple alleles

Principles of Inheritance and VariationGenetics & EvolutionStep-by-step Topic
Multiple alleles means 3+ alleles of one gene in the population (though each individual carries two): ABO has IA, IB, i giving 6 genotypes and 4 phenotypes. Hierarchy: IA and IB dominate i, and codominate each other. Rabbit coat colour (C, cch, ch, c) and Drosophila eye colours extend the pattern - dominance series with stepwise expression. Population genetics counts allele frequencies, not just individual pairs.

Topic in Depth · Multiple alleles

Multiple alleles mean 3+ gene versions population-wide (each individual still carries two): ABO (IA, IB, i), rabbit coat series (C > cch > ch > c dominance hierarchy), Drosophila eye colours. Hierarchies stack - IA and IB dominate i but tie each other. Population genetics then counts allele frequencies (p, q, r) rather than individual pairs, extending Hardy-Weinberg to three alleles. More alleles mean more genotypes combinatorially but unchanged meiotic rules per individual.
NEET Snapshot:
  • Population carries many; individual carries two.
  • Dominance hierarchies stack (rabbit C series classic).
Remember
  • Count genotypes combinatorially; apply dominance pairwise.
NEET Practice MCQ
1

ABO system has how many alleles and genotypes?

Solution: IA, IB, i combine into six genotypes collapsing to four phenotypes.

Index / Genetics & Evolution
Chapter 12 · Topic 10

ABO blood groups

Principles of Inheritance and VariationGenetics & EvolutionStep-by-step Topic
ABO genotypes to phenotypes: IAIA + IAi = A; IBIB + IBi = B; IAIB = AB (codominant); ii = O. Antigens on RBCs, opposite antibodies in plasma - a person never attacks their own type. An AxB couple can produce all four groups in children (classic pedigree puzzle). Rh (D/d) inherits independently, so combined ABO x Rh problems multiply: 4 x 2 = 8 possible phenotypes from suitable crosses.

Related Diagram · ABO genotypes to groups

Fig: Three alleles, six pairings, four blood groups - dominance with a codominant tie on top.

Topic in Depth · ABO blood groups

ABO maps genotype to transfusion fate: IAIA/IAi make A antigen (anti-B plasma), IBIB/IBi make B (anti-A plasma), IAIB makes both (no antibodies - universal recipient), ii makes none (both antibodies - universal donor cells). Antigens sit on RBCs, opposite antibodies float in plasma - mismatch agglutinates. AxB parents can yield all four groups in children (classic pedigree puzzle); Rh (D/d) inherits independently, multiplying to 8 combined phenotypes.
NEET Snapshot:
  • Antigens on cells, opposite antibodies in plasma - never self-attack.
  • IAIB universal recipient; O universal donor cells.
NEET Practice MCQ
1

A child with O blood group can have parents of groups

Solution: Only heterozygous A (IAi) x heterozygous B (IBi) can both donate i for ii children.

Index / Genetics & Evolution
Chapter 12 · Topic 11

Sex determination

Principles of Inheritance and VariationGenetics & EvolutionStep-by-step Topic
Mechanisms: XX–XY (humans, Drosophila — male heterogametic), ZZ–ZW (birds — female heterogametic), XX–XO (grasshopper), haplodiploidy (honeybees — males haploid from unfertilised eggs). X-linked recessive disorders (haemophilia, colour blindness) show criss-cross inheritance (father → daughter → grandson) and strike males more. Genetic disorders: autosomal recessive (sickle-cell anaemia — GAG→GUG point mutation; thalassemia; cystic fibrosis; PKU), autosomal dominant (myotonic dystrophy), chromosomal — Down (trisomy 21), Turner (XO female), Klinefelter (XXY male).

A haemophilic man has children with a normal homozygous woman. What about their sons and grandsons?

Answer: Sons get Y from father and normal X from mother — all normal. Daughters are all carriers; their sons face a 50% risk (criss-cross pattern).

Common mistakes: honeybee males have no father but have grandfathers (haploid from unfertilised eggs).

Deeper Understanding · Barr body - dosage compensation

Females carry two X chromosomes but males one; to equalise gene dose, each female cell randomly inactivates one X (Barr body) early in development - which is why calico cats are patchy (different X active in different skin patches). This explains Turner (XO: one active X, viable female) vs the lethality of YO (no X at all - the X carries essential genes). Klinefelter (XXY) males show one Barr body; Down syndrome severity varies because chromosome 21 is gene-poor enough to tolerate triplication.

Related Diagram · XX-XY inheritance

Fig: Mothers give X always; fathers decide - sons get Y, daughters get the second X.

Topic in Depth · Sex determination

Sex-determining systems assign heterogamety differently: XX-XY (humans, Drosophila - male heterogametic, father decides), ZZ-ZW (birds, butterflies - female heterogametic, mother decides), XX-XO (grasshopper - male has one X), haplodiploidy (honeybees - fertilised eggs become diploid females/workers/queen by feeding, unfertilised eggs become haploid drone males with grandfathers but no father). Human Y carries SRY (testis-determining); X carries housekeeping genes explaining X-linked disease patterns.
NEET Snapshot:
  • Whoever is heterogametic decides offspring sex.
  • Honeybee drones are haploid with no father - haplodiploidy extreme.
Systems
PointDetail
XX-XYHumans, Drosophila (male decides)
ZZ-ZWBirds (female decides)
XX-XOGrasshopper (male one X)
HaplodiploidBees (fertilisation decides)

In birds, sex of offspring is decided by the

Solution: Female birds are heterogametic (ZW) - mothers determine sons versus daughters.

Chapter 12 · Topic 12

Sex-linked inheritance

Principles of Inheritance and VariationGenetics & EvolutionStep-by-step Topic
X-linked recessive traits (haemophilia, colour blindness, Duchenne muscular dystrophy) strike males far more (single X exposed) and show criss-cross inheritance: father to daughter (carrier) to grandson. X-linked dominant (fragile X) hits heterozygous females too. Y-linked (holandric) traits (hypertrichosis ear hair) pass father to son only, never to daughters. Barr-body counting and pedigree shading patterns diagnose each mode in problems.

Topic in Depth · Sex-linked inheritance

X-linked recessive traits (haemophilia, colour blindness, DMD) strike males harder (single exposed X) and show criss-cross inheritance (father to daughter carrier to grandson - never father to son). X-linked dominant (fragile X, vitamin-D-resistant rickets) hits heterozygous females too. Y-linked holandric traits (ear hypertrichosis) pass father-to-son only. Barr-body dosage compensation (one X inactivated per female cell) explains calico-cat mosaicism and Turner/Klinefelter viability.
NEET Snapshot:
  • X-recessive: male excess plus criss-cross. Y-linked: father-son only.
  • Barr body inactivates one X per female cell.
Remember
  • Father-to-son transmission rules out X-linkage instantly.
NEET Practice MCQ
1

A haemophilic man marries a normal homozygous woman. Their sons will be

Solution: Sons get Y from father plus normal X from mother - all spared; daughters all carriers.

Chapter 12 · Topic 13

Pedigree analysis

Principles of Inheritance and VariationGenetics & EvolutionStep-by-step Topic
Pedigree reading rules: autosomal dominant appears every generation with affected heterozygotes; autosomal recessive skips generations with carrier parents (consanguinity raises risk); X-linked recessive shows criss-cross male excess; Y-linked is straight father-son lines; mitochondrial is mother-to-all-children only. Shaded = affected, half-shaded = carrier, horizontal line = mating, vertical = descent. Haemophilia in European royalty is the textbook X-linked pedigree.

Topic in Depth · Pedigree analysis

Pedigrees diagnose modes by pattern: autosomal dominant appears every generation (affected heterozygote parent); autosomal recessive skips generations with carrier parents (consanguinity raises risk); X-linked recessive shows criss-cross male excess; Y-linked runs strict father-son lines; mitochondrial passes mother-to-all-children only. Symbols: squares males, circles females, shaded affected, half-shaded carriers, horizontal mating lines, vertical descent. European royal haemophilia is the textbook X-linked pedigree.
NEET Snapshot:
  • Every generation = dominant. Skips = recessive. Criss-cross males = X-linked.
  • Consanguinity flags recessive risk.
NEET Practice MCQ
1

A trait appearing every generation with affected heterozygotes is likely

Solution: Dominant alleles show in heterozygotes, so affected parents appear each generation.

Chapter 12 · Topic 14

Genetic disorders

Principles of Inheritance and VariationGenetics & EvolutionStep-by-step Topic
Mendelian disorders: autosomal recessive - sickle-cell anaemia (GAG to GUG point mutation, Glu to Val), thalassemia (reduced globin synthesis), cystic fibrosis, PKU, albinism, Tay-Sachs; autosomal dominant - myotonic dystrophy, Huntington (late onset), achondroplasia; X-linked recessive - haemophilia, colour blindness, DMD. Recessive disorders need carrier parents; dominant ones need just one affected parent (usually heterozygous, as homozygous is often lethal).

Topic in Depth · Genetic disorders

Mendelian disorders follow single-gene logic: autosomal recessive (sickle-cell GAG-to-GUG point mutation, thalassemia reduced synthesis, cystic fibrosis, PKU, albinism, Tay-Sachs - carrier parents, 25 percent risk), autosomal dominant (myotonic dystrophy, Huntington late-onset, achondroplasia - one affected parent suffices, homozygous often lethal), X-linked recessive (haemophilia, colour blindness, DMD - male excess). Counselling multiplies probabilities per pregnancy independently - each child rolls fresh odds.
NEET Snapshot:
  • Recessive needs two carriers; dominant needs one affected parent.
  • Sickle-cell: GAG to GUG, Glu to Val - the famous point mutation.
NEET Practice MCQ
1

Sickle-cell anaemia is caused by

Solution: Single-base substitution swaps one amino acid, polymerising deoxygenated haemoglobin.

Chapter 12 · Topic 15

Chromosomal disorders

Principles of Inheritance and VariationGenetics & EvolutionStep-by-step Topic
Chromosomal disorders come from nondisjunction: Down syndrome (trisomy 21 - mongoloid slant, simian crease, mental retardation; risk rises with maternal age), Turner (XO female - sterile, webbed neck, shield chest, no Barr body), Klinefelter (XXY male - tall, gynaecomastia, sterile, one Barr body). Autosomal monosomies are lethal (missing essential genes); sex-chromosome variants survive because X-inactivation and Y gene-poverty cushion the imbalance.

Topic in Depth · Chromosomal disorders

Chromosomal disorders come from nondisjunction numbers, not gene spellings: Down (trisomy 21 - slant eyes, simian crease, intellectual disability; risk rises with maternal age), Turner (XO female - sterile, webbed neck, shield chest, no Barr body), Klinefelter (XXY male - tall, gynaecomastia, sterile, one Barr body). Autosomal monosomies die (missing essential genes); sex-chromosome variants survive via X-inactivation cushioning and Y gene-poverty. Karyotype (arranged chromosome portrait) diagnoses all three.
NEET Snapshot:
  • Down 47 (21), Turner 45 (X0), Klinefelter 47 (XXY).
  • Maternal age raises Down risk; Barr bodies count extra Xs.

Chapter 13 · Step-by-step

Molecular Basis of Inheritance

DNA, replication, transcription, code, translation, operon and fingerprinting.

Molecular Basis of InheritanceGenetics & EvolutionChapter Introduction

Subtopics in this Chapter

1. DNA structure2. RNA3. DNA replication4. Transcription5. Genetic code6. Translation7. Protein synthesis8. Gene regulation9. Lac operon10. Mutation11. DNA fingerprinting12. Human Genome Project

Deep Dive · Experiments and enzymes of molecular biology

Three experiments prove DNA is genetic material: Griffith (1928) showed harmless R-strain bacteria transformed into deadly S-strain inside mice; Avery-MacLeod-McCarty proved the transforming substance was DNA (destroyed by DNase, not protease); Hershey-Chase (1952) blended bacteriophages off bacteria and found only 32P-labelled DNA entered cells while 35S protein stayed outside. Meselson-Stahl then proved replication is semi-conservative using heavy 15N DNA across generations.
Replication-transcription-translation enzymes
ProcessEnzyme / factorJob
ReplicationHelicase, DNA polymerase III, ligaseUnwind; extend leading strand, Okazaki fragments on lagging; join fragments
TranscriptionRNA polymerase + sigma / rho factorsCopy template 3' to 5' into mRNA; start and stop signals
RNA processingSpliceosome machineryCapping, tailing, splicing out introns - exons joined
TranslationRibosomes, tRNA, aminoacyl synthetaseRead codons; charge tRNAs; start at AUG, stop at UAA/UAG/UGA
Fig: tRNA cloverleaf - anticodon reads mRNA below, amino acid rides on top; charging by aminoacyl synthetase.
HGP + fingerprinting numbers
  • Human genome: ~3 billion bp, ~30,000 genes, under 2% coding; chromosome 1 most genes, Y fewest; 99.9% bases identical in all humans.
  • DNA fingerprinting (Alec Jeffreys) compares VNTR/minisatellite patterns by Southern blotting.
NEET Practice MCQ
1

Solution: The lagging strand grows as short Okazaki fragments that DNA ligase seals into one continuous strand.

Fig: One fork, two strategies - smooth leading strand, patchwork lagging strand sealed by ligase.

Chapter 13 · Topic 1

DNA structure

Molecular Basis of InheritanceGenetics & EvolutionStep-by-step Topic
Watson-Crick DNA: antiparallel right-handed double helix, sugar-phosphate backbones outside, bases inside - A=T (2 H-bonds), G triple C (3 H-bonds) (Chargaff: A=T, G=C in any duplex). One turn = 10 bp = 3.4 nm, rise 0.34 nm per pair. DNA wraps histone octamers (H2A, H2B, H3, H4) into nucleosomes (beads on a string), coiling to metaphase chromosomes - 2 metres packed into micrometres.

Topic in Depth · DNA structure

Watson-Crick DNA pairs antiparallel sugar-phosphate backbones outside with complementary bases inside - A=T (2 bonds), G triple C (3 bonds) - coiling right-handed at 10 bp per 3.4 nm turn. Chargaff equality (A=T, G=C) plus X-ray diffraction plus model-building converged on the helix in 1953. Packaging then compresses 2 metres into micrometres: histone octamer nucleosomes, 30-nm fibres, looped domains, metaphase chromosomes - 10,000-fold compaction for shipping, unspooled locally for reading.
NEET Snapshot:
  • A=T double, G triple C; antiparallel 5 to 3 plus 3 to 5.
  • Nucleosomes (H2A H2B H3 H4) coil to chromosomes.
Helix numbers

Pairs per turn10 (3.4 nm)

In a DNA duplex, if adenine is 30 percent, guanine is

Chapter 13 · Topic 2

RNA

Molecular Basis of InheritanceGenetics & EvolutionStep-by-step Topic
DNA: deoxyribose sugar, bases A/T/G/C, usually double-stranded, stable — the genetic material (proven by Hershey–Chase blender experiment on bacteriophages; Griffith's transformation + Avery–MacLeod–McCarty pointed the way). RNA: ribose sugar, uracil instead of thymine, usually single-stranded; types — mRNA (message), tRNA (adaptor with anticodon + CCA amino-acid arm), rRNA (ribosome machine). Some viruses use RNA as genetic material (retroviruses carry reverse transcriptase).
Fig: DNA double helix — antiparallel strands, A=T (2 bonds), G≡C (3 bonds), following Chargaff's rules.

Deeper Understanding · Chargaff mathematics

Chargaff rules let you compute any base: if A = 30%, then T = 30% (pairing), leaving G + C = 40%, so G = C = 20% each. The ratio (A+T)/(G+C) is species-specific (low in humans, high in some bacteria) because G-C pairs carry 3 hydrogen bonds vs 2 for A-T - GC-rich DNA melts at higher temperature, which PCR exploits. RNA replaces thymine with uracil and usually stays single-stranded, folding into functional shapes (tRNA cloverleaf, ribozymes) - a few viruses even use RNA as genetic material, with reverse transcriptase copying it back to DNA.

Related Diagram · Three RNA workers

Fig: Messenger copies, transfer carries, ribosomal assembles - translation needs all three.

Topic in Depth · RNA

RNA trades thymine for uracil, ribose for deoxyribose, and usually runs single-stranded - folding into functional shapes instead of duplexes. Three workers divide translation: mRNA (message copy with UTR regulators), tRNA (cloverleaf adaptor with anticodon plus CCA amino-acid arm), rRNA (ribosome catalytic core - the ribosome is a ribozyme). Some viruses store genomes as RNA, copying via reverse transcriptase - the retroviral exception proving DNA usual rule. RNA world hypotheses even cast RNA as life first molecule (information plus catalysis combined).
NEET Snapshot:
  • mRNA message, tRNA adaptor, rRNA machine.
  • Uracil replaces thymine; usually single-stranded and folded.
Remember
  • Ribosome peptide-bond formation is RNA-catalysed - protein machine with RNA engine.
NEET Practice MCQ
1

Adaptor molecule between mRNA codon and amino acid is

Solution: tRNA anticodons read codons while CCA arms deliver matched amino acids.
Back to Index
Index / Genetics & Evolution
Chapter 13 · Topic 3

DNA replication

Molecular Basis of InheritanceGenetics & EvolutionStep-by-step Topic
Watson–Crick model: antiparallel strands coiled right-handed around a common axis. DNA wraps on histone octamers (H2A, H2B, H3, H4) forming nucleosomes ("beads on a string") that coil into chromatin. Replication is semi-conservative (Meselson–Stahl ¹⁵N experiment): helicase unwinds, DNA polymerase III extends the leading strand continuously and the lagging strand as Okazaki fragments joined by ligase.
NEET facts:
  • Chargaff: A = T, G = C; (A+T)/(G+C) varies between species.
  • Replication proceeds 5′ → 3′ only; the template is read 3′ → 5′.

Deeper Understanding · Chromatin packing levels

Two metres of human DNA fit in a micrometre nucleus through layered packing: DNA wraps histone octamers (H2A, H2B, H3, H4) into nucleosomes (beads on a string, ~200 bp each), coiling into 30-nm fibres, looped domains and finally metaphase chromosomes (~10,000-fold compaction). Euchromatin is loose and transcriptionally active; heterochromatin is dense and silent (centromeres, Barr body). Histone acetylation loosens packing (genes on), methylation tightens it - the epigenetic layer above the sequence itself.

Topic in Depth · DNA replication

Replication copies semi-conservatively (Meselson-Stahl 15N proof): helicase unzips, single-strand proteins stabilise, primase lays RNA primers, DNA polymerase III extends 5 to 3 only - continuous leading strand, patchwork lagging Okazaki fragments sealed by ligase - with proofreading exonuclease correcting errors to one per billion bases. Origins fire bidirectionally; telomerase maintains chromosome ends in germ and stem cells. Speed plus accuracy plus coordination makes replication the cell most regulated synthesis.
NEET Snapshot:
  • Semi-conservative; leading smooth, lagging patchwork plus ligase.
  • Polymerase extends 5 to 3 only; helicase unzips ahead.
NEET Practice MCQ
1

Okazaki fragments are joined by

Solution: Ligase seals lagging-strand patches into one continuous strand.
Back to Index
Index / Genetics & Evolution
Chapter 13 · Topic 4

Transcription

Molecular Basis of InheritanceGenetics & EvolutionStep-by-step Topic
mRNA is copied by RNA polymerase from the template (3′→5′) strand; promoter (with sigma factor in bacteria) starts it, terminator (rho factor) ends it. Eukaryotic genes are split — coding exons interrupted by non-coding introns; hnRNA is capped, tailed and spliced into mature mRNA. Only one strand (with polarity 3′→5′) acts as template.

Deeper Understanding · Split genes surprise

Until 1977 everyone assumed genes were continuous; Roberts and Sharp (Nobel) showed eukaryotic genes are split - coding exons interrupted by non-coding introns removed by splicing. One gene can therefore yield several proteins via alternative splicing (human ~30,000 genes make ~100,000+ proteins). Only the template strand (3' to 5') is copied; the coding strand matches mRNA sequence (with T instead of U). Bacterial genes lack introns, so their transcription needs no processing - mRNA is translation-ready instantly.

Topic in Depth · Transcription

Transcription copies the template (3 to 5) strand into mRNA: RNA polymerase plus sigma factor recognise promoters, unwind a bubble, extend RNA 5 to 3, and rho factor terminates - no primers needed (unlike replication). Bacterial mRNA translates instantly; eukaryotic hnRNA needs capping, poly-A tailing and spliceosome intron removal first. Only one strand per gene serves as template, though different genes use opposite strands - transcription is local, not genome-wide.
NEET Snapshot:
  • Template read 3 to 5; RNA built 5 to 3; no primer needed.
  • Eukaryotes process hnRNA (cap, tail, splice) before export.
Remember
  • Promoter starts (sigma), terminator ends (rho) - bacterial signals.
NEET Practice MCQ
1

Transcription template strand runs

Solution: Polymerase reads template 3-to-5 while synthesising RNA 5-to-3.

Index / Genetics & Evolution
Chapter 13 · Topic 5

Genetic code

Molecular Basis of InheritanceGenetics & EvolutionStep-by-step Topic
The code is triplet, degenerate, unambiguous, universal (nearly): 61 sense codons for 20 amino acids, start AUG (methionine), stops UAA, UAG, UGA. Degeneracy clusters in the third base (wobble) - most third-position mutations are silent. Read 5' to 3' without overlap or punctuation; a frameshift garbles everything downstream while a nonsense mutation truncates early. Mitochondria slightly rewrite it (UGA = Trp).

Topic in Depth · Genetic code

The code maps 64 triplets to 20 amino acids plus stops: triplet reading, degenerate synonyms (wobble third base), unambiguous assignment (one codon, one meaning), near-universal dialect (mitochondria tweak UGA to Trp). Start AUG doubles as methionine; stops UAA, UAG, UGA end chains via release factors. Degeneracy buffers mutations (third-base changes often silent); frameshifts garble everything downstream while nonsense truncates early - mutation severity follows code position.
NEET Snapshot:
  • Triplet, degenerate, unambiguous, near-universal; AUG starts; UAA/UAG/UGA stop.
  • Wobble third base absorbs most silent mutations.
Code landmarks
PointDetail
StartAUG (methionine)
StopsUAA, UAG, UGA
Degeneracy61 sense codons, 20 amino acids
ExceptionMitochondrial UGA = Trp
NEET Practice MCQ
1

Wobble hypothesis explains

Solution: Loose third-position pairing lets one tRNA read several synonymous codons.

Index / Genetics & Evolution
Chapter 13 · Topic 6

Translation

Molecular Basis of InheritanceGenetics & EvolutionStep-by-step Topic
Ribosomes read mRNA codons; tRNA adaptors bring amino acids (charged by aminoacyl synthetase). Code features: triplet, degenerate, unambiguous, universal; start codon AUG (methionine); stop codons UAA, UAG, UGA. Translation has UTRs at both ends for regulation and ends when a release factor meets a stop codon.

A 900-nucleotide mRNA (excluding UTRs and stop codon) is fully translated. How long is the polypeptide?

Answer: 900 ÷ 3 = 300 codons → 300 amino acids (stop codon excluded from the count as given).

Deeper Understanding · Wobble and the code table

The code 61 sense codons for 20 amino acids is degenerate: the third base often wobbles (Crick wobble hypothesis), so one tRNA reads several codons - fewer tRNAs needed. Consequences: most point mutations in the third position are silent, while nonsense mutations create early stops and frameshifts garble everything downstream. Start codon AUG doubles as methionine; mitochondria slightly rewrite the code (UGA = tryptophan) - proof the code is near-universal, not perfectly universal.

Topic in Depth · Translation

Translation reads mRNA on ribosomes: charged tRNAs (aminoacyl synthetases match each amino acid to its tRNA) enter A-site, peptide bonds form, ribosome translocates, spent tRNAs exit - elongating chains that fold (often chaperone-assisted) into proteins. UTRs regulate, release factors end at stops, and multiple ribosomes (polysomes) translate one message simultaneously for speed. Antibiotics exploit bacterial ribosome differences (tetracycline, streptomycin) - selective poisoning by structural detail.
NEET Snapshot:
  • Charge, read, bond, move - ribosome assembly line with tRNA suppliers.
  • Polysomes parallelise; release factors terminate at stops.
NEET Practice MCQ
1

Aminoacyl synthetase enzymes function in

Solution: Correct amino-acid-to-tRNA matching (translation fidelity step one) is their job.
Back to Index
Index / Genetics & Evolution
Chapter 13 · Topic 7

Protein synthesis

Molecular Basis of InheritanceGenetics & EvolutionStep-by-step Topic
Protein synthesis in flow: DNA template transcribed to hnRNA, processed (capping, tailing, splicing out introns) to mRNA, exported, then ribosomes translate codons with tRNA adaptors charged by aminoacyl synthetases. Chaperones fold the chain; signal peptides route it (ER, mitochondria, secretion). One gene can yield many proteins via alternative splicing - ~30,000 human genes make 100,000+ proteins. Antibiotics like tetracycline and streptomycin jam bacterial ribosomes selectively.

Topic in Depth · Protein synthesis

Protein synthesis chains transcription plus processing plus translation plus folding plus routing: DNA to hnRNA (cap, tail, splice) to mRNA to ribosome chain to chaperone fold to signal-peptide destination (cytosol, ER secretion, mitochondria, nucleus). Alternative splicing multiplies outputs (one gene, many proteins); post-translational edits (phosphorylation, glycosylation, cleavage like proinsulin to insulin) finalise function. Errors at any stage cause disease - thalassemia (globin shortage), cystic fibrosis (misfolded channel).
NEET Snapshot:
  • Transcribe, process, translate, fold, route - five-stage pipeline.
  • Alternative splicing: 30,000 genes make 100,000+ proteins.
Remember
  • Proinsulin cleavage to insulin is post-translational editing in action.
NEET Practice MCQ
1

Alternative splicing increases

Solution: Exon shuffling yields multiple messages (and proteins) per gene.

Index / Genetics & Evolution
Chapter 13 · Topic 8

Gene regulation

Molecular Basis of InheritanceGenetics & EvolutionStep-by-step Topic
Bacteria regulate in operons (operator + structural genes under one promoter): inducible systems (lac - substrate switches on) vs repressible systems (trp - end product switches off), each with negative (repressor) and sometimes positive (CAP-cAMP) control. Eukaryotes add layers: chromatin packing (acetylation on, methylation off), transcription factors, splicing choices, mRNA stability, and post-translational modification - regulation at every step from DNA to protein.

Topic in Depth · Gene regulation

Gene regulation layers control from DNA to protein: chromatin packing (acetylation opens, methylation closes), transcription factors at promoters/enhancers, splicing choices, mRNA stability, translational blocks, post-translational edits. Bacteria economise with operons (lac inducible, trp repressible, plus CAP-cAMP positive control); eukaryotes orchestrate development with master regulators (homeotic genes ordering body plans). Same genome, different expression - liver versus neuron differs by regulation, not genes.
NEET Snapshot:
  • Bacteria: operons. Eukaryotes: chromatin plus factors plus splicing.
  • Regulation explains cell diversity from one genome.
NEET Practice MCQ
1

Histone acetylation generally causes

Solution: Acetylation loosens nucleosomes, exposing DNA to transcription machinery.

Index / Genetics & Evolution
Chapter 13 · Topic 9

Lac operon

Molecular Basis of InheritanceGenetics & EvolutionStep-by-step Topic
An operon = operator + structural genes under one promoter (prokaryotes). The lac operon has regulator gene i (repressor), promoter p, operator o, and structural genes z (β-galactosidase), y (permease), a (transacetylase). Without lactose the repressor blocks transcription; lactose (allolactose inducer) binds the repressor, freeing the operator so the enzymes digest lactose — a classic inducible, negative-control system.
Fig: Lac operon — inducer (lactose) switches the system ON by inactivating the repressor.

Deeper Understanding · Positive control via CAP-cAMP

The lac operon has a second switch: when glucose is absent, rising cAMP binds CAP, and the complex supercharges the promoter - so the operon runs full speed only with lactose present AND glucose absent (eat dessert first, digest milk later). This combines negative control (repressor) with positive control (CAP) - the lac operon is inducible, while the trp operon (amino-acid synthesis) is repressible: tryptophan activates its repressor to shut production off. Attenuation adds a third, translation-linked control in trp.

Topic in Depth · Lac operon

Lac operon runs lactose digestion on combined logic: regulator gene i makes repressor blocking the operator (negative control OFF), lactose-derived allolactose binds repressor to free it (induction ON), while glucose absence raises cAMP-CAP supercharging the promoter (positive control FULL SPEED) - structural genes z (beta-galactosidase), y (permease), a (transacetylase) express only with lactose present AND glucose absent. Jacob-Monod operon model earned the Nobel for revealing gene regulation logic.
NEET Snapshot:
  • Repressor blocks; inducer frees; CAP-cAMP boosts - triple logic.
  • z, y, a: digest, import, modify lactose pathway.
Operon parts
PointDetail
i geneRepressor protein
p and oPromoter plus operator switches
z, y, aEnzymes for lactose use
CAP-cAMPGlucose-absence booster
NEET Practice MCQ
1

Lac operon expresses maximally when

Solution: Inducer ON plus CAP booster ON (no glucose) gives full transcription speed.

Index / Genetics & Evolution
Chapter 13 · Topic 10

Mutation

Molecular Basis of InheritanceGenetics & EvolutionStep-by-step Topic
Mutations: point (substitution - silent, missense like sickle-cell GAG-GUG, or nonsense creating stops), frameshift (insertion/deletion scrambling all downstream codons - usually worst), and chromosomal (deletion, duplication, inversion, translocation - e.g. Philadelphia chromosome in CML). Spontaneous (replication errors) vs induced (radiation, chemicals like mustard gas, base analogues). Most are neutral or harmful; rare beneficial ones fuel evolution - the raw material de Vries celebrated.

Topic in Depth · Mutation

Mutations edit the script at three scales: point substitutions (silent, missense like sickle-cell GAG-GUG, nonsense creating early stops), frameshift insertions-deletions (garbling all downstream codons - usually worst), chromosomal changes (deletion, duplication, inversion, translocation like Philadelphia CML). Spontaneous replication errors versus induced damage (radiation, mustard gas, base analogues); germline inherits, somatic affects only the individual (plus cancer risk). Most neutral, many harmful, rare beneficial - evolution raw material.
NEET Snapshot:
  • Point (swap), frameshift (shift), chromosomal (rearrange) - severity scales.
  • Germline inherits; somatic stays personal (except cancer).
Remember
  • Frameshifts beat point mutations for damage - downstream garbling.
NEET Practice MCQ
1

Sickle-cell anaemia is a

Solution: Single-base swap changes Glu to Val, polymerising deoxyhaemoglobin.

Index / Genetics & Evolution
Chapter 13 · Topic 11

DNA fingerprinting

Molecular Basis of InheritanceGenetics & EvolutionStep-by-step Topic
HGP (1990–2003): ~3 billion bp sequenced; ~30,000 genes (<2% codes for proteins); chromosome 1 has most genes, Y the fewest; 99.9% of bases identical in all humans. DNA fingerprinting (Alec Jeffreys) compares VNTR/minisatellite patterns via Southern blotting — used in forensics, paternity and pedigree analysis.
Common mistakes: the template strand runs 3′ → 5′; the coding strand matches mRNA (except T for U).

Deeper Understanding · Reading a fingerprint

VNTR loci vary hugely between people (tens to hundreds of repeats) but are inherited stably, so a child bands match parental bands - every band in a child must appear in mother or father, the paternity-test logic. Southern blotting (gel + probe hybridisation) visualises them; modern labs use STR/PCR instead (smaller samples, faster). Monozygotic twins share fingerprints fully; all crime-scene uses need statistical match probabilities, not just visual similarity. HGP spin-offs include pharmacogenomics - dosing drugs by genotype.

Topic in Depth · DNA fingerprinting

DNA fingerprinting (Alec Jeffreys) compares hypervariable VNTR/minisatellite repeats via Southern blotting - band patterns unique per individual (except identical twins), stable through life, identical in all tissues. Paternity logic: every child band must appear in mother or father. Forensics matches crime samples; pedigrees resolve disputes; conservation tracks individuals. Modern STR-PCR works faster on tinier samples (touch DNA), extending the same repeat-variation principle.
NEET Snapshot:
  • VNTR repeats vary hugely yet inherit stably - identity plus inheritance.
  • Child bands must all trace to parents - paternity logic.
NEET Practice MCQ
1

DNA fingerprinting compares

Solution: Hypervariable repeats give individual-specific Southern-blot barcodes.

Chapter 13 · Topic 12

Human Genome Project

Molecular Basis of InheritanceGenetics & EvolutionStep-by-step Topic
Human Genome Project (1990-2003): ~3 billion bp sequenced; ~30,000 genes with under 2% coding for proteins; chromosome 1 most genes, Y fewest; 99.9% of bases identical in all humans. Spin-offs: disease-gene mapping, pharmacogenomics (dose by genotype), and ancestry testing. ENCODE later showed much non-coding DNA regulates expression - junk DNA was a premature verdict.

Topic in Depth · Human Genome Project

HGP (1990-2003, international) sequenced ~3 billion bp revealing ~30,000 genes with under 2 percent protein-coding; chromosome 1 most genes, Y fewest; 99.9 percent bases identical across humanity. Spin-offs transformed medicine: disease-gene mapping, pharmacogenomics (dose by genotype), ancestry testing, cancer genomics. ENCODE later showed non-coding DNA largely regulates expression - junk-DNA verdict reversed into regulatory treasure.
NEET Snapshot:
  • 3 billion bases, ~30,000 genes, 99.9 percent shared humanity.
  • Medicine spin-offs: mapping, dosing, ancestry, cancer.
Remember
  • Under 2 percent codes protein - regulation fills the rest.

Chapter 14 · Step-by-step

Evolution

Origin of life, evidences, Darwin, synthesis, Hardy-Weinberg and human journey.

EvolutionGenetics & EvolutionChapter Introduction

Subtopics in this Chapter

1. Origin of life2. Evidences of evolution3. Darwinism4. Natural selection5. Modern synthetic theory6. Mutation7. Genetic drift8. Gene flow9. Hardy-Weinberg principle10. Adaptive radiation11. Human evolution

Deep Dive · Theories, Hardy-Weinberg and human journey

Theories of evolution compared
TheoryCore ideaStatus
LamarckUse and disuse; inheritance of acquired characters (long giraffe necks)Rejected - acquired traits are not inherited
DarwinBranching descent + natural selection; survival of the fittestFoundation; lacked genetics
de Vries (mutation theory)Sudden large mutations drive evolutionPartially absorbed into modern synthesis
Modern synthetic theoryGene frequency change via mutation, recombination, gene flow, drift, selectionAccepted
Hardy-Weinberg worked example (exact NEET pattern): if 16% of a population shows a recessive trait, q2 = 0.16 so q = 0.4, p = 1 - 0.4 = 0.6, and carriers 2pq = 2 x 0.6 x 0.4 = 48%. Equilibrium holds only with random mating and no selection, mutation, migration or drift - any disturbance means evolution is occurring. Genetic drift (bottleneck + founder effects) bites hardest in small populations. Living proofs of selection: industrial melanism (soot-darkened moths), DDT-resistant mosquitoes, antibiotic-resistant bacteria appearing within months.
Fig: Brain capacity climbs 650 to 900 to 1400 cc along the human journey; agriculture began ~10,000 years ago.

Chapter 14 · Topic 1

Origin of life

EvolutionGenetics & EvolutionStep-by-step Topic
Early Earth: hot, volcanic, reducing atmosphere (CH₄, NH₃, H₂, water vapour — no free O₂). Abiogenesis: simple molecules → organic monomers → polymers → first anaerobic, heterotrophic cells (~3.9 billion years ago); photosynthesis (cyanobacteria) later oxygenated the air. Miller–Urey experiment (electric discharge through the gas mixture) produced amino acids, supporting chemical evolution. Panspermia (life from elsewhere) remains unproven speculation.
NEET facts:
  • First organisms: anaerobic chemoheterotrophs; first photosynthesizers: cyanobacteria.
  • Oparin–Haldane proposed the chemical theory of life's origin.

Deeper Understanding · Disproving spontaneous generation

Redi (covered vs open meat jars) killed maggot abiogenesis; Spallanzani (sealed boiled broth) and finally Pasteur swan-neck flasks killed microbial abiogenesis - dust trapped in the bend, broth stayed sterile until tilted. But this only disproved present-day spontaneous generation in oxygen-rich, microbe-filled conditions. Oparin-Haldane chemical evolution proposes ancient reducing-atmosphere synthesis instead, which Miller-Urey simulated (CH4, NH3, H2 + sparks gave amino acids) - origin chemistry, not kitchen biology.

Related Diagram · Miller-Urey apparatus

Fig: Sparks through ancient gases plus rain cycles produced amino acids - chemistry before biology.

Topic in Depth · Origin of life

Life origin layers hypotheses: ancient reducing atmosphere (CH4, NH3, H2, vapour, no O2) brewing monomers (Oparin-Haldane chemical theory), Miller-Urey sparks confirming amino-acid synthesis, polymers to replicators (RNA world - information plus catalysis combined), first anaerobic heterotroph cells ~3.9 billion years ago, cyanobacterial photosynthesis oxygenating air ~2.5 billion years ago enabling aerobes. Panspermia (life from space) relocates rather than solves origin - still unproven. Pasteur disproved present-day spontaneous generation, not ancient chemical evolution.
NEET Snapshot:
  • Reducing air plus sparks gave monomers; RNA world gave replicators.
  • First cells anaerobic heterotrophs; oxygen came later via cyanobacteria.
NEET Practice MCQ
1

Miller-Urey experiment simulated

Solution: Sparks through CH4/NH3/H2 yielded amino acids - chemical evolution proof of principle.

Index / Genetics & Evolution
Chapter 14 · Topic 2

Evidences of evolution

EvolutionGenetics & EvolutionStep-by-step Topic
Homologous organs (same origin, different functions — human arm, whale flipper, bat wing, cheetah leg) prove divergent evolution; analogous organs (different origin, same function — butterfly vs bird wings; sweet potato vs potato) prove convergent evolution. Embryological evidence (similar vertebrate embryos), fossils across geological strata, and connecting links like Archaeopteryx (reptile–bird) complete the picture.

Sweet potato (root) and potato (stem) store food. Homologous or analogous?

Answer: Analogous — same function from different origins (convergent evolution).

Deeper Understanding · Connecting links catalogue

Each link bridges two groups: Archaeopteryx (reptilian teeth, claws, long tail + avian feathers, wings), lungfishes (fish gills + lungs), platypus (mammalian hair, milk + reptilian egg-laying), Peripatus (annelid-arthropod). Homologous structures (same origin: forelimb bones of human, whale, bat, cheetah) prove divergent evolution; analogous ones (different origin: insect vs bird wings, potato stem vs sweet-potato root) prove convergent evolution under similar pressures. Embryos of all vertebrates pass through gill-slit and tail stages - ontogeny echoes phylogeny.

Related Diagram · Homologous vs analogous

Fig: Same origin different jobs proves divergence; different origins same job proves convergence.

Topic in Depth · Evidences of evolution

Evolution evidence converges from four directions: homologous organs (same bones, different jobs - human arm, whale flipper, bat wing - divergent descent), analogous organs (different origins, same job - insect vs bird wings - convergent pressures), embryology (vertebrate embryos pass gill-slit plus tail stages), fossils in dated strata plus connecting links (Archaeopteryx reptile-bird, lungfish fish-amphibian, platypus reptile-mammal). Biogeography (marsupial Australia, finch islands) plus molecular clocks (DNA divergence timing splits) complete the modern case.
NEET Snapshot:
  • Homologous = divergent origin. Analogous = convergent function.
  • Archaeopteryx bridges reptiles and birds in one fossil.
Evidence quartet
PointDetail
AnatomyHomology vs analogy
EmbryologyGill slits, tails in embryos
FossilsStrata sequence plus links
MoleculesDNA clocks date splits

1

Sweet potato (root) and potato (stem) are

Solution: Same storage function from different organs - convergent evolution.
